Linear and nonlinear supersymmetry in field and string theory
Gabriele Casagrande

Abstract




See pages - of
1ere.pdf

Abstract

The current state of high-energy physics is characterized by a division into two distinct branches. The Standard Model, validated with high precision, governs phenomena at the
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
scale, but it must be modified in order to reach higher energy scales. Key steps in this respect are the coupling to gravity, following the principles of general relativity, and the quantization of such gravitational interaction. String theory, operating near the Planck scale, remains the only consistent framework achieving such unification. Within the effective field theory approach, one expects a series of intermediate models connecting the Standard Model in the infrared to String Theory in the ultraviolet, progressively disclosing the features of quantum gravity. However, the concrete realization of this connection remains elusive.

A major role in bridging this gap is played by supersymmetry, as it is compellingly motivated at both ends of the energy spectrum. In field theory, it is the sole extension of the Poincaré group and leads naturally to gravity when realized locally. In string theory, supersymmetry is necessary for describing fermionic excitations and ensuring vacuum stability, with supergravity emerging as its low-energy limit. Furthermore, spontaneous supersymmetry breaking provides a coherent framework for transitioning from string theory to the non-supersymmetric Standard Model, with the associated breaking scale acting as a vehicle for high-energy physics to manifest at lower energies.

This Ph.D. thesis investigates effective field and string theories in which supersymmetry is realized and broken in various ways. First, we address effective theories with nonlinearly realized supersymmetry, constructed using the formalism of constrained superfields. We establish a criterion for identifying inconsistent constraints, formulate their improved versions, and propose, along these lines, improved supergravity models of inflation. Then, we discuss a peculiar application of this framework: the gravitational production of massive gravitinos in time-dependent backgrounds. We emphasize some physical subtleties in the standard description of this mechanism, which become particularly severe in the case of the gravitino. Next, the focus shifts to linear supersymmetry, and we investigate the leading-order coupling of the massive spin-2 field supermultiplet to pure supergravity in four dimensions. This analysis is carried out using the supercurrent superfield formalism, and it shows that only a single class of such couplings can be consistently defined. This is fundamentally distinct from the coupling obtained by compactifying higher-dimensional supergravities and it has interesting connections to string theory. Finally, we turn to string theory and present a novel orientifold projection of the type IIB string. This is a Scherk–Schwarz orientifold in which supersymmetry is entirely broken, and the O-planes couple only to the twisted sector of the theory. We argue that this model is invariant under S-duality and can be formulated as an F-theory compactification. We also analyze the D-brane spectrum of the theory and compare the results with earlier similar constructions.

Résumé en français

L’état actuel de la physique des hautes énergies se caractérise par une division en deux branches distinctes. Le Modèle Standard, validé avec une grande précision, décrit les phénomènes à l’échelle du
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
, mais il doit être modifié pour atteindre des énergies plus élevées. Les étapes clés dans cette perspective sont le couplage à la gravité, selon les principes de la relativité générale, ainsi que la quantification de cette interaction gravitationnelle. La théorie des cordes, opérant à proximité de l’échelle de Planck, demeure le seul cadre cohérent permettant une telle unification. Dans le cadre de la théorie des champs efficace, on s’attend dès lors à une série de modèles intermédiaires reliant le Modèle Standard dans l’infrarouge à la théorie des cordes dans l’ultraviolet, révélant progressivement les caractéristiques de la gravité quantique. Toutefois, la réalisation concrète de cette connexion reste insaisissable.

Un rôle majeur dans la tentative de combler cet écart est joué par la supersymétrie, car elle est solidement motivée aux deux extrémités du spectre énergétique. En théorie des champs, elle constitue la seule extension du groupe de Poincaré et conduit naturellement à la gravité lorsqu’elle est réalisée localement. Dans la théorie des cordes, la supersymétrie est nécessaire pour décrire les excitations fermioniques et garantir la stabilité du vide, la supergravité apparaissant comme sa limite à basse énergie. En outre, la rupture spontanée de la supersymétrie offre un cadre cohérent pour la transition de la théorie des cordes vers le Modèle Standard non-supersymétrique, l’échelle de rupture jouant alors le rôle de vecteur permettant à la physique des hautes énergies de se manifester à basse énergie.

Cette thèse de doctorat étudie des théories des champs et de cordes efficaces dans lesquelles la supersymétrie est réalisée et brisé de diverses manières. On commençe par examiner des théories efficaces avec une supersymétrie réalisée de manière non linéaire, construites à l’aide du formalisme des superchamps contraints. Nous établissons un critère permettant d’identifier les contraintes inconsistantes, formulons leurs versions améliorées, et proposons, dans cette optique, des modèles améliorés de supergravité pour l’inflation. Ensuite, on discute une application particulière de ce cadre : la production gravitationnelle de gravitinos massifs dans des fonds dépendant du temps. Nous soulignons certaines subtilités physiques dans la description standard de ce mécanisme, qui deviennent particulièrement sévères dans le cas du gravitino. Par la suite, l’attention se porte sur la supersymétrie linéaire, et nous étudions le couplage au premier ordre du supermultiplet de spin-2 massif à la supergravité pure en quatre dimensions. Cette analyse est réalisée à l’aide du formalisme du superchamp de supercourant, et elle montre qu’une seule classe de tels couplages peut être définie de manière cohérente. Cela est fondamentalement distinct de celui obtenu en compactifiant la supergravité en dimensions supérieures, et il est d’une manière interessante lié à la théorie des cordes. Enfin, nous nous tournons vers la théorie des cordes et présentons une nouvelle projection orientifold de la corde de type IIB. Il s’agit d’un orientifold de Scherk–Schwarz dans lequel la supersymétrie est complètement brisé, et les O-plans ne se couplent qu’au secteur twisté de la théorie. Nous soutenons que ce modèle est invariant sous la S-dualité et peut être formulé comme une compactification en théorie F. Nous analysons également les D-branes de cette théorie et comparons les résultats à ceux obtenus dans des constructions similaires antérieures.

Introduction

The modern perspective on quantum field theory is to view every model as an
effective field theory
, describing physics consistently only up to a certain energy scale. An effective theory successfully describes the physical processes whose typical scale lies within the appropriate energy range, whereas beyond this energy threshold the spectrum of phenomena expands and the theory eventually fails to be predictive. Famous examples of effective field theories are the Fermi theory of weak interactions and the chiral lagrangian describing the low-energy dynamics of QCD in terms of pions. In both cases, we know that the underlying physics is more complex: weak interactions are mediated by the
Z_{0}
and
W_{\pm}
bosons, and the pions in the chiral lagrangian are bound states of quarks. However, these fields are fine structures that cannot be resolved at the energy scale at which the effective field theory is defined, and the relevant physics is more easily described by this effective theory. More precisely, within the energy range where it applies, the effective field theory is as predictive as the "complete" theory from which it is derived.

Beyond the purely phenomenological meaning, quantum field theories are now regarded as effective field theories in the sense that we do not expect a given theory to be descriptive of all phenomena at all energies. We expect instead to have to deal with different theories at different energy scales, all seen as low-energy realizations of the same, unifying theory. Taking this perspective, the spectrum of energy scales is divided into infrared (IR) and ultraviolet (UV) regions which have traditionally been critically disconnected. On the infrared side there is the Standard Model of particle physics
[
5
,
6
,
7
]
, which has been tested
with great precision and confirmed to be the theory describing physics at the
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
scale
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
. However, it is evident that this theory is incomplete, as there are several crucial questions that the Standard Model fails to address, highlighting the need for its extension. For example, the Standard Model does not predict any neutrino masses at the renormalizable level, and it fails to explain why the mass of the Higgs boson is so small and protected by radiative corrections. Also, it does not provide any viable dark matter candidate and suffers from the well-known CP problem. In addition, recent precision tests have challenged some of its predictions – for instance, the muon magnetic moment
[
14
]
.

From a broader point of view, the main issue with the Standard model is that it does not include the gravitational interaction. Moreover, gravity does not only need to be coupled to the Standard Model fields following Einstein’s general relativity principles, but it must also be properly quantized in order to be truly unified in a single picture with all other forces and fields. However, this task is as crucial as it is difficult to accomplish, since Einstein’s general relativity is inherently non-renormalizable
[
15
,
16
]
. This failure of the standard quantum field theory paradigm in describing the gravitational interaction is actually a major indication that our understanding of the laws of nature needs to evolve and in which direction. To date, the only framework that has achieved a quantum description of gravity is String Theory
[
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
,
24
]
, which lives close to the Planck scale and represents the UV side of the theories’ energy spectrum. According to the effective field theory principles, we expect String Theory and the Standard Model to be connected through a series of effective models living between the
\mathrm{T}\mathrm{e}\mathrm{V}
and Planck scales, gradually revealing the quantum features of gravity. However, how to concretely realize such a connection is neither obvious nor unique. Following a top-down approach, one finds an extraordinarily large number of possible four-dimensional effective theories that can be derived from string theory via compactification
[
25
]
, which is known as the string landscape, and no vacuum selection mechanism leading uniquely to the Standard Model has yet been identified
[
26
]
.

Bridging this IR–UV gap and making the quantum description of gravity at high energies consistent with the low-scale physics are the main challenges of modern theoretical physics. In this respect, a crucial role is played by
supersymmetry
, which has been a highly productive framework and a prominent example of unification since its first formulation around fifty years ago
[
27
,
28
]
and is the central focus of the present thesis. In fact, supersymmetry has strong motivations to be realized on both sides of the energy spectrum and can therefore help to concretely establish a bridge between the two IR and UV regions we long to connect.

On the field theory side, supersymmetry is the maximal possible extension of the Poincaré group of special relativity, as it is the only way to evade the famous Coleman–Mandula theorem
[
29
,
30
]
. Moreover, following this general symmetry principle, when realized as a local symmetry, it automatically yields gravitational interactions in what are known as supergravity theories
[
31
,
32
]
, which realize a first unification of gravity and the other interactions in a unified theoretical picture. Some notable advantages of supersymmetry from the quantum field theory perspective include a natural solution to the Standard Model’s hierarchy problem, potential dark matter candidates, improved renormalizability (including for gravity
[
33
]
) and the unification of gauge interactions at high energy scales.

On the String Theory side, supersymmetry must necessarily be part of the worldsheet theory in order to describe fermionic
excitations and to ensure the stability of string vacua by removing the tachyonic closed string mode. Furthermore, the low-energy limits of superstring theories coincide with the consistent supergravity theories in ten dimensions. From this point of view, one may say that supersymmetry is actually predicted by String Theory, assuming its validity.

Furthermore, supersymmetry offers a well-defined framework in which to embed the transition from String Theory to low-energy field theories. This is the framework of the
supersymmetry breaking
mechanisms, which can occur both in field and string theory. Supersymmetry must ultimately be broken at some point in this evolution, as the Standard Model is clearly non-supersymmetric. As a result, the scale of supersymmetry breaking acts as the channel through which the UV physics propagates at lower energies.

The research I conducted during my doctoral training takes place within this broader context and focuses on the study of string and field theories characterized by different amounts of supersymmetry. The main outcomes of this work are the articles
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
]
, which are the core of the present thesis:

•

the subject of
[
1
]
is nonlinear rigid supersymmetry and its implementation through the so-called constrained superfields. We prove the inconsistency of a certain class of superfield constraints, whose associated goldstino lagrangians exhibit nontrivial causality constraints that are in tension with the effective field theory setup. We propose an alternative, consistent version of these constraints and use them to formulate improved minimal models of inflation in supergravity.

•

In
[
2
]
we revisit the theoretical aspects of what is known as gravitational particle production mechanism. This phenomenon takes place when fields are quantized in a time-dependent background, whose expansion drives the creation of particles from the vacuum. More specifically, we analyze the physical consequences of some ambiguities of the formalism used to describe this phenomenon, which we found to be particularly relevant in the case of the massive gravitino in cosmological supergravity models.

•

In
[
3
]
we construct a novel orientifold projection of the type IIB string. It is a Scherk–Schwarz deformation that lives in nine dimensions and that has no leftover supersymmetry. The resulting theory contains orientifold planes which couple only to the massive twisted states and is conjectured to exhibit S-duality invariance.

•

Finally, in
[
4
]
we investigate the supersymmetric coupling of a single massive spin-2 field to
D=4
,
\mathcal{N}=1
supergravity at leading order in the Planck mass. Using the supercurrent superfield formalism, we demonstrate that this coupling is unique, that it involves a non-minimal, higher-derivative coupling to the metric field, and that it matches the coupling arising from the first oscillator mode of the open superstring.

The content of the thesis is organized in the following way. In Chapter
1
we give a general introduction to rigid supersymmetry and the superspace formulation. We then discuss the nonlinear realization of supersymmetry, the constrained superfield formalism and present the results of
[
1
]
. Chapter
2
is instead centered on supergravity. We first introduce supergravity as the gauge theory of the Super-Poincaré group, describing its coupling to matter fields and the supersymmetric Higgs mechanism. Focusing on this setup, we discuss the dynamics of the massive gravitino in cosmological models based on nonlinear supergravity and present the results of
[
2
]
on their gravitational production. Next, we discuss the various off-shell formulations of
D=4
,
\mathcal{N}=1
supergravity, in relation to the different types of supercurrent superfields. Then, we present in detail the analysis and results of
[
4
]
on the couplings of the massive spin-2 supermultiplet to supergravity. In the end, Chapter
3
is devoted to string theory. We give a comprehensive review of the bosonic string and the superstring theories, with specific emphasis on the one-loop vacuum amplitudes and the orientifold projections. We then specify the discussion to orientifold compactifications and describe in detail the new such construction put forward in
[
3
]
. Finally, Chapter
4
summarizes the results of my work presented in this manuscript and gives some outlook for its future developments. The content of the various chapters is supplemented and integrated by four appendices
A
–
D
.

Chapter 1
Rigid supersymmetry and constrained superfields

The first chapter of this manuscript is devoted to the description of the general framework of rigid supersymmetry. The main focus will be on the case of
D=4
,
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry, its spontaneous breaking, and the resulting nonlinear realization. The original results discussed in this chapter were presented in
[
1
]
and concern the construction of low-energy effective theories of nonlinear supersymmetry using the so-called constrained superfield formalism. More specifically, we identify and motivate a criterion for distinguishing between superfield constraints which define consistent effective theories and those which do not.

The chapter is organized as follows. In Section
1.1
we review the basic elements of the four-dimensional
Super-Poincaré algebra
and its representations. In Section
1.2
we introduce the
superspace
formalism and the general elements of supersymmetric field theories. In Section
1.3
we discuss the mechanism of
spontaneous supersymmetry breaking
, present explicit models in which it occurs, and examine the
nonlinear realization
of supersymmetry that emerges and characterizes the dynamics of the goldstino, the Goldstone fermion of supersymmetry breaking. In Section
1.4
we move to the more advanced topic of
constrained superfields
and the construction of effective theories of nonlinear supersymmetry, presenting the original results of
[
1
]
.

In this chapter we follow the conventions of
[
34
]
, which we collect, together with useful formulae, in Appendix
A
. Further references are
[
35
,
36
,
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
.

1.1
The Super-Poincaré algebra

The core idea and best-known feature of supersymmetry is the relationship it establishes between bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom:

\text{Bosons}\ \xleftrightarrow{\text{ \ SUSY \ }}\ \text{Fermions}\ .

(1.1.1)

Thus, the field content of a supersymmetric theory will organize into multiplets of bosons and fermions, joined together by supersymmetry. The fact that it relates particles of different spin implies that supersymmetry must be a
spacetime
symmetry (
i.e.
not an internal one).

This property is actually very deep and has powerful implications. The Coleman–Mandula theorem
[
29
]
states that the largest continuous symmetry that the S-matrix of a given quantum field theory can posses is given by the Poincaré group times possible internal symmetries. The Poincaré group is generated by the translations
P_{\mu}
and the Lorentz transformations
M_{\mu\nu}
, which satisfy the standard algebra

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(1.1.2)

\displaystyle[P_{\mu},M_{\rho\sigma}]

\displaystyle=-i\left(\eta_{\mu\rho}P_{\sigma}-\eta_{\mu\sigma}P_{\rho}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle[M_{\mu\nu},M_{\rho\sigma}]

\displaystyle=i\left(\eta_{\mu\rho}M_{\nu\sigma}-\eta_{\mu\sigma}M_{\nu\rho}-\eta_{\nu\rho}M_{\mu\sigma}+\eta_{\nu\sigma}M_{\mu\rho}\right)\ .

The Coleman–Mandula theorem holds provided some physical assumptions (
e.g.
locality, causality, positivity of the energy, …) are satisfied. One of these assumptions is that the generators of the S-matrix symmetry algebra are bosonic,
i.e.
satisfy standard commutation relation, like those of eq. (
1.1.2
). Thus, the Coleman–Mandula theorem can be extended by allowing the algebra to contain also
fermionic
generators, satisfying anticommutation relations. It was then proven by Haag, Lopuszanski and Sohnius
[
30
]
that the specific combination of Poincaré and supersymmetry, namely the Super-Poincaré group, is the only possible fermionic extension of the S-matrix symmetry algebra. Therefore, the Super-Poincaré group is the largest symmetry group that a quantum field theory can enjoy. This is the main theoretical motivation in favor of supersymmetry and its realisation in nature.

The algebra of the super Super-Poincaré group is an example of
graded
Lie algebra, which is a vector space
L
given by

L=\oplus_{i=0}^{i=n}L_{i}\ ,

(1.1.3)

with
L_{i}
also being vector spaces, endowed with a product

[\;\;,\;\;\}:\ L\times L\to L\ ,

(1.1.4)

such that

\displaystyle[L_{i},L_{j}\}\in L_{i+j}\qquad\text{mod}\ n+1\ ,

(1.1.5)

\displaystyle[L_{i},L_{j}\}=-\left(-1\right)^{\eta_{i}\eta_{j}}[L_{j},L_{i}\}\ ,

\displaystyle[L_{i},[L_{j},L_{k}\}\}\left(-1\right)^{\eta_{i}\eta_{k}}+[L_{j},[L_{k},L_{i}\}\}\left(-1\right)^{\eta_{i}\eta_{j}}+[L_{k},[L_{i},L_{j}\}\}\left(-1\right)^{\eta_{j}\eta_{k}}=0\ ,

where
\eta_{i}
=0 for bosonic generators and
\eta_{i}=1
for fermionic ones. The first equation in (
1.1.5
) implies that the first element
L_{0}
must be a Lie algebra, while all the others are not. The second equation is instead known as supersymmetrization condition, while the third one is the generalization of the standard Jacobi identity
[
35
]
. The Super-Poincaré algebra is then given by

L_{\textup{SP}}=L_{0}\oplus L_{1}\ ,

(1.1.6)

with
L_{0}
being the Poincaré group (
1.1.2
) and
L_{1}
the actual supersymmetry algebra. For
\mathcal{N}
supersymmetries, this is generated by a set of
2\mathcal{N}
fermionic generators,
Q_{\alpha}^{I}
and
\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}
, with
I=1,\dots,\mathcal{N}
and
\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}=\left(Q_{\alpha}^{I}\right)^{\dagger}
, transforming respectively in the
\left(\frac{1}{2},0\right)
and
\left(0,\frac{1}{2}\right)
representations of the Lorentz group, to which the indices
\alpha=1,2
and
\dot{\alpha}=\dot{1},\dot{2}
are associated. In addition to (
1.1.2
), the Super-Poincaré algebra involves the following commutation relations:

\displaystyle=[P_{\mu},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}]=0\ ,

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{J}\}

\displaystyle=2\delta^{IJ}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}P_{\mu}\ ,

(1.1.7)

\displaystyle[M_{\mu\nu},Q_{\alpha}^{I}]

\displaystyle=-i\left(\sigma_{\mu\nu}\right)_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}Q_{\beta}^{I}\ ,

\displaystyle\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},Q_{\beta}^{J}\}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}Z^{IJ}\ ,

\displaystyle[M_{\mu\nu},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}]

\displaystyle=-i\left(\bar{\sigma}_{\mu\nu}\right)_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\bar{Q}^{I}{}^{\dot{\beta}}\ ,

\displaystyle\{\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\beta}}^{J}\}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\left(Z^{IJ}\right)^{*}\ .

The antisymmetric matrix
Z^{IJ}=-Z^{JI}
is the central extension of the algebra. It commutes in fact with all the generators and it is usually given by some combinations of the generators of the internal symmetries, which we did not include explicitly in eq. (
1.1.7
).
An important example of such symmetries is the so-called R-symmetry, which is the largest internal symmetry group that can have nontrivial commutators with supersymmetry generators
\left(Q_{\alpha}^{I},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}\right)
. In the case of
\mathcal{N}
supersymmetries, it can be shown that this R-symmetry group is actually
\mathrm{U}(\mathcal{N})
.

The Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
) allows one to prove explicitly some of the main properties of all supersymmetric theories. First, we can see that the action of supersymmetry relates bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom. Let’s take for instance the generators
Q_{1}^{I}
and an eigenstate
\left|j,m\right\rangle
of the angular momentum
J_{i}=\varepsilon_{ijk}M^{jk}
, namely

\displaystyle J^{2}\left|j,m\right\rangle=j\left(j+1\right)\left|j,m\right\rangle\ ,

\displaystyle J_{3}\left|j,m\right\rangle=m\left|j,m\right\rangle\ .

(1.1.8)

Then, the state
Q_{1}^{I}\left|j,m\right\rangle
is such that

J_{3}\left(Q_{1}^{I}\left|j,m\right\rangle\right)=\left(m-\frac{1}{2}\right)Q_{1}^{I}\left|j,m\right\rangle\ ,

(1.1.9)

which means that the supersymmetry generator
Q_{1}^{I}
decreases the angular momentum along the
z
-axis by
\frac{1}{2}
. The same is done by the generator
\bar{Q}_{\dot{2}}^{I}
, while the generators
Q_{2}^{I}
and
\bar{Q}_{\dot{1}}^{I}
instead increase the angular momentum along
z
by
\frac{1}{2}
. Hence, the action of the supersymmetry generators on a state is to shift its spin by
\frac{1}{2}
, mapping therefore bosonic states into fermionic ones and vice versa.
On the contrary, the operator
P^{2}
is a Casimir invariant of the Super-Poincaré algebra, since the supersymmetry generators commute with the momentum
P_{\mu}
, and therefore the states belonging to a supersymmetric representation will have the same mass.

Another consequence of the algebra (
1.1.7
) is that the energy of any supersymmetric state
\left|\psi\right\rangle
is positive. This follows from

\displaystyle\left\langle\psi\right|\left\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}\right\}\left|\psi\right\rangle

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{N}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\left\langle\psi\right|P_{\mu}\left|\psi\right\rangle

(1.1.10)

\displaystyle\Big|

\displaystyle=\left\langle\psi\right|\left[Q_{\alpha}^{I}\left(Q_{\alpha}^{I}\right)^{\dagger}+\left(Q_{\alpha}^{I}\right)^{\dagger}Q_{\alpha}^{I}\right]\left|\psi\right\rangle=\lVert Q_{\alpha}^{I}\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}+\lVert\left(Q_{\alpha}^{I}\right)^{\dagger}\left|\psi\right\rangle\rVert^{2}\ ,

which, taking the trace on the
(\alpha,\dot{\alpha})
indices, yields the semi-positivity condition

\left\langle\psi\right|P_{0}\left|\psi\right\rangle\geq 0\ .

(1.1.11)

Finally, we can show how any representation of the Super-Poincaré group contains the same number of bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom. Taking
N_{\textup{F}}
to be the fermion number operator, the operator
\left(-1\right)^{N_{\textup{F}}}
is equal to 1 for bosonic states and to
-1
for fermionic ones, so that its trace over a finite supersymmetric representation is equal to the number of bosons
n_{\textup{B}}
minus the number of fermions
n_{\textup{F}}
contained in such representation:

\text{Tr}\left(-1\right)^{N_{\textup{F}}}=n_{\textup{B}}-n_{\textup{F}}\ .

(1.1.12)

The operator
\left(-1\right)^{N_{\textup{F}}}
satisfies the relation

\left\{\left(-1\right)^{N_{\textup{F}}},Q_{\alpha}^{I}\right\}=0\ ,

(1.1.13)

which combined with the cyclicity property of the trace and the algebra (
1.1.7
) gives the identity

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\text{Tr}\left[\left(-1\right)^{N_{\textup{F}}}Q_{\alpha}^{I}\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}-\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}\left(-1\right)^{N_{\textup{F}}}Q_{\alpha}^{I}\right]=\text{Tr}\left[\left(-1\right)^{N_{\textup{F}}}\left\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{I}\right\}\right]=

(1.1.14)

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{N}\left[\text{Tr}\left(-1\right)^{N_{\textup{F}}}\right]\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}P_{\mu}=2\mathcal{N}\left(n_{\textup{B}}-n_{\textup{F}}\right)\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}P_{\mu}\ ,

which implies that a finite representation of the Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
) contains an equal number of bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom
n_{\textup{B}}=n_{\textup{F}}
.

1.1.1
Massless representations

After having introduced the Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
) and its general properties, we can now discuss in detail its representations. As said before, such representations contain particle states with different spins but equal mass. We then distinguish between massless and massive representations.

We start from the case of massless representations,
i.e.
those for which
P^{2}=0
. The associated 4-momentum is written, in the rest frame, as
P_{\mu}=\left(-E,0,0,E\right)
, with
E
being the (positive) energy, so that the algebra (
1.1.7
) gives

\left\{Q_{\alpha}^{I},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}^{J}\right\}=2\delta^{IJ}\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}P_{\mu}=\begin{pmatrix}4E&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\delta^{IJ}\ .

(1.1.15)

Thus, the generators
Q_{2}^{I}
and
\bar{Q}_{\dot{2}}^{I}
are totally anticommuting and can therefore be set consistently to zero. In addition, also the central charges
Z^{IJ}
must vanish, as implied by eq. (
1.1.7
). The generators
Q_{1}^{I}
and
\bar{Q}_{\dot{1}}^{I}
define instead a set of
\mathcal{N}
pairs of creation and annihilation operators:

\displaystyle a_{I}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{E}}Q_{1}{}_{I}\ ,

\displaystyle a_{I}^{\dagger}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{E}}\bar{Q}_{\dot{1}}{}_{I}=\left(a_{I}\right)^{\dagger}\ ,

(1.1.16)

satisfying

\displaystyle\left\{a_{I},a_{J}^{\dagger}\right\}=\delta_{IJ}\ ,

\displaystyle\left\{a_{I},a_{J}\right\}=\left\{a^{\dagger}_{I},a^{\dagger}_{J}\right\}=0\ .

(1.1.17)

The annihilation operators
a^{I}
lower the helicity of a state by a factor
\frac{1}{2}
, the creation operators
a^{\dagger}{}^{I}
raise it by
\frac{1}{2}
. A given representation is built starting from a state, called the Clifford vacuum
\left|\lambda_{0}\right\rangle
, of energy
E
and helicity
\lambda_{0}
, which is annihilated by all the annihilation operators, namely

a_{I}\left|\lambda_{0}\right\rangle=0\quad\forall\,\,I\ ,

(1.1.18)

and then acting repeatedly on it with the creation operators. The representation, which is called supermultiplet, will thus contain the states

\displaystyle\left|\lambda_{0}\right\rangle,

\displaystyle a^{\dagger}{}_{I}\left|\lambda_{0}\right\rangle=\left|\lambda_{0}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\right\rangle_{I},

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle a_{\mathcal{N}}{}^{\dagger}\dots a_{1}{}^{\dagger}\left|\lambda_{0}\right\rangle=\left|\lambda_{0}+\textstyle{\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}}\right\rangle\ ,

(1.1.19)

carrying helicities from
\lambda_{0}
up to
\lambda_{0}+\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}
. Consistent theories involve fields of helicity not greater than 2, so that
\mathcal{N}=8
is the highest possible number of supersymmetries. For
4<\mathcal{N}\leq 8
, the supermultiplets necessarily involve the spin-2 state,
i.e.
the graviton, and/or its spin-
\frac{3}{2}
partner, the gravitino. These are therefore gravitational theories, where supersymmetry must be realized as a local symmetry. Coherent theories of rigid supersymmetry can therefore be defined up to
\mathcal{N}=4
.

This is not yet enough to have a well-defined irreducible representation of the Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
). The representations have in fact to be consistent with the
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}
theorem. The
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}
transformations flip the helicity of a given state, so that consistent representations must have a symmetric helicity spectrum. It is often the case that one has to combine "by hand" set of states of the type (
1.1.19
) with mirrored helicity spectra to make up a good supermultiplet.

\mathcal{N}=1
massless multiplets

We now focus on the
\mathcal{N}=1
case and analyze concretely the possible massless representations (
1.1.19
). In this case we have one creation and one annihilation operators,
a
and
a^{\dagger}
, so that for every Clifford vacuum
\left|\lambda_{0}\right\rangle
, such that
a\left|\lambda_{0}\right\rangle=0
, there exist one single superpartner state
a^{\dagger}\left|\lambda_{0}\right\rangle=\left|\lambda_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\right\rangle
. Thus, the
\mathcal{N}=1
supermultiplets are given by the couples

\left\{\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}\oplus\left\{-\left(\lambda_{0}+\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\right),-\lambda_{0}\right\}\ ,

(1.1.20)

where
\oplus
denotes the addition of the
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}
-conjugate states. The possible
\mathcal{N}=1
multiplets are then the following:

\bullet
Chiral multiplet:

the chiral multiplet is constructed starting from the Clifford vacuum
\left|\lambda_{0}=0\right\rangle
and contains the degrees of freedom

\left\{0,\frac{1}{2}\right\}\oplus\left\{-\frac{1}{2},0\right\}\ ,

(1.1.21)

which correspond to a complex scalar and a Weyl (chiral) fermion.

\bullet
Vector multiplet:

The gauge multiplet is instead constructed from the Clifford vacuum
\left|\lambda_{0}=\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\right\rangle
, which yields

\left\{\frac{1}{2},1\right\}\oplus\left\{-1,-\frac{1}{2}\right\}\ ,

(1.1.22)

which are the degrees of freedom of a gauge field and a Weyl fermion, usually called gaugino. Standing in the same multiplet, the gaugino must sit in the same representation of the gauge group as the vector field, namely the adjoint one.

Chiral and vector multiplets are the
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric generalization of matter and gauge fields and allow one to describe supersymmetric renormalizable theories in four dimensions. In principle there exist two more
\mathcal{N}=1
multiplets which do not involve higher-helicity states, the ones that are constructed from the Clifford vacua
\left|\lambda_{0}=1\right\rangle
and
\left|\lambda_{0}=\textstyle{\frac{3}{2}}\right\rangle
. These multiplets contain though the helicity states corresponding to the gravitino and the graviton, meaning that they should be embedded in the framework of local supersymmetry rather than global, hence we do not discuss them further here.

Extended supermultiplets

In this work we will mainly be concerned with
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry and so we do not discuss extended representations in detail. We though present, for completeness, one such example, the
\mathcal{N}=2
matter multiplets, which are known as hypermultiplets. They are built from the Clifford vacuum
\left|\lambda_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\right\rangle
and contain

\left\{-\frac{1}{2},0,0,\frac{1}{2}\right\}\oplus\left\{-\frac{1}{2},0,0,\frac{1}{2}\right\}\ ,

(1.1.23)

which are the degrees of freedom of two complex scalars and two Weyl fermions. The reason why a seemingly identical
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}
-conjugate was added is the underlying R-symmetry of extended representations, which in this case is given by the group
\mathrm{SU}(2)_{\textup{R}}
. Under such R-symmetry, the fermions states are singlets but the helicity-0 ones must come into a doublet, which requires then the addition of the
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}
-conjugate to be formed. In the language of
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry, they correspond to two chiral multiplets. However, belonging to the same group, the two Weyl fermions must transform in the same representation of the given internal symmetry group, so that the
\mathcal{N}=2
hypermultiplet is not chiral.

1.1.2
Massive
\mathcal{N}=1
representations

The procedure to build massive representations,
i.e.
those for which
P^{2}=-m^{2}
, is completely analogous to the massless case discussed in Section
1.1.1
. The substantial difference is that now there are no trivial supersymmetry generator, which means a larger set of ladder operators and therefore longer supermultiplets. Taking the rest-frame momentum
P_{\mu}=\left(-M,0,0,0\right)
, the
\mathcal{N}=1
non-trivial
1
1

1

Recall that in the case of
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry there is no central extension. This is not the case for massive representations of extended supersymmetry, which we will briefly discuss at the end of this section.

anticommutator of eq. (
1.1.7
) becomes

\left\{Q_{\alpha},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}\right\}=2M\delta_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\ ,

(1.1.24)

which allows one to define the system of massive ladder operators

\displaystyle b_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2M}}Q_{\alpha}\ ,

\displaystyle b^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2M}}\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}\ ,

(1.1.25)

satisfying

\displaystyle\left\{b_{\alpha},b^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}}{}\right\}=\delta_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\ ,

\displaystyle\left\{b_{\alpha},b_{\beta}{}\right\}=\left\{b^{\dagger}_{\dot{\alpha}},b^{\dagger}_{\dot{\beta}}\right\}=0\ .

(1.1.26)

Notice that, while the creation operator
b_{\dot{1}}^{\dagger}
raises the spin by a factor
\frac{1}{2}
, as we saw previously, the new one
b_{\dot{2}}^{\dagger}
instead decreases it by the same amount.

The Clifford vacuum
\left|j_{0}\right\rangle
for massive representations carries now spin
j_{0}
and it is annihilated by both
b_{1}
and
b_{2}
, as in eq. (
1.1.18
). The massive version of the chiral and vector massless multiplets of eq. (
1.1.21
) and (
1.1.22
) are given by

\displaystyle\bullet\,\,\textbf{chiral multiplet:}

\displaystyle\left|j_{0}=0\right\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\left\{-\frac{1}{2},0,0,\frac{1}{2}\right\}\ ,

(1.1.27)

\displaystyle\bullet\,\,\textbf{vector multiplet:}

\displaystyle\left|j_{0}=\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\longrightarrow

\displaystyle\left\{0,2\times\frac{1}{2},1\right\}\oplus\left\{-1,2\times-\frac{1}{2},0\right\}\ .

(1.1.28)

The massive chiral multiplet (
1.1.27
) contains the same degrees of freedom of the massless one (
1.1.21
), although it is
\mathcal{C}\mathcal{P}\mathcal{T}
-invariant by itself. The states described by the massive vector multiplet
1.1.28
are those of one massive vector, one massive Dirac fermion and one massive real scalar. In terms of massless multiplets, it corresponds to one massless vector (
1.1.22
) and one massless chiral (
1.1.21
) multiplet. This is the supersymmetric version of the Stückelberg mechanism for the massive Proca field, which can be consistently written as the sum of a massless gauge field and a scalar mode.

Massive representations of extended supersymmetries

Massive representations of extended supersymmetry are richer because of the possible nontrivial central extension of the algebra (
1.1.7
). Taking the number
\mathcal{N}
of supersymmetries to be even, the non-trivial central charges are
Z_{r}
, with
r=1,\dots,\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}
. Then, the algebra (
1.1.7
) allows one to define a set of
2\mathcal{N}
pairs of creation and annihilation operators, respectively
\left(c_{\alpha}^{r},\left(c_{\alpha}^{r}\right)^{\dagger}\right)
and
\left(d_{\alpha}^{r},\left(d_{\alpha}^{r}\right)^{\dagger}\right)
, such that the non-trivial anticommutators of the algebra they define are

\displaystyle\left\{c_{\alpha}^{r},\left(c_{\beta}^{s}\right)^{\dagger}\right\}=\left(2m+Z_{r}\right)\delta^{rs}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\ ,

\displaystyle\left\{d_{\alpha}^{r},\left(d_{\beta}^{s}\right)^{\dagger}\right\}=\left(2m-Z_{r}\right)\delta^{rs}\delta_{\alpha\beta}\ .

(1.1.29)

From the positivity of the Hilbert space defined by such ladder operators, it follows that the central charges are bounded as

\lvert Z_{r}\rvert\leq 2m\quad\forall\,\,r\ ,

(1.1.30)

from which we find back that massless representations cannot be associated with nontrivial central charges. Moreover, we see that if some of the central charges saturate this bound, the correspondent
d
-ladder operators are trivially realized and can be set to zero. This is an example of BPS condition and the multiplets associated with such BPS configurations are shorter. For instance, the
\mathcal{N}=2
massive short multiplets contain the same degrees of freedom as the massless ones. Thus, the massive representations of extended supersymmetry can be realized as long multiplets, short multiplets or ultra-short multiplets, based on the number of central charges that satisfy the BPS condition
Z_{r}=2m
that follows from the bound (
1.1.30
).

1.1.3
Representations on fields and supersymmetry in components

The pattern described to build the representations of the Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
) in terms of spin states can be applied to construct the supermultiplets of fields. The starting point is a bottom-component field, analogous to the Clifford vacuum, on which half of the supersymmetry generators act trivially. Its superpartners are then obtained as the (nontrivial) iterative action of the supersymmetry generators. Each step of the procedure is controlled by the Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
), in particular by the generalized Jacobi identity of eq. (
1.1.5
), and it goes on as long as the supersymmetry transformations result in new degrees of freedom.

We illustrate this in the case of the
\mathcal{N}=1
chiral multiplet of eq. (
1.1.21
). Its bottom component is a complex scalar field
\phi(x)
such that

\left[\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}},\phi\right]=0\ .

(1.1.31)

Then, the second element of the multiplet associated with such a field
\phi
is given by
2
2

2

Note that if
\phi
were real, it would follow from eq. (
1.1.31
) that the action of all supersymmetry generators would be trivial.

\left[Q_{\alpha},\phi\right]\equiv\psi_{\alpha}\ .

(1.1.32)

It follows from the algebra (
1.1.7
) that the supersymmetry generators have mass dimension equal to
\frac{1}{2}
, so that the field
\psi_{\alpha}
has mass dimension
\frac{3}{2}
and it carries a Weyl index: it is therefore a spin-
\frac{1}{2}
Weyl fermion. The next combinations in line are given by

\displaystyle\left\{Q_{\alpha},\psi_{\beta}\right\}=F_{\alpha\beta}\ ,

\displaystyle\left\{\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}},\psi_{\beta}\right\}=H_{\dot{\alpha}\beta}\ .

(1.1.33)

Making use of the generalised Jacobi identity of eq. (
1.1.5
), one can see that

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} F_{\alpha\beta}+F_{\beta\alpha}=0&&\longleftrightarrow&&F_{\alpha\beta}=F\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\end{aligned}\ ,

\displaystyle H_{\dot{\alpha}\beta}=2\sigma^{\mu}_{\beta\dot{\alpha}}\left[P_{\mu},\phi\right]\sim\partial_{\mu}\phi\ ,

(1.1.34)

so the only new degree of freedom emerging at this step is the complex scalar field
F
, of mass dimension
2
. Proceeding in the same way, namely acting on
F
with the supersymmetry generators
Q_{\alpha}
and
\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}
and using the Jacobi identity (
1.1.5
), one obtains

\displaystyle\left[Q_{\alpha},F\right]=0\ ,

\displaystyle\left[\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}},F\right]=-2\left(\partial_{\mu}\psi\sigma^{\mu}\right)_{\dot{\alpha}}\ .

(1.1.35)

Thus, since no new degrees of freedom arose from this last step, the supersymmetric multiplet defined by the field
\phi
of eq. (
1.1.31
) is given by

\left\{\phi,\psi,F\right\}\ .

(1.1.36)

Compared to the one in eq. (
1.1.21
), this multiplet contains the complex scalar field
F
and accommodates therefore twice the degrees of freedom, four bosonic and four fermionic. The reason is that the multiplet (
1.1.36
) is an
off-shell
multiplet. The field
F
is an auxiliary field, carrying no actual dynamical degree of freedom. Going on-shell, it is fixed to a given function
F(\phi,\psi)
, depending on the specific model considered, while the Dirac equation for
\psi
halves its degrees of freedom, giving back the (on-shell) chiral multiplet of eq. (
1.1.21
). Despite seemingly being a redundant description, off-shell supermultiplets are very useful and will come naturally in the superspace formalism, as we will see in Section
1.2
.

The field components of the supermultiplet are related, as described, by supersymmetry transformations, which are usually given at the infinitesimal level, depending on a parameter
\epsilon
of mass dimension
-\frac{1}{2}
. The infinitesimal action of supersymmetry is given by

\delta_{\epsilon}=i\left(\epsilon Q+\bar{\epsilon}\bar{Q}\right)\ .

(1.1.37)

The infinitesimal supersymmetry transformations of the off-shell are, in the standard normalization convention,

\displaystyle\delta\phi

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\epsilon\psi\ ,

(1.1.38)

\displaystyle\delta\psi

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{2}\left(\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\right)\partial_{\mu}\phi+\sqrt{2}F\epsilon\,

\displaystyle\delta F

\displaystyle=i\sqrt{2}\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi\ ,

in agreement with the computation above. This set of transformations closes on the spacetime translations as

\left[\delta_{\epsilon_{1}},\delta_{\epsilon_{2}}\right]=-2i\left(\epsilon_{1}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}_{2}-\epsilon_{2}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}_{1}\right)\partial_{\mu}\ ,

(1.1.39)

as prescribed by the Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
). The transformations (
1.1.38
) characterize the so-called free Wess–Zumino model
[
28
,
27
]
.

1.2
Superspace and superfields

In Section
1.1
we described how supersymmetry emerges naturally as the maximal extension of the Poincaré group, resulting in the so-called Super-Poincaré algebra given in eq. (
1.1.7
). We have then discussed its main properties and its representations, the supersymmetric multiplets, first in terms of spin states and finally in terms of component fields.

From now on, we restrict to
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetry. The most elegant, general as well as effective way to construct and study
\mathcal{N}=1
supersymmetric field theories is the framework of
superspace

[
42
,
43
]
. The idea behind superspace is the following. As the standard Poincaré group (
1.1.2
), describing translations
P_{\mu}
and rotations
M_{\mu\nu}
of the ordinary spacetime coordinates
x^{\mu}
, has been extended to the Super-Poincaré group (
1.1.7
), one can think of associating also the new supersymmetry generators
Q_{\alpha}
and
\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}
to some additional coordinates. By construction, these are
2+2
Grassmann coordinates,
3
3

3

See Section
A.1.1
for a summary of properties and useful formulae on Grassmann algebras.
which are usually noted as
\left(\theta_{\alpha},\bar{\theta}_{\dot{\alpha}}\right)
. A point in superspace is thus given by the set of eight coordinates
\left(x^{\mu},\theta_{\alpha},\bar{\theta}_{\dot{\alpha}}\right)
. In this setup, a supersymmetry transformation of parameter
\epsilon
corresponds to the following translation of the superspace coordinates:

\displaystyle\theta\to\theta+\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}\to\bar{\theta}+\bar{\epsilon}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}x^{\mu}=i\left(\epsilon\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}-\theta\sigma\bar{\epsilon}\right)\ ,

(1.2.1)

where the spacetime coordinate transformation
\delta_{\epsilon}x^{\mu}
follows by consistency with
\left\{Q,\bar{Q}\right\}\sim P_{\mu}
(
1.1.7
). A full "supertranslation" is described by the Super-Poincaré group element

G\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)=e^{i\left(-x^{\mu}P_{\mu}+\epsilon Q+\bar{\epsilon}\bar{Q}\right)}\ .

(1.2.2)

A field defined on such superspace,
i.e.
depending on the full set of superspace coordinates,
e.g.

F\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)
, is called
superfield
. Since Grassmann numbers are nilpotent, a superfield has a finite expansion in the superspace coordinates, of the type

\displaystyle F\left(x^{\mu},\theta_{\alpha},\bar{\theta}_{\dot{\alpha}}\right)=

\displaystyle f(x)+\theta\psi(x)+\bar{\theta}\bar{\chi}(x)+\theta^{2}M(x)+\bar{\theta}^{2}\bar{N}(x)+\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}A_{\mu}(x)

(1.2.3)

\displaystyle+\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}\bar{\lambda}(x)+\bar{\theta}^{2}\theta\zeta(x)+\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}D(x)\ .

The action of a supersymmetry transformation of parameter
\epsilon
on such a superfield is computed as

e^{i\left(\epsilon Q+\bar{\epsilon}\bar{Q}\right)}F\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)e^{-i\left(\epsilon Q+\bar{\epsilon}\bar{Q}\right)}\ ,

(1.2.4)

in agreement with eq. (
1.1.37
), and yields, once expanded, the representation of the infinitesimal supersymmetry transformations as differential operators in superspace:

\displaystyle Q_{\alpha}=-i\partial_{\alpha}-\left(\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\right)_{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}=i\bar{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}+\left(\theta\sigma^{\mu}\right)_{\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu},

(1.2.5)

which explicitly verify the Super-Poincaré algebra condition

\left\{Q_{\alpha},\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}\right\}=-2i\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\ .

(1.2.6)

of eq. (
1.1.7
). In addition to
Q_{\alpha}
and
\bar{Q}_{\dot{\alpha}}
, we introduce two further differential operators, the covariant derivatives

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}+i\left(\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\right)_{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}=-\bar{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}-i\left(\theta\sigma^{\mu}\right)_{\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\ ,

(1.2.7)

which satisfy the anticommutation relations

\displaystyle\left\{D,D\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\bar{D},\bar{D}\right\}=\left\{D,Q\right\}=\left\{D,\bar{Q}\right\}=\left\{\bar{D},Q\right\}=\left\{\bar{D},\bar{Q}\right\}=0\ ,

(1.2.8)

\displaystyle\left\{D_{\alpha},\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\right\}

\displaystyle=-2i\sigma^{\mu}_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\ .

The crucial advantage of the superspace formalism is that it allows one to construct supersymmetric lagrangians, including in particular interactions among the component fields, in a straightforward manner. The reason is that a superspace action constructed in terms of superfields (
1.2.3
), given by
4
4

4

A product of superfields is, by definition (
1.2.3
), itself a superfield, so that (
1.2.9
) is, for a real
F
, the most general form of a superspace action.

\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,F\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)\ ,

(1.2.9)

is automatically a supersymmetric object. Acting on (
1.2.9
) and using eq. (
1.1.37
), (
1.2.1
) and (
1.2.5
), the integration measure is by definition invariant, so that

\displaystyle\delta_{\epsilon}\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}

\displaystyle F\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)=\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,\delta_{\epsilon}F\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)=

(1.2.10)

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\left(i\epsilon Q+i\bar{\epsilon}\bar{Q}\right)F\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)=

\displaystyle=\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\left[\epsilon^{\alpha}\partial_{\alpha}+\bar{\epsilon}_{\dot{\alpha}}\partial^{\dot{\alpha}}+i\left(\epsilon\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}-\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\right)\partial_{\mu}\right]F\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)\ .

The first two terms vanish because of the Grassmann integration, while the last term is a total spacetime derivative and thus vanishes as well. Therefore, starting from a superspace action of the type (
1.2.9
) and performing the integration in the fermionic coordinates yields a standard action which is, by construction, invariant under supersymmetry.

Moreover, irreducible representations of the Super-Poincaré algebra are obtained in superspace in a simple and unified way, starting from the general superfield (
1.2.3
) and reducing its components through supersymmetry-invariant conditions. We’ll see this explicitly for the chiral (
1.1.21
)-(
1.1.36
) and vector (
1.1.22
) multiplets in what follows.

1.2.1
Chiral superfields

Chiral multiplets (
1.1.21
) contain a complex scalar a spin-
\frac{1}{2}
Weyl fermion, together with a second complex scalar in its off-shell formulation (
1.1.36
). In superspace, this multiplet is described by a superfield
\Phi
that satisfies the condition

\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\Phi=0\ .

(1.2.11)

In order to see that this constraint reduces the components of
\Phi
to those of the off-shell chiral multiplet (
1.1.36
), it is convenient to use a modified superspace coordinates system, where the spacetime coordinate is redefined into

\displaystyle x^{\mu}\quad\longrightarrow\quad y^{\mu}=x^{\mu}+i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\ .

(1.2.12)

In the basis
\left(y,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)
, the superspace covariant derivatives (
1.2.7
) are given by
5
5

5

We will use, with abuse of notation and the help of the context, the symbol
\partial_{\mu}
to indicate derivatives with respect to both
x^{\mu}
and
y^{\mu}
.

\displaystyle D_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}+2i\left(\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\right)_{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}=-\bar{\partial}_{\dot{\alpha}}\ .

(1.2.13)

Since we have by construction that
\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}y^{\mu}=0
and
\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\theta_{\alpha}=0
, a superfield that is function only of the coordinates
y^{\mu}
and
\theta
will be automatically chiral, solving the condition (
1.2.11
) directly. The superspace expansion of a chiral superfield is therefore

\Phi\left(y,\theta\right)=\phi(y)+\sqrt{2}\theta\psi(y)+\theta^{2}F(y)\ ,

(1.2.14)

in which we recognize the off-shell field components
\{\phi,\psi,F\}
of eq. (
1.1.36
). In terms of the original basis
\left(y,\theta,\bar{\theta},\right)
, it reads

\displaystyle\Phi\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)=

\displaystyle\phi(x)+\sqrt{2}\theta\psi(x)+\theta^{2}F(x)+i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}\partial_{\mu}\phi(x)

(1.2.15)

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\theta^{2}\partial_{\mu}\psi(x)\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}+\frac{1}{4}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\Box\phi(x)\ .

Then, following eq. (
1.1.37
) and (
1.2.5
), the supersymmetry transformations of the field components of
\Phi
are precisely those given in eq. (
1.1.38
).

Similarly to eq. (
1.2.11
), a superfield
\bar{\Phi}
satisfying
D_{\alpha}\bar{\Phi}=0
is an antichiral superfield and it is the complex conjugate of (
1.2.14
). The simplest superspace action describing such chiral superfields is then

S=\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,\bar{\Phi}\Phi\ ,

(1.2.16)

and the superspace integration yields the kinetic lagrangian

\mathcal{L}=-\partial_{\mu}\bar{\phi}\partial^{\mu}\phi-i\psi\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\psi}+\bar{F}F\ ,

(1.2.17)

which is indeed invariant (up to total spacetime derivatives) under the supersymmetry transformations (
1.1.38
).

Kähler potential and superpotential

Following the construction of chiral superfields just presented, we can now go on and study their interactions in a general fashion. Again, the superspace formalism allows one to do so in a compact and effective manner. The first step in this direction concerns the kinetic action (
1.2.16
). This can be generalized, to

\int d^{4}xd^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,K\left(\bar{\Phi},\Phi\right)\ ,

(1.2.18)

where
K\left(\bar{\Phi},\Phi\right)
is a real superfield, of mass dimension 2, known as
Kähler potential
, and is a polynomial in the chiral superfields
\Phi
and
\bar{\Phi}
. The action (
1.2.18
) it defines is symmetric under

K\left(\bar{\Phi},\Phi\right)\quad\longrightarrow\quad K\left(\bar{\Phi},\Phi\right)+\Lambda\left(\Phi\right)+\bar{\Lambda}\left(\bar{\Phi}\right)\ ,

(1.2.19)

which is called Kähler transformation. Considering
n
chiral superfields
\Phi_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, of the type (
1.2.14
) and employing the notation

\displaystyle K_{i}\equiv\partial_{i}K\equiv\frac{\partial K}{\partial\phi^{i}}\ ,

(1.2.20)

the contribution to the component action of the Kähler potential term (
1.2.18
) is equal to

\displaystyle\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,K\left(\Phi,\bar{\Phi}\right)=

\displaystyle K_{i\bar{j}}\left[-\partial_{\mu}\phi^{i}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{j}}-\left(\frac{i}{2}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{j}}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi^{i}+h.c.\right)+F^{i}\bar{F}^{\bar{j}}\right]

(1.2.21)

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{i}{2}K_{ij\bar{k}}\left(\psi^{i}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{k}}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{j}-i\psi^{i}\psi^{j}\bar{F}^{\bar{k}}\right)+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}K_{ij\bar{k}\bar{l}}\,\psi^{i}\psi^{j}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{k}}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{l}}\ .

The first line of this formula is the generalization of the kinetic lagrangian (
1.2.17
), which is characterised by the scalar field-dependent kinetic coefficient
K_{i\bar{j}}
, known as Kähler metric. Supersymmetric theories of this type have in fact a geometrical description in terms of the so-called Kähler geometry
[
44
]
. A Kähler manifold is a Riemannian manifold, parameterised here by the complex scalars
\phi^{i}
, whose metric is defined in terms of a real function
K(\phi,\bar{\phi})
, the Kähler potential, as

K_{i\bar{j}}=\partial_{i}\partial_{\bar{j}}K\ ,

(1.2.22)

in such a way to be invariant under the Kähler transformations (
1.2.19
). The Kähler geometry characterizes naturally the supersymmetric theories under consideration, since the general chiral superfield action (
1.2.18
) is invariant under Kähler transformations by construction.
Starting from the Kähler metric (
1.2.22
), of inverse
K^{i\bar{j}}
, one can define the associated connection and Riemann tensor as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{i}_{jk}=K^{i\bar{l}}K_{jk\bar{l}}\ ,

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\bar{i}}_{\bar{j}\bar{k}}=K^{l\bar{i}}K_{\bar{j}\bar{k}l}\ ,

\displaystyle R_{i\bar{j}k\bar{l}}=K_{m\bar{l}}\partial_{\bar{j}}\Gamma^{m}_{ik}\ ,

(1.2.23)

as well as covariant derivatives

\displaystyle D_{\mu}\psi^{i}=\partial_{\mu}\psi^{i}+\Gamma^{i}_{jk}\left(\sigma^{\mu}\psi^{j}\right)\partial_{\mu}\phi^{k}\ ,

\displaystyle D_{i}V_{j}=\partial_{i}V_{j}-\Gamma^{l}_{ij}V_{l}\ .

(1.2.24)

The Kähler term (
1.2.21
) already contains some interactions for the chiral supermultiplets. However, chiral superfields allows for a further extension of the supersymmetric action (
1.2.9
)-(
1.2.21
), given in terms of an holomorphic function
W\left(\Phi\right)
as

\int d^{2}\theta\,\,W\left(\Phi\right)+h.c.\ .

(1.2.25)

This superspace integral is supersymmetric because the
\theta^{2}
component of
W\left(\Phi\right)
, being a chiral superfield itself, transforms as a total spacetime derivative. The function
W\left(\Phi\right)
is called
superpotential
and describes the interactions of chiral superfields. The associated component lagrangian is given by

\int d^{2}\theta\,\,W\left(\Phi\right)=W_{i}F^{i}-\frac{1}{2}W_{ij}\psi^{i}\psi^{j}\ .

(1.2.26)

Therefore, a chiral superfield theory is completely specified by the Kähler potential
K\left(\Phi,\bar{\Phi}\right)
and the superpotential
W\left(\Phi\right)
. The complete superspace action is given by

\displaystyle S_{\textup{chiral}}=\int d^{4}x\left\{\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,K\left(\Phi,\bar{\Phi}\right)+\left(\int d^{2}\theta\,\,W\left(\Phi\right)+h.c.\right)\right\}\ ,

(1.2.27)

which turns into the off-shell lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\textup{chiral}}=

\displaystyle K_{i\bar{j}}\left[-\partial_{\mu}\phi^{i}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{j}}-\left(\frac{i}{2}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{j}}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi^{i}+h.c.\right)+F^{i}\bar{F}^{\bar{j}}\right]

(1.2.28)

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{i}{2}K_{ij\bar{k}}\left(\psi^{i}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{k}}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{j}-i\psi^{i}\psi^{j}\bar{F}^{\bar{k}}\right)+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}K_{ij\bar{k}\bar{l}}\,\psi^{i}\psi^{j}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{k}}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{l}}+\left(W_{i}F^{i}-\frac{1}{2}W_{ij}\psi^{i}\psi^{j}+h.c.\right)\ .

The auxiliary fields
F^{i}
can be integrated out explicitly in such theory. Their equation of motion fixes

F^{i}=-K^{i\bar{j}}\overline{W}_{\bar{j}}+\Gamma^{i}_{jk}\psi^{j}\psi^{k}\ ,

(1.2.29)

and therefore the on-shell supersymmetric lagrangian describing
n
chiral superfields and their interactions is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\textup{chiral}}=

\displaystyle-K_{i\bar{j}}\left[\partial_{\mu}\phi^{i}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{j}}+\left(\frac{i}{2}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{j}}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}D_{\mu}\psi^{i}+h.c.\right)\right]-K^{i\bar{j}}D_{i}W\bar{D}_{\bar{j}}\overline{W}

(1.2.30)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\left(D_{i}D_{j}W\psi^{i}\psi^{j}+h.c.\right)+\frac{1}{4}R_{i\bar{j}k\bar{l}}\psi^{i}\psi^{k}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{j}}\bar{\psi}^{\bar{l}}\ .

All the terms in this lagrangian have been written in a Kḧaler-geometrical form, according to eq. (
1.2.23
)-(
1.2.24
), so that
D_{i}W=W_{i}
and
D_{i}D_{j}W=W_{ij}-\Gamma^{l}_{ij}W_{l}
. In particular, the second term in the first line of (
1.2.30
) is the scalar potential of supersymmetric theories:

V\left(\phi,\bar{\phi}\right)=K^{i\bar{j}}D_{i}W\bar{D}_{\bar{j}}\overline{W}\ ,

(1.2.31)

which is again completely specified by the Kähler and superpotential.

An example of chiral superfield theory is the famous Wess–Zumino model
[
28
,
27
]
, which is the supersymmetric realisation of a Yukawa-type lagrangian. This model contains one chiral superfield
\Phi
, of Kähler and scalar potentials

\displaystyle K_{\textup{WZ}}\left(\Phi\right)=\bar{\Phi}\Phi\ ,

\displaystyle W_{\textup{WZ}}\left(\Phi\right)=\frac{m}{2}\Phi^{2}+\frac{\lambda}{3}\Phi^{3}\ ,

(1.2.32)

and results in the lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{\textup{WZ}}=-\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\bar{\phi}-\left(\frac{i}{2}\bar{\psi}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\psi+h.c.\right)-\frac{m}{2}\left(\psi\psi+\bar{\psi}\bar{\psi}\right)-\lvert m\phi+\lambda\phi^{2}\rvert^{2}-\lambda\left(\phi\psi\psi+h.c.\right)\ ,

(1.2.33)

which is left invariant by the transformations

\displaystyle\delta\phi=\sqrt{2}\epsilon\psi\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\psi=i\sqrt{2}\left(\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\right)\partial_{\mu}\phi-\sqrt{2}\left(m\bar{\phi}+\lambda\bar{\phi}^{2}\right)\epsilon\ ,

(1.2.34)

in agreement with (
1.1.38
).

1.2.2
Vector superfields

A vector superfield
V
is defined by the reality condition

V=\overline{V}\ .

(1.2.35)

The resulting superfield can be expanded as

\displaystyle V\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)=

\displaystyle C(x)+i\theta\chi(x)-i\bar{\theta}\bar{\chi}(x)-\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}A_{\mu}(x)

(1.2.36)

\displaystyle+\frac{i}{2}\theta^{2}\left[M(x)+iN(x)\right]-\frac{i}{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\left[M(x)-iN(x)\right]

\displaystyle+i\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}\left[\bar{\lambda}(x)+\frac{i}{2}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\chi(x)\right]-i\bar{\theta}^{2}\theta\left[\lambda(x)+\frac{i}{2}\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\chi}(x)\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}\left[D(x)+\Box C(x)\right]\ .

The supersymmetric version of the
\mathrm{U}(1)
gauge transformation is given by

V\quad\longrightarrow\quad V+i\Lambda-i\bar{\Lambda}\ ,

(1.2.37)

with
\Lambda\left(y,\theta\right)=\alpha\left(y\right)+\sqrt{2}\theta\psi(y)+\theta^{2}F(y)
being a chiral superfield. The
\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}
-term of this transformation reproduces in fact the standard gauge transformations on the field
A_{\mu}

A_{\mu}\quad\longrightarrow\quad A_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\left(\alpha+\bar{\alpha}\right)\ .

(1.2.38)

Moreover, there exists a choice of
\Lambda\left(y,\theta\right)
, called Wess–Zumino gauge, for which the component fields
C
,
\chi
,
M
and
N
can be removed. In this parameterization the vector superfield
V
takes the form

V\left(x,\theta,\bar{\theta}\right)=-\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}A_{\mu}(x)+i\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}\bar{\lambda}(x)-i\bar{\theta}^{2}\theta\lambda(x)+\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}D(x)\ ,

(1.2.39)

where
\lambda
and
D
are invariant under the gauge transformation (
1.2.37
). The fields
A_{\mu}
and
\lambda
are the degrees of freedom of the vector multiplet we introduced in eq. (
1.1.22
), while
D
is the auxiliary field of its off-shell formulation, analogous the one of the chiral multiplet. The supersymmetry transformations relating the degrees of freedom of this vector multiplet are
6
6

6

The supersymmetry transformations (
1.2.40
) actually do not preserve the Wess–Zumino gauge at the level of the superfield V of eq. (
1.2.39
) and need to be accompanied by a compensating gauge transformation of parameter
\Lambda=-i\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}A_{\mu}-\theta^{2}\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\lambda}
.

\displaystyle\delta A_{\mu}=i\left(\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\lambda-\bar{\lambda}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\epsilon\right)\ ,

(1.2.40)

\displaystyle\delta\lambda=iD\epsilon+F_{\mu\nu}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\delta D=-\epsilon\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\lambda}-\partial_{\mu}\lambda\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\ ,

where
F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}
. Notice that in the Wess–Zumino gauge the vector superfield
V
of eq. (
1.2.39
) can appear at most quadratically, since
V^{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}A^{\mu}A_{\mu}
and
V^{3}=0
.

In order to write a superspace lagrangian for the vector superfield (
1.2.35
), it is natural to define the superfield version of the gauge field strength
F_{\mu\nu}
. This object is given by

W_{\alpha}=-\frac{1}{4}\overline{D}^{2}D_{\alpha}V\ .

(1.2.41)

By construction, the super-field strength
W_{\alpha}
is invariant under the supersymmetric gauge transformations (
1.2.37
) and it is a chiral superfield,
i.e.

\overline{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}W_{\alpha}=0
. It is also such that
D^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}=\overline{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\overline{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}
. In the Wess–Zumino gauge it takes the form

W_{\alpha}\left(y,\theta\right)=-i\lambda_{\alpha}(y)+\left[D(y)\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}-iF_{\mu\nu}(y)\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right)_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\right]+\theta^{2}\left(\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\lambda}\right)_{\alpha}\ ,

(1.2.42)

and the lagrangian

\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\theta\,\,W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{4}\int d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,\overline{W}_{\dot{\alpha}}\overline{W}^{\dot{\alpha}}=-\frac{1}{4}F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}-\left(\frac{i}{2}\lambda\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\lambda}+h.c.\right)+\frac{1}{2}D^{2}\ ,

(1.2.43)

is the supersymmetric (off-shell) extension of the free Maxwell lagrangian.

There is yet another possible real, supersymmetric and gauge invariant action that one can write for abelian gauge groups (or abelian factors of non-abelian groups which are not semi-simple). The
\theta^{2}\bar{\theta}^{2}
-component of the real vector superfield (
1.2.39
),
i.e.
the auxiliary field
D
, is gauge invariant and transforms as a total spacetime derivative under supersymmetry (
1.2.40
). The associated lagrangian contribution

\mathcal{L}_{\textup{FI}}=2\xi\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,V=\xi D\ ,

(1.2.44)

is called Fayet–Iliopoulos term
[
45
]
and will play an important role in the context of supersymmetry breaking mechanisms.

Super-Yang–Mills theories

The non-abelian generalization of the above discussion is obtained extending the definition of vector superfield (
1.2.35
) and supersymmetric gauge transformations (
1.2.37
) to

\displaystyle V=V_{a}T^{a}\ ,

\displaystyle\Lambda=\Lambda_{a}T^{a}\ ,

\displaystyle\left[T^{a},T^{b}\right]=f^{a}{}_{bc}T^{c}\ ,

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\left(T^{a}T^{b}\right)=\delta^{ab}\ ,

(1.2.45)

where
T^{a}
are the generators of the non-abelian gauge group and
f_{abc}
the associated with the structure constants. In the Wess–Zumino gauge,
V_{a}
takes the form of eq. (
1.2.39
) for every
a
. Calling
g
the gauge coupling, the correct non-abelian super-field strength is

W_{\alpha}=-\frac{1}{8g}\overline{D}^{2}\left(e^{-2gV}D_{\alpha}e^{2gV}\right)\ .

(1.2.46)

Such field-strength
W_{\alpha}
transforms as
W_{\alpha}\to e^{-i\Lambda}W_{\alpha}e^{i\Lambda}
under gauge transformations, so that the Yang–Mills term
\text{Tr}\left(W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}\right)
is indeed gauge invariant. In the Wess–Zumino gauge it reduces to

W_{\alpha}=-\frac{1}{4}\overline{D}^{2}D_{\alpha}V+\frac{g}{4}\overline{D}^{2}\left[V,D_{\alpha}V\right]\ ,

(1.2.47)

which turns into

W^{a}_{\alpha}\left(y,\theta\right)=-i\lambda^{a}_{\alpha}(y)+\left[D^{a}(y)\delta_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}-iF^{a}_{\mu\nu}(y)\left(\sigma^{\mu\nu}\right)_{\alpha}{}^{\beta}\right]\theta_{\beta}+\theta^{2}\left(\sigma^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\bar{\lambda}^{a}\right)_{\alpha}\ ,

(1.2.48)

where, consistently, we have

\displaystyle F_{\mu\nu}^{a}=\partial_{\mu}A^{a}_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A^{a}_{\mu}-gf^{a}{}_{bc}A^{b}_{\mu}A^{c}_{\nu}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\lambda^{a}=\partial_{\mu}\lambda^{a}-gf^{a}{}_{bc}A^{b}_{\mu}\lambda^{c}\ .

(1.2.49)

Notice that the gauginos
\lambda^{a}
transform in the adjoint representation of the non-abelian gauge group, as they belong to the same multiplet as the gauge fields.

The supersymmetric coupling of gauge and matter fields,
i.e.
of vector (
1.2.35
) to chiral superfields (
1.2.11
) is obtained as follows. The supersymmetric gauge transformations of chiral superfields say in the fundamental representation is

\Phi\quad\longrightarrow\quad e^{-i\Lambda}\Phi\ ,

(1.2.50)

which respects the chiral structure of the superfield, and so a gauge invariant kinetic term has the form

\bar{\Phi}e^{2gV}\Phi\ .

(1.2.51)

Thus, an example of supersymmetric Yang–Mills theory, where a non-abelian vector multiplet is coupled to
n
chiral superfields of minimal kinetic term (
1.2.51
) and superpotential
W\left(\Phi\right)
(which must be gauge invariant), is given by

\mathcal{L}_{\textup{SYM}}=\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\,\,\bar{\Phi}e^{2gV}\Phi+\left(\int d^{2}\theta\left[\frac{1}{4}\text{Tr}\left(W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}\right)+W\left(\Phi\right)\right]+h.c.\right)\ .

(1.2.52)

The off-shell supersymmetry transformations characterizing this theory are

\displaystyle\delta\phi^{i}=\sqrt{2}\bar{\epsilon}\psi^{i}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta A^{a}_{\mu}=i\left(\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\lambda^{a}-\bar{\lambda}^{a}\bar{\sigma}_{\mu}\epsilon\right)\ ,

(1.2.53)

\displaystyle\delta\psi^{i}=i\sqrt{2}\left(\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\right)\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\phi^{i}+\sqrt{2}F^{i}\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\lambda^{a}=iD^{a}\epsilon+F^{a}_{\mu\nu}\sigma^{\mu\nu}\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\delta F^{i}=\sqrt{2}\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\psi+2ig\left(T_{a}\right)^{i}{}_{j}\phi^{j}\bar{\epsilon}\bar{\lambda}^{a}

\displaystyle\delta D^{a}=-\epsilon\sigma^{\mu}\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\bar{\lambda}^{a}-\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\lambda^{a}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\ ,

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\phi^{i}=\partial_{\mu}\phi^{i}+igA^{a}_{\mu}\left(T_{a}\right)^{i}{}_{j}\phi^{j}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mu}\psi^{i}=\partial_{\mu}\psi^{i}+igA^{a}_{\mu}\left(T_{a}\right)^{i}{}_{j}\psi^{j}\,

(1.2.54)

are the fundamental representation covariant derivatives. A very important element of theories of this type is again the scalar potential. The coupling to vector multiplets introduces a new term in the scalar potential, associated with the
D^{a}
auxiliary fields and thus called D-term, which adds the so-called
F
-term coming from the superpotential
W\left(\Phi\right)
(
1.2.31
). In the case of the theory (
1.2.52
), it is given by

V\left(\phi,\bar{\phi}\right)=\sum_{i}\lvert F^{i}\rvert^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a}D^{a}D_{a}\ ,

(1.2.55)

and the on-shell equations for the auxiliary fields are

\displaystyle F^{i}=-\overline{W}_{\bar{i}}\ ,

\displaystyle D^{a}=-g\left(T^{a}\right)_{ij}\phi^{i}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{j}}\ .

(1.2.56)

The scalar potential in eq. (
1.2.55
)-(
1.2.56
) can easily be further generalized by including a non-minimal Kähler potential, as well as Fayet–Iliopoulos terms (
1.2.44
) (see eq. (
1.3.1
)), and it is the key element to study the phenomenon of spontaneous supersymmetry breaking, as we do in the next section.

1.3
Spontaneous breaking and nonlinear realisations of supersymmetry

The breaking of supersymmetry is a central feature of supersymmetric theories. It gives rise to rich and complex dynamics and, most importantly, is essential for supersymmetry to be phenomenologically viable. Since the Standard Model is clearly non-supersymmetric, supersymmetry breaking plays a key role in the physics beyond the Standard Model and, more generally, in mechanisms that connect the observed low-energy world to higher energy scales.

Here we focus on the case where supersymmetry is broken
spontaneously
.
This is a natural scenario from a high-energy perspective, where supersymmetry is expected to be realized as a local symmetry, namely as supergravity. Restricting ourselves to the
\mathcal{N}=1
(rigid) case, the spontaneous breaking of supersymmetry is entirely governed by the scalar potential. Since the vacuum state of the theory must be a Lorentz-invariant quantity, only scalar fields can acquire a non-trivial vacuum expectation value. Furthermore, as discussed in Section
1.1
, a general consequence of the Super-Poincaré algebra is that the energy in a supersymmetric theory is semi-positive definite, as stated in Eq. (
1.1.11
). It follows that supersymmetric vacua correspond to the zeros of the scalar potential, while any non-zero minimum defines a vacuum in which supersymmetry is spontaneously broken. This is illustrated in Figure
1.1
.

Figure 1.1:
Basic representation of supersymmetry preserving versus supersymmetry breaking vacua.

The general form of the scalar potential in a supersymmetric field theory that couples vector and chiral multiplets was given in eq. (
1.2.55
). Off-shell, it is a function of the auxiliary fields
F^{i}
and
D^{a}
, which is indeed semi-positive definite, in agreement with eq. (
1.1.11
). Therefore, supersymmetric vacua are determined by the conditions

\displaystyle F^{i}=-K^{i\bar{j}}\overline{W}_{\bar{j}}=0\ ,

\displaystyle D^{a}=-g\left(T^{a}\right)_{ij}\phi^{i}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{j}}-\xi^{a}=0\ ,

(1.3.1)

where we included a non-minimal Kähler metric
7
7

7

Although it does modify the shape of the scalar potential, a (non-singular) non-minimal Kähler metric does not influence the F-term vanishing condition in eq. (
1.3.1
), which is any case given by
W_{i}=0
.
and the Fayet–Iliopoulos terms (
1.2.44
). The scalar field values for which both the F and D-term equations are solved is called the moduli space. If the F-term and/or the D-term equations cannot be solved, the theory breaks supersymmetry spontaneously. We now discuss simple models which realize the two possibilities.

D-term breaking and the Fayet–Iliopoulos term

Fayet–Iliopoulos terms
[
45
]
generally introduce a positive contribution to the vacuum energy and realize the spontaneous breaking of supersymmetry in supersymmetric gauge theories. For instance, we take the following theory of an abelian vector superfield
V
and two chiral superfields
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
:

\mathcal{L}=\int d^{2}\theta d^{2}\bar{\theta}\left(\bar{\Phi}_{1}e^{2gV}\Phi_{1}+\bar{\Phi}_{2}e^{-2gV}\Phi_{2}+2\xi V\right)+\left[\int d^{2}\theta\left(\frac{1}{4}W^{\alpha}W_{\alpha}+m\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\right)+h.c.\right]\ .

(1.3.2)

The scalar potential (
1.2.55
) in this model is given by

V=m^{2}\left(\lvert\phi_{1}\rvert^{2}+\lvert\phi_{2}\rvert^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left[g\left(\lvert\phi_{1}\rvert^{2}-\lvert\phi_{2}\rvert^{2}\right)+2\xi\right]\ ,

(1.3.3)

and the F-term and D-term equations (
1.3.1
) are

\displaystyle F_{1}=-m\bar{\phi}_{2}=0\ ,

\displaystyle F_{2}=-m\bar{\phi}_{1}=0\ ,

\displaystyle D=-\xi-g\left(\lvert\phi_{1}\rvert^{2}-\lvert\phi_{2}\rvert^{2}\right)=0\ .

(1.3.4)

These equations cannot be solved simultaneously because of the nontrivial Fayet–Iliopoulos term, and supersymmetry is thus broken spontaneously.

F-term breaking

We now turn to the case where supersymmetry is broken through an F-term. A generic superpotential
W\left(\Phi\right)
depending on
n
chiral superfields
\Phi_{i}
, can be written as the polynomial sum

W\left(\Phi\right)=a_{i}\Phi_{i}+\frac{1}{2}b_{ij}\Phi^{i}\Phi^{j}+\frac{1}{3}c_{ijk}\Phi^{i}\Phi^{j}\Phi^{k}+\dots\ .

(1.3.5)

For a minimal Kähler potential, the F-term equation (
1.3.1
) takes then the form

a_{i}+b_{ij}\phi^{j}+c_{ijk}\phi^{i}\phi^{j}+\dots=0\ .

(1.3.6)

Hence, spontaneous supersymmetry breaking is possible if at least one of the coefficients
a_{i}
is different from zero, namely if there is at least a linear term in the superpotential (
1.3.5
). Otherwise, there always exist a supersymmetric vacuum, for some/all the scalar fields set to zero.

The Polonyi model

The so-called Polonyi model is the simplest instance of F-term supersymmetry breaking. It describes a single chiral superfield
\Phi
with linear superpotential:

\displaystyle K\left(\Phi,\bar{\Phi}\right)=\bar{\Phi}\Phi\ ,

\displaystyle W\left(\Phi\right)=f\Phi\ .

(1.3.7)

The scalar potential is constant
V=\lvert f\rvert^{2}
and supersymmetry is clearly broken in the whole field space.

The O’Raifeartaigh model

A more sophisticated and widely studied setup for spontaneous supersymmetry breaking is the so-called The O’Raifeartaigh model
[
46
,
47
]
. The simplest O’Raifeartaigh setup amounts to three chiral superfields
\Phi_{1}
,
\Phi_{2}
and
X
, with canonical Kähler potential and interacting through the superpotential

W_{\text{O'R}}=\frac{h}{2}X\Phi_{1}^{2}+m\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}+fX\ ,

(1.3.8)

with
h
,
m
and
f
positive coefficients. The F-term equations are
8
8

8

We employ the same notation, namely
X
, for the superfield and its bottom scalar field component.

\displaystyle\bar{F}_{1}

\displaystyle=-hX\phi_{1}-m\phi_{2}=0\ ,

(1.3.9)

\displaystyle\bar{F}_{2}

\displaystyle=-m\phi_{1}=0\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{F}_{X}

\displaystyle=-\frac{h}{2}\phi_{1}^{2}-f=0\ ,

and cannot be solved all at the same time. O’Raifeartaigh models are characterized by interaction terms engineered precisely to give rise to a set of F-term equations whose solutions are in conflict with each other. The model (
1.3.8
) is actually characterized by a (pseudo)moduli space of supersymmetry breaking vacua. Defining the parameter

y\equiv\left\lvert\frac{hf}{m^{2}}\right\rvert\ ,

(1.3.10)

for
y<1
this (pseudo)moduli space is parameterized by
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=0
and arbitrary
X
. In the
y>1
case, the (pseudo)moduli spaces are instead two, given by

\displaystyle\phi_{2}=-\frac{h}{m}X\phi\ ,

\displaystyle\phi_{1}=\pm i\sqrt{\frac{2f}{h}\left(\frac{y-1}{y}\right)}\ ,

(1.3.11)

and
X
still being a flat direction.

This vacuum degeneracy is actually lifted by 1-loop corrections
[
47
]
, which are generally given by the Coleman–Weinberg potential
[
48
]
. Moreover, supersymmetry can be restored by deforming the superpotential (
1.3.8
) with a term breaking the R-symmetry it originally enjoys.
9
9

9

The R-charges of the superfields entering the superpotential (
1.3.8
) are
\text{R}_{X}=\text{R}_{\Phi_{2}}=2
and
\text{R}_{\Phi_{1}}=0
.
For (parametrically) small enough R-symmetry breaking, the new supersymmetric vacuum is pushed far away with respect to the old one, which survives in this setup as a metastable vacuum
[
47
]
.

1.3.1
The Goldstino and nonlinear supersymmetry

When a symmetry is spontaneously broken, the Goldstone theorem predicts the existence of massless particles, associated with the number of broken generators,
i.e.
that do not leave the vacuum invariant
[
49
,
50
,
51
]
. Supersymmetry makes, of course, no exception, and having anticommuting generators the Goldstone particle must be a spin-
\frac{1}{2}
fermion, known as the
goldstino
.

Let us focus on the model (
1.2.52
). When supersymmetry is broken spontaneously, either via an F-term
\langle F^{i}\rangle\neq 0
or a D-term
\langle D^{a}\rangle\neq 0
, the transformations of the fermions (
1.2.53
) become inhomogeneous:

\displaystyle\langle\delta\psi^{i}\rangle\sim\langle F^{i}\rangle\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\langle\delta\lambda^{a}\rangle\sim\langle D^{a}\rangle\epsilon\ ,

(1.3.12)

and the goldstino mode
G
is given as the following combinations of fermionic fields

G=\frac{1}{f}\left(\langle F_{i}\rangle\psi^{i}+\langle D_{a}\rangle\lambda^{a}\right)\ ,

(1.3.13)

where
f=\langle\lvert F^{i}\rvert^{2}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle D^{a}D_{a}\rangle
is the scale of supersymmetry breaking. The combination (
1.3.13
) is in fact an eigenvector of vanishing eigenvalue of the fermionic mass matrix,

-\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{i}&\lambda^{a}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}W_{ij}&-i\sqrt{2}g\bar{\phi}^{\bar{j}}\left(T_{b}\right)_{i\bar{j}}\\
-i\sqrt{2}g\bar{\phi}^{j}\left(T_{a}\right)_{ij}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{j}\\
\lambda^{b}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(1.3.14)

originating from the lagrangian in eq. (
1.2.52
).
