The inhomogeneous supersymmetry transformations of the fermions (
1.3.12
) and the goldstino dynamics that emerges can be elegantly understood in terms of a
nonlinear realization
of the supersymmetry algebra. Nonlinear supersymmetry relates a fermion to a scalar combination of the fermion itself, while still satisfying the supersymmetry algebra (
1.1.7
). To develop such formalism, one starts from a fermionic field
\chi\left(x\right)
whose supersymmetry transformation is taken to be
[
52
]

\chi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\chi\left(x\right)+f\epsilon\ ,

(1.3.15)

in which
f
is a real coefficient of mass dimension 2, while
x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+i\left(\epsilon\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\theta}-\theta\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}\right)
is the transformation of the spacetime coordinate
x^{\mu}
in superspace, as in eq. (
1.2.1
). The transformation (
1.3.15
) is actually the generalization of the other coordinate transformation
\theta^{\prime}=\theta+\epsilon
(
1.2.1
) — which are similar in form to the transformations in eq. (
1.3.12
) — to an arbitrary spinor field. The infinitesimal field variation at equal spacetime point is then

\delta_{\epsilon}\chi\left(x\right)=\chi^{\prime}\left(x\right)-\chi\left(x\right)=f\epsilon+\frac{i}{f}\left[\chi\left(x\right)\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}-\epsilon\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\chi}\left(x\right)\right]\partial_{\mu}\chi\left(x\right)\ ,

(1.3.16)

which satisfies the supersymmetry algebra (
1.1.7
):

\left[\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\right]\chi=-2i\left(\epsilon_{1}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}_{2}-\epsilon_{2}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\epsilon}_{1}\right)\partial_{\mu}\chi\ .

(1.3.17)

Therefore, the nonlinear transformation (
1.3.15
) is a supersymmetry transformation, relating the spinor
\chi
to a scalar combination of
\chi
itself. Invariant lagrangians are then constructed in a geometric way, starting from the generalized vielbein

a_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}=\delta_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}+\frac{i}{f^{2}}\left(\chi\sigma^{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\chi}-\partial_{\mu}\chi\sigma^{\alpha}\bar{\chi}\right)\ .

(1.3.18)

The determinant
\det a
transforms in fact as a total spacetime derivative under the supersymmetry transformation (
1.3.16
) — which is interpreted as a diffeomorphism for the vielbein
a_{\mu}{}^{\alpha}
(
1.3.18
) — and thus defines the supersymmetric lagrangian

\mathcal{L}_{\text{VA}}=-\frac{f^{2}}{2}\det a=-\frac{f^{2}}{2}-\frac{i}{2}\left(\chi\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\chi}-\partial_{\mu}\chi\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\chi}\right)+\dots\ ,

(1.3.19)

where the dots stand for higher-derivative self-interactions of the spinor
\chi
. This lagrangian is the famous Volkov–Akulov lagrangian of nonlinear supersymmetry
[
52
]
. The positive vacuum energy, proportional to the constant
f
, spontaneously breaks linear supersymmetry, leaving though a residual nonlinear supersymmetry (
1.3.16
), realized in terms of the massless fermion
\chi
, which is then identified with the goldstino.

1.4
Constrained superfields

Theories with spontaneously broken supersymmetry present a fermionic Goldstone mode, the goldstino (
1.3.13
), on which the original supersymmetry is now nonlinearly realized (
1.3.16
). Such nonlinear supersymmetry completely determines the dynamics of the goldstino, which is of the Volkov–Akulov type (
1.3.19
)
[
52
]
. In particular, the residual nonlinear supersymmetry should also characterize the interactions of the goldstino with the other fields in the theory.

As we will see more in detail in Section
2.1.2
, in supergravity, that is, when supersymmetry becomes a local symmetry, the fermionic version of the Higgs mechanism takes place
[
53
]
: the massless goldstino is eaten by the gauge field of local supersymmetry, the spin-
\frac{3}{2}

gravitino
, which becomes massive. The original goldstino corresponds now to the longitudinal polarizations of the massive gravitino. However, if the supersymmetry breaking scale
f
is well below the Planck mass
M_{\textup{P}}
, the low-energy (
i.e.
at a scale below
f
) interactions of such longitudinal modes of the massive gravitino dominate over those of the original transverse ones, since the former scale as
\nicefrac{{1}}{{f}}
while the latter as
\nicefrac{{1}}{{M_{\textup{P}}}}
. This property is expressed in general terms by the gravitino equivalence theorem
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
, so that at energies below the supersymmetry breaking scale — but above the gravitino mass — the couplings of the gravitino are controlled by the longitudinal mode and can be studied in terms of the goldstino interactions in the rigid limit. From this point of view, nonlinear supersymmetry and the associated goldstino dynamics are a direct manifestation of the high-energy physics at lower scales, in this case even with gravity decoupled.

A particularly simple and effective tool to construct effective theories invariant under nonlinear supersymmetry is the so-called
constrained superfield
formalism
[
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
,
61
]
. The idea is that extra constraints are imposed on superfields, which effectively remove some of their components, simulating the decoupling of a massive degree of freedom while allowing one to use the standard supersymmetric lagrangians. In this framework, the goldstino
G
is embedded into a chiral superfield
S
subject to the nilpotency condition
[
57
,
62
,
63
,
59
]

S^{2}=0\ .

(1.4.1)

This constraint fixes
S
to be of the form

S=\frac{G^{2}}{2F_{S}}+\sqrt{2}\theta G+\theta^{2}F_{S}\ ,

(1.4.2)

namely it determines the bottom scalar component of the superfield to be a function of the fermion
G
and the auxiliary field
F_{S}
. Notice that
F_{S}
must be different from zero in order for the nilpotent constraint (
1.4.1
) to be solved, signaling already the breaking of supersymmetry. Moreover, the supersymmetry transformation of
S
now relates the fermion
G
to the bilinear
s\sim G^{2}
of the fermion itself. Thus, supersymmetry is realized nonlinearly on the nilpotent superfield
S
, so that its fermionic component
G
is identified with the goldstino of supersymmetry breaking. The most general 2-derivative theory that one can write with
S
is given by

\displaystyle K\left(S,\bar{S}\right)=\bar{S}S\ ,

\displaystyle W\left(S\right)=fS\ .

(1.4.3)

The associated on-shell lagrangian, obtained after integrating out the auxiliary field as

F_{S}=-f\left(1+\frac{\bar{G}^{2}}{4f^{4}}\Box G^{2}-\frac{3}{16f^{8}}G^{2}\bar{G}^{2}\Box G^{2}\Box\bar{G}^{2}\right)\ ,

(1.4.4)

is found to be

\mathcal{L}_{S}=-f^{2}-iG\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\bar{G}+\frac{1}{4f^{2}}\bar{G}^{2}\Box G^{2}-\frac{1}{16f^{6}}G^{2}\bar{G}^{2}\Box G^{2}\Box\bar{G}^{2}\ ,

(1.4.5)

which can be shown to be equivalent to the Volkov–Akulov lagrangian (
1.3.19
)
[
52
]
by means of a nonlinear field redefinition
[
62
,
63
,
64
]
.

Therefore, the nilpotent superfield
S
correctly describes the goldstino field and the associated nonlinear supersymmetry. Its coupling to other fields allows then to construct in a very direct way effective theories of spontaneously broken supersymmetry. Following these lines, the superfield
S
also allows for the definition of further superfield constraints, which decouple superpartners in different ways, providing a richer spectrum of effective field theories of nonlinearly realized supersymmetry.
[
59
]
. This constraining procedure through the nilpotent goldstino superfield (
1.4.1
) can be generalised into a prescription that allows one to remove specific, single superfield components
[
61
]
.

However, despite being algebraically clear, this procedure is not always physically well-defined
[
1
]
. This is the case when the given superfield constraint also affects the auxiliary field of the constrained multiplet. In fact, whereas the removal by the constraints of a scalar or fermion can naturally fit with the microscopic picture of the standard decoupling of heavy particles, the same interpretation does not apply to auxiliary fields — which are not dynamical and, hence, are not associated with any physical mass — and their removal requires higher-derivative couplings directly in the UV, as proposed already in
[
61
]
. This feature manifests in low-energy goldstino lagrangians in terms of higher-derivative operators which are subject to positivity bounds in order to be consistent
[
65
,
66
]
, and therefore cannot originate from a 2-derivative action in the UV. On the other hand, any constraint that does not affect the auxiliary fields does not suffer from any causality issue of this type and so, as expected from the physical point of view, there is no obstruction to an uplift to a 2-derivative UV theory.

This is the main result of the paper
[
1
]
, subject of the present thesis, which we now discuss in detail.
We begin by analyzing two examples of superfield constraints, proposed in
[
59
]
and further studied in
[
1
]
. The theories defined by these constraints describe the (on-shell) coupling of the goldstino to a real and a complex scalar field, respectively. After introducing the constraints, we compute the most general lagrangian they determine and show that they involve operators that may lead to superluminal goldstino propagation unless appropriate positivity bounds on the scattering amplitudes are taken into account. Then, we provide evidence that the obstruction to a UV completion of such effective theories is related to the non-trivial solution imposed by such constraints on the auxiliary fields. We do so first studying a third superfield constraint, that couples the goldstino to a fermion and which leaves untouched the auxiliary field, and show that the resulting lagrangian does not contain any dangerous operator. Second, we refine the models that suffer from potential acausal behavior through the generalized constraints of
[
61
]
, by constructing effective models with the same field spectrum of the ill-defined ones but without touching the auxiliary fields. The resulting theories are found to be completely causal in the whole field space.

1.4.1
Coupling to a real scalar – the orthogonal constraint

The first superfield constraint induced by the goldstino superfield (
1.4.1
) is the so-called
orthogonal constraint
, which acts on a chiral superfield as

S\left(\Phi-\bar{\Phi}\right)=0\ ,

(1.4.6)

which implies also
\left(\Phi-\bar{\Phi}\right)^{3}=0
. Calling the
\Phi
components

\displaystyle\Phi=\phi+\sqrt{2}\theta\chi_{\phi}+\theta^{2}F_{\phi}\ ,

\displaystyle\phi=A+iB\ ,

(1.4.7)

the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
) is solved by
[
59
]

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=-\frac{G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}}{2\lvert F_{S}\rvert^{2}}\partial_{\mu}A\ ,

(1.4.8)

\displaystyle\chi_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-i\sigma^{\mu}\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\partial_{\mu}\phi\ ,

\displaystyle F_{\phi}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\nu}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\sigma^{\nu}\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\partial_{\mu}A+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)^{2}\Box A\ .

The resulting on-shell degrees of freedom are the goldstino
G
and a real scalar
A
. Because of such minimal spectrum, the orthogonal constraint played a significant role in defining minimal models of inflation in supergravity
[
67
,
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
]
. We will discuss this in Section
2.2
.

The most general theories coupling the nilpotent superfield
S
(
1.4.1
) and such orthogonally-constrained superfield
\Phi
(
1.4.6
) involves the following Kähler and superpotential
[
67
]
:

\displaystyle K=h\left(\mathcal{A}\right)\mathcal{B}^{2}+\bar{S}S\ ,

\displaystyle W=f\left(\Phi\right)S+g\left(\Phi\right)\ ,

(1.4.9)

where the function
h
is real, while
f
and
g
are holomorphic, and where we employed the definition

\displaystyle\Phi=\mathcal{A}+i\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\Phi+\bar{\Phi}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\frac{1}{2i}\left(\Phi-\bar{\Phi}\right)\ ,

(1.4.10)

Then, making use of the general formula (
1.2.28
), one obtains the following off-shell lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle\lvert F_{S}\rvert^{2}+\frac{h}{2}\partial_{\mu}A\partial^{\mu}A-\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}+h.c.\right)+\frac{1}{4}\frac{{\bar{G}}^{2}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\Box\left(\frac{G^{2}}{F_{S}}\right)

(1.4.11)

\displaystyle+\frac{h}{2}\left[i\,\partial^{\mu}\left(\frac{G}{F_{S}}\right)\sigma^{\nu}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)\partial_{\mu}A\partial_{\nu}A-\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}\left(\frac{G}{F_{S}}\right)\sigma^{\mu}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)(\partial A)^{2}+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle+\left[fF_{S}+\frac{i}{2}f^{\prime}\frac{G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\partial_{\mu}A+\frac{g^{\prime\prime}}{2}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)^{2}(\partial A)^{2}+\frac{g^{\prime}}{2}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)^{2}\Box A\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad-g^{\prime}\partial_{\nu}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\sigma^{\nu}\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\partial_{\mu}A+h.c.\right].

Next, one has to integrate out the goldstino auxiliary field
F_{S}
. The solution to its equations of motion can be found iteratively, expanding in the number of fermions. Truncating such solution to the quadratic order in goldstinos — which corresponds to quartic operators in the lagrangian — one finds the on-shell spacetime lagrangian
[
1
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\lvert f\rvert^{2}-\frac{h}{2}\partial_{\mu}A\partial^{\mu}A+\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}+h.c.\right)-\left(i\frac{f^{\prime}}{2f}+h.c.\right)G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}A

(1.4.12)

\displaystyle+\frac{h}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left[i\,\partial^{\mu}G\sigma^{\nu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}A\partial_{\nu}A-\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\partial_{\nu}A\partial^{\nu}A+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime}}{\bar{f}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}G^{2}\Box A-\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}G\partial_{\nu}A\right)+\xi\,G^{2}\,\partial_{\mu}A\partial^{\mu}A+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\bar{G}^{2}\Box G^{2}\ ,

in which

\xi\equiv\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime\prime}}{2\bar{f}^{2}}-\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime}\bar{f}^{\prime}}{\bar{f}^{3}}\ .

(1.4.13)

The lagrangian (
1.4.12
) captures exactly all the terms quadratic in the goldstino. An alternative form of this lagrangian is obtained performing the following field redefinitions:

\displaystyle\delta G^{\alpha}=\frac{h}{4\lvert f\rvert^{2}}(\partial A)^{2}\,G^{\alpha}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta G^{\alpha}=-i\frac{g^{\prime}}{f^{2}}(\bar{G}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu})^{\alpha}\,\partial_{\mu}A\ ,

\displaystyle\delta A=\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime}}{2h\bar{f}^{2}}G^{2}+h.c\ ,

(1.4.14)

which in the quartic limit yield

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\lvert f\rvert^{2}-\frac{h}{2}\partial_{\mu}A\partial^{\mu}A+\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}+h.c.\right)+\left(mG^{2}+h.c.\right)+\lambda G^{2}\bar{G}^{2}

(1.4.15)

\displaystyle-\left(i\frac{f^{\prime}}{2f}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}A+\tilde{\xi}G^{2}\partial_{\mu}A\partial^{\mu}A+h.c.\right)-\frac{1}{8h}\left(\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime 2}}{\bar{f}^{4}}G^{2}\Box G^{2}+h.c.\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(h-\frac{2\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)\left(i\,\partial^{\mu}G\sigma^{\nu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}A\partial_{\nu}A+h.c.\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(1-\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{h\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)\bar{G}^{2}\Box G^{2}\ ,

where the coefficients
m
,
\lambda
and
\tilde{\xi}
are short-hand notations for

\displaystyle m=\frac{g^{\prime}f^{\prime}}{hf}\ ,

\displaystyle\lambda=\frac{\left(f^{\prime}{}^{2}+ff^{\prime\prime}\right)g^{\prime}{}^{2}}{2h^{2}f^{4}}\ ,

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}=\xi-\frac{g^{\prime}h^{\prime}}{4hf^{2}}\ .

(1.4.16)

In this field basis, the dependence on the quadratic goldstino operators is not exact anymore, but it makes manifest some peculiar quartic interactions, which will be subject to positivity bounds.

Causality constraints

The lagrangians (
1.4.12
)-(
1.4.15
) involve peculiar derivative self-interactions of the goldstino
G
. This is directly related to the fact that the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
) implies a nontrivial solution for the auxiliary field
F_{\phi}
, as in (
1.4.8
). Focusing on the quartic-limit lagrangian (
1.4.15
), such operators are directly related to nontrivial positivity bounds on the scattering amplitudes
[
65
,
66
,
72
]
. The relevant
2\to 2
scattering amplitudes, twice subtracted and in the forward limit, are

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,\bar{G}\to G,\bar{G})

\displaystyle=\frac{h\lvert f\rvert^{2}-\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{h\lvert f\rvert^{4}}s^{2}

(1.4.17)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,G\to\bar{G},\bar{G})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\bar{g}^{\prime 2}}{h\bar{f}^{4}}s^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(G,A\to G,A)

\displaystyle=\frac{h\lvert f\rvert^{2}-2\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{2\lvert f\rvert^{4}}s^{2}\ ,

and they are found, following
[
66
,
72
,
73
,
74
]
, to be subject to the following positivity bounds:

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\mathcal{A}\left(G,\bar{G}\to G,\bar{G}\right)_{\big|_{s=t=0}}

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle h\lvert f\rvert^{2}\geq\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}\ ,

(1.4.18)

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\mathcal{A}\left(G,A\to G,A\right)_{\big|_{s=t=0}}

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle h\lvert f\rvert^{2}\geq 2\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}}{ds^{2}}\left[\mathcal{A}\left(G,\bar{G}\to G,\bar{G}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\lvert\mathcal{A}\left(G,G\to\bar{G},\bar{G}\right)\rvert\right]_{\big|_{s=t=0}}

\displaystyle\geq 0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle h\lvert f\rvert^{2}\geq 2\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}\ ,

which thus yield
[
1
]

h\left(0\right)\lvert f\left(0\right)\rvert^{2}\geq 2\lvert g^{\prime}\left(0\right)\rvert^{2}\ .

(1.4.19)

The positivity bounds (
1.4.18
)-(
1.4.19
) actually hold in the vacuum expectation value for the scalar field
A
, which can be taken to be
A=0
in full generality. The higher-order terms in the expansions around such vacuum are actually operators of higher-order in the number of fields and do not contribute to the amplitudes of eq. (
1.4.17
).

However, we argue in
[
1
]
that the vacuum positivity bound (
1.4.19
) must nevertheless be extended to the whole functions domain in order to have a causal propagation of the goldstino
G
. This is related to the sound speed parameter, which parameterize the propagation of the goldstino and in presence of non-minimal couplings and a nontrivial background can develop peculiar features like the possibility to vanish and also to become superluminal
[
69
,
70
,
1
]
. This property was actually studied for the massive gravitino in spontaneously broken supergravity
[
75
,
71
,
76
,
69
,
77
,
78
,
70
,
79
]
, but it directly relates to the goldstino in the rigid limit by means of the equivalence theorem
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
. In the case at hand, the sound speed can be computed exactly from the quadratic-limit lagrangian (
1.4.12
), since it contains all the operators quadratic in the goldstino, and assuming an homogeneous background solution for the scalar field
A=A\left(t\right)
.
10
10

10

Derivatives with respect to the time
t
will be denoted with a dot,
i.e.

\partial_{0}A\equiv\dot{A}
.

Working in momentum space, parameterized for the goldstino by
k^{\mu}=\left(k_{0},0,0,k_{3}\right)
, the quadratic equation of motion for
G
that follows from the lagrangian (
1.4.12
) is found to be
[
1
]

\left\{\left(1+\frac{h\dot{A}{}^{2}}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)^{2}k_{0}^{2}-\left[\left(1-\frac{h\dot{A}{}^{2}}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)^{2}+4\dot{A}{}^{2}\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\lvert f\rvert^{4}}\right]k_{3}^{3}\right\}G_{\alpha}=0\ ,

(1.4.20)

which yields the time-dependent sound speed

c_{s}^{2}=1-\frac{4\dot{A}{}^{2}}{\left(\lvert f(A)\rvert^{2}+\frac{h(A)\dot{A}{}^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}}\left(\frac{h(A)}{2}\lvert f(A)\rvert^{2}-\lvert g^{\prime}(A)\rvert^{2}\right)\ .

(1.4.21)

Therefore, in order to have subluminal goldstino propagation,
i.e.

c_{s}^{2}\leq 1
, at all times one must require

h\left(A\right)\lvert f\left(A\right)\rvert^{2}\geq 2\lvert g^{\prime}\left(A\right)\rvert^{2}\ ,

(1.4.22)

which is indeed the extension of the positivity bound (
1.4.19
) to all field space. This result is coherent with
[
72
,
71
]
and with the longitudinal gravitino dynamics in supergravity
[
77
,
69
,
70
]
.

The causality bound (
1.4.22
) implies that the theory (
1.4.9
), despite being the most general one with respect to the nonlinear supersymmetry setup defined by the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), is not automatically well-defined. From the low-energy effective field theory point of view, we interpret this as an obstruction to a completion into a microscopic 2-derivative theory, which instead requires higher-derivative operators already in the UV
[
61
]
. We will substantiate this claim in Section
1.4.3
.

1.4.2
Coupling to a complex scalar

The second model we examine, very similar to the orthogonal constraint one, involves again a chiral superfield

\mathcal{H}=H+\sqrt{2}\theta\chi_{H}+\theta^{2}F_{H}\ ,

(1.4.23)

subject now to the constraint

S\overline{\mathcal{H}}=\text{chiral}\ .

(1.4.24)

This constraint is solved by
[
59
]

\displaystyle\chi_{H}

\displaystyle=-i\sigma^{\mu}\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\partial_{\mu}H\ ,

(1.4.25)

\displaystyle F_{H}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\nu}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\sigma^{\nu}\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\partial_{\mu}H+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)^{2}\Box H\ ,

and thus describes the coupling of the goldstino to a complex scalar
H
. The most general Kähler and superpotential for such constrained superfields are
11
11

11

The goldstino kinetic term in the Kähler potential (
1.4.26
) is canonical since any non-minimal coefficient
\xi\left(\mathcal{H},\overline{\mathcal{H}}\right)
can actually be reabsorbed into the goldstino superfield as
S\to\xi^{-\frac{1}{2}}S
, which preserves the chiral nature of
S
because of the constraints (
1.4.1
) and (
1.4.24
)
[
60
]
. The whole dependence on
\xi
is then redefined away up to terms in the lagrangian which are beyond the quartic order in the fields discussed here.

\displaystyle K=\kappa\left(\mathcal{H},\overline{\mathcal{H}}\right)+\bar{S}S\ ,

\displaystyle W\left(\mathcal{H}\right)=f\left(\mathcal{H}\right)S+g\left(\mathcal{H}\right)\ ,

(1.4.26)

where
f
and
g
are holomorphic functions. Proceeding then in the same way as for the orthogonal constraint, the on-shell lagrangian is determined by (
1.4.26
) is
[
1
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\lvert f\rvert^{2}+\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}{\bar{G}}+h.c.\right)-\kappa_{H\bar{H}}\partial_{\mu}H\partial^{\mu}{\bar{H}}

(1.4.27)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\lvert f\rvert^{2}}{\bar{G}}^{2}\Box G^{2}+\frac{\kappa_{H\bar{H}}}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(i\partial^{\mu}G\sigma^{\nu}{\bar{G}}+h.c.\right)\left(\partial_{\mu}H\partial_{\nu}{\bar{H}}+\partial_{\nu}H\partial_{\mu}{\bar{H}}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{\kappa_{H\bar{H}}}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(i\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}+h.c.\right)\partial_{\nu}H\partial^{\nu}\bar{H}-\left[i\frac{f_{H}}{f}G\sigma^{\mu}{\bar{G}}\partial_{\mu}H+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\kappa_{H\bar{H}}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left[\frac{1}{2}G\sigma^{\mu}{\bar{G}}\left(i\,\partial_{\mu}H\Box{\bar{H}}+h.c.\right)-\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma_{\nu}{\bar{G}}\partial_{\alpha}H\partial_{\beta}{\bar{H}}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{g^{\prime}}{f^{2}}{\bar{G}}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\sigma^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}{\bar{G}}\partial_{\mu}H-\frac{g^{\prime\prime}}{2f^{2}}{\bar{G}}^{2}(\partial H)^{2}-\frac{g^{\prime}}{2f^{2}}{\bar{G}}^{2}\Box H+h.c.\right]\ .

This is the lagrangian in what we refer to as the quadratic limit,
i.e.
it is exact in the operators quadratic in the goldstino
G
, as the orthogonal constraint lagrangian (
1.4.12
). One can switch to the quartic-limit basis through the field redefinitions

\displaystyle\delta G^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\kappa_{H\bar{H}}}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\partial_{\mu}H\partial^{\mu}\bar{H}G^{\alpha}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta H

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}H\ ,

(1.4.28)

\displaystyle\delta G^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=i\frac{g^{\prime}}{f^{2}}({\bar{G}}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu})^{\alpha}\,\partial_{\mu}H\ ,

\displaystyle\delta H

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime}}{2\kappa_{H\bar{H}}\bar{f}^{2}}G^{2}\ ,

which yield

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\lvert f\rvert^{2}-\kappa_{H\bar{H}}\partial_{\mu}H\partial^{\mu}{\bar{H}}+\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}{\bar{G}}+h.c.\right)-\left(m_{1}\,G^{2}+h.c.\right)

(1.4.29)

\displaystyle+2\left[\bar{m}_{1}({\bar{k}}{\bar{H}}-kH){\bar{G}}^{2}+h.c.\right]+\frac{1}{4\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(1-\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}\kappa_{H\bar{H}}}\right){\bar{G}}^{2}\Box G^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left(k^{2}H^{2}+{\bar{k}}^{2}{\bar{H}}^{2}-2\lvert k\rvert^{2}\lvert H\rvert^{2}\right)\left(m_{1}G^{2}+\bar{m}_{1}{\bar{G}}^{2}\right)-m_{2}\,G^{2}{\bar{G}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(\kappa_{H\bar{H}}+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)\left(i\partial^{\mu}G\sigma^{\nu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}H\partial_{\nu}{\bar{H}}+h.c.\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(\kappa_{H\bar{H}}-3\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)\left(i\partial^{\mu}G\sigma^{\nu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}{\bar{H}}\partial_{\nu}H+h.c.\right)

\displaystyle-\left[\lambda_{1}G^{2}\partial_{\mu}H\partial^{\mu}{\bar{H}}+\lambda_{2}{\bar{G}}^{2}(\partial H)^{2}+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{i\left(2f_{H}\bar{f}-\partial_{H}\lvert f\rvert^{2}\right)}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}G\sigma^{\mu}{\bar{G}}\partial_{\mu}H+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(\kappa_{H\bar{H}}+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)\epsilon^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma_{\nu}{\bar{G}}\partial_{\alpha}H\partial_{\beta}{\bar{H}},

where
m_{1}
,
m_{2}
,
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
are again functions of the starting ones
f
and
g
, similar to eq. (
1.4.16
), whose expression is not relevant.

Therefore, also in the case of the constraint (
1.4.24
) the resulting lagrangians (
1.4.27
)-(
1.4.29
) are characterised by peculiar quartic, derivative self-couplings of the goldstino. Their source is, as for the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
)-(
1.4.8
), the nontrivial condition imposed by the constraint (
1.4.24
) on the auxiliary field
F_{H}
in eq. (
1.4.25
).

Causality constraints

Analogously to the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
) and the discussion of Section
1.4.1
, the resulting self-interactions of the goldstino are subject to positivity bounds
[
1
]
. The
2\to 2
scattering amplitudes, computed from the quartic-limit lagrangian (
1.4.29
), that are nontrivially bounded are the elastic ones, involving the real part of
H
, which all yield the bound

\kappa_{H,\bar{H}}\left(0\right)\lvert f\left(0\right)\rvert^{2}\geq\lvert g^{\prime}\left(0\right)\rvert\ .

(1.4.30)

As in eq. (
1.4.19
), this positivity bound holds in the vacuum
H=0
, but it is extended to the whole fields domain by requiring a causal goldstino propagation. The sound speed parameter is in fact computed from the quadratic-limit lagrangian (
1.4.27
) to be

c_{s}^{2}=1-\frac{4\lvert\dot{H}\rvert^{2}}{\left(\lvert f\rvert^{2}+\kappa_{H\bar{H}}\lvert\dot{H}\rvert^{2}\right)^{2}}\left[\kappa_{H\bar{H}}\lvert f\rvert^{2}-\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}\right]\ ,

(1.4.31)

so that one must have

\kappa_{H,\bar{H}}\left(H,\bar{H}\right)\lvert f\left(H\right)\rvert^{2}\geq\lvert g^{\prime}\left(H\right)\rvert\ ,

(1.4.32)

in order to have subluminal propagation. The sound speed (
1.4.31
) matches again its supergravity version in the rigid limit
12
12

12

In this case, the sound speed (
1.4.31
) differs from the supergravity one by
1/M_{\text{P}}^{2}
-suppressed terms. These gravitational corrections are still consistent with causality, which should be either be satisfied or be unresolvable
[
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
,
84
]
.

[
1
]
.

The appearance of such nontrivial causality constraints (
1.4.30
)-(
1.4.32
) suggest that, like the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), also the complex scalar constrain (
1.4.24
) does not have a well-defined 2-derivative UV origin. This is linked to the solution imposed on the auxiliary fields by both constraints (
1.4.6
) and (
1.4.24
), which has no obvious microscopic interpretation in terms of decoupling of physical degrees of freedom in a two-derivative lagrangian.

1.4.3
Consistent superfield constraints

The orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
) and the complex field constraint (
1.4.24
), despite being consistent with (nonlinear) supersymmetry, as well as from the algebraic point of view, give rise to couplings of the goldstino that are subject to nontrivial causality bounds (
1.4.22
)-(
1.4.32
). These bounds prevent an uplifting of the associated effective theories into 2-derivative UV completions. One of the main aspects of
[
1
]
— which we now discuss more in detail — is that the constraints generating these effective constructions have the subtle feature of imposing a set of conditions on the auxiliary fields, as in eq. (
1.4.8
)-(
1.4.25
). These may in fact require higher-derivative operators directly at the microscopic level, as already suggested in
[
61
]
for the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
).

In this section we consider superfield constraints which do not affect the auxiliary fields and show that they are indeed causal throughout the entire field space. The first direct example in this sense is the nilpotent constraint (
1.4.1
) defining the goldstino superfield itself. Indeed, such constraint does not act on the auxiliary field (see eq. (
1.4.2
)) and the associated lagrangian is the Volkov–Akulov one (
1.3.19
)
[
52
,
57
,
62
,
59
,
64
]
, which is perfectly causal and has a clear UV origin.

A second model, more similar to the two constraints (
1.4.6
) and (
1.4.24
) but not acting on the auxiliary field, is given by

S\mathcal{Q}=0\ ,

(1.4.33)

where
\mathcal{Q}
is the chiral superfield

\mathcal{Q}=Q+\sqrt{2}\theta\chi_{Q}+\theta^{2}F_{Q}\ .

(1.4.34)

The constraint (
1.4.33
), which also implies
\mathcal{Q}^{3}=0
like the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), fixes the complex scalar
Q
to be
[
59
]

Q=\frac{\chi_{Q}G}{F_{S}}-\frac{F_{Q}}{2F_{S}^{2}}G^{2}\ .

(1.4.35)

and thus describes a coupling between the goldstino
G
and a second fermion
\chi_{Q}
. The Kähler and superpotential are taken to be
[
1
]

\displaystyle K=S\bar{S}+\mathcal{Q}\bar{\mathcal{Q}}+\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{Q}\bar{\mathcal{Q}}\left(h_{1}\mathcal{Q}+\bar{h}_{1}\bar{\mathcal{Q}}\right)+\frac{h_{2}}{4}\left(\mathcal{Q}\bar{\mathcal{Q}}\right)^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle W=f(\mathcal{Q})S+g(\mathcal{Q})\ ,

(1.4.36)

in which
f
and
g
are holomorphic functions, as in the previous cases, while
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are real constants. In the resulting off-shell lagrangian, the auxiliary field
F_{Q}
, being unconstrained, has to be integrated out together with
F_{S}
, giving

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}+h.c.\right)+\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}\chi_{q}\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\chi}_{q}+h.c.\right)+\frac{1}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left[\frac{g^{\prime}}{f}\partial_{\mu}\left(\chi_{q}G\right)\partial^{\mu}\bar{G}^{2}+h.c.\right]+

(1.4.37)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(\bar{\chi}_{q}\bar{G}\right)\Box\left(\chi_{q}G\right)+\frac{1}{4}\lvert f\rvert^{2}\left(1+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)\bar{G}^{2}\Box G^{2}+\dots\ ,

where we omitted four-fermion contact terms, which are not relevant for the present discussion.

The lagrangian (
1.4.37
) does involve nontrivial quartic, derivative interactions of the goldstino and the fermion
\chi_{Q}
but these operators satisfy automatically the associated positivity bounds
[
1
,
66
]
, namely without the need to impose any extra condition on the general model (
1.4.36
). Decoupling a scalar field has a well-defined physical meaning and can indeed be done from a 2-derivative UV theory. The outcome is a well-defined effective lagrangian (
1.4.37
),
i.e.
that does not suffer from the causality issues that arise when also an auxiliary field gets constrained in this procedure.

Improved, generalized constraints

The strongest evidence supporting the claim on constrained auxiliary fields as sources for the potential inconsistency of the goldstino effective theories is the construction of an improved version of the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), which maintains the minimal field spectrum but without constraining the auxiliary field. This is done in
[
1
]
by means of the generalized superfield constraints derived in
[
61
]
. This generalized constraint has the form

\bar{S}SQ_{L}=0\ ,

(1.4.38)

where
S
is the goldstino nilpotent superfield (
1.4.1
) and
Q_{L}
is a generic superfield which may carry a Lorentz index
L
. This constraint has the implicit solution

Q_{\textup{L}}=-2\frac{\bar{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{S}\bar{D}^{\dot{\alpha}}Q_{\textup{L}}}{\bar{D}^{2}\bar{S}}-\frac{\bar{S}\bar{D}^{2}Q_{\textup{L}}}{\bar{D}^{2}\bar{S}}-2\frac{D^{\alpha}SD_{\alpha}\bar{D}^{2}\left(\bar{S}Q_{\textup{L}}\right)}{D^{2}S\bar{D}^{2}\bar{S}}-S\frac{D^{2}\bar{D}^{2}\left(\bar{S}Q_{\textup{L}}\right)}{D^{2}S\bar{D}^{2}\bar{S}}\ ,

(1.4.39)

which is nontrivial only on the lowest component of
Q_{L}

[
61
]
.

The power of such constraint is that it can be used to remove single components of the superfields it acts on. It follows in particular that standard constraints can be expressed in terms of combinations of such generalized constraint (
1.4.38
). This is the case of the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), which was shown in
[
61
]
to be equivalent to the set of three constraints

\displaystyle\bar{S}S\left(\Phi-\bar{\Phi}\right)

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(1.4.40)

\displaystyle\bar{S}SD_{\alpha}\Phi

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(1.4.41)

\displaystyle\bar{S}SD^{2}\Phi

\displaystyle=0\ ,

(1.4.42)

each one removing, respectively, the imaginary scalar, the fermion, and the auxiliary component of
\Phi
, as in eq. (
1.4.8
). Note in particular that the condition (
1.4.42
) removing the auxiliary field is a higher-derivative constraint.

Thus, following the previous discussion about ill-defined goldstino couplings and constraints on the auxiliary fields, the decomposition above suggest considering the effective theory generated only by the first two constraints (
1.4.40
)-(
1.4.41
)

\displaystyle\bar{S}S\left(\Phi-\bar{\Phi}\right)=0\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{S}SD_{\alpha}\Phi=0\ ,

(1.4.43)

without the third one (
1.4.42
): the resulting model will be an improved version of the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), with the same minimal field spectrum but without touching the auxiliary field
[
1
]
. The two constraints of eq. (
1.4.43
) bound the imaginary scalar
B
and the fermion
\chi_{\phi}
of the chiral superfield
\Phi
(
1.4.7
) to be

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{4}\left[\left(\frac{\bar{G}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\right)^{2}\bar{F}_{\phi}-\left(\frac{G}{F_{S}}\right)^{2}F_{\phi}\right]-\frac{G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}}{2|F_{S}|^{2}}\partial_{\mu}A+\dots\ ,

(1.4.44)

\displaystyle\psi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{F_{\phi}}{F_{S}}G_{\alpha}-i\frac{\left(\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\right)_{\alpha}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\partial_{\mu}A+\frac{i}{4}\frac{\left(\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\right)_{\alpha}}{\bar{F}_{S}}\partial_{\mu}\left[\left(\frac{G}{F_{S}}\right)^{2}F_{\phi}\right]+\dots\ ,

(1.4.45)

in which we neglect terms higher-order in the number of fields with respect to the quartic one under consideration. The most general Kähler and superpotential that can be defined in this improved setup are

\displaystyle K=\bar{S}S+\kappa\left(\Phi,\bar{\Phi}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle W\left(\Phi\right)=f\left(\Phi\right)S+g\left(\Phi\right)\ ,

(1.4.46)

where the function
\kappa
is real and
f
and
g
are holomorphic. The associated on-shell lagrangian in the quadratic limit, obtained integrating out both
F_{S}
and
F_{\phi}
in the standard way, is equal to

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\left[\lvert f\rvert^{2}+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}\right]-\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}\partial_{\mu}A\partial^{\mu}A+Z\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}+h.c.\right)+\left(mG^{2}+h.c.\right)

(1.4.47)

\displaystyle+c\,G^{2}\bar{G}^{2}-Z\left(i\frac{f^{\prime}}{2f}+h.c.\right)G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}A+\left(d\,G^{2}+h.c.\right)\left(\partial A\right)^{2}

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime}}{\bar{f}^{2}}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{\sigma}^{\nu}G\partial_{\nu}A+\frac{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}}i\partial^{\mu}G\sigma^{\nu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}A\partial_{\nu}A-\frac{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}{2\lvert f\rvert^{2}}i\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\left(\partial A\right)^{2}+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(1+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{2\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)\bar{G}^{2}\Box G^{2}-\frac{1}{16\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}\left(\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime 2}}{\bar{f}^{4}}G^{2}\Box G^{2}+h.c.\right)\ ,

where

Z\equiv 1+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\ ,

(1.4.48)

while the coefficients
m
,
c
and
d
are functions of the original
\kappa
,
f
and
g
of which we do not need the explicit form. The main differences with the lagrangian (
1.4.12
) produced by the orthogonal constraint are the scalar potential, which now includes also the term coming from
F_{\phi}
, and the non-minimal goldstino kinetic term, parameterized in this case by the function
Z
(
1.4.48
). Instead, the quartic limit lagrangian is obtained through the field redefinitions

\displaystyle\delta G^{\alpha}=\frac{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}{2Z\lvert f\rvert^{2}}G^{\alpha}\left(\partial A\right)^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta G^{\alpha}=-\frac{i}{Z}\frac{g^{\prime}}{f^{2}}\left(\bar{G}\bar{\sigma}^{\mu}\right)^{\alpha}\partial_{\mu}A\ ,

(1.4.49)

which yield

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\left[\lvert f\rvert^{2}+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}\right]-\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}\partial_{\mu}A\partial^{\mu}A+Z\left(\frac{i}{2}\partial_{\mu}G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}+h.c.\right)

(1.4.50)

\displaystyle+\left(mG^{2}+h.c.\right)+\lambda G\sigma^{\mu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}A+c\,G^{2}\bar{G}^{2}+\left(\tilde{d}\,G^{2}+h.c.\right)\partial_{\mu}A\partial^{\mu}A

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}{\lvert f\rvert^{2}+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}}\right)\left(i\partial^{\mu}G\sigma^{\nu}\bar{G}\partial_{\mu}A\partial_{\nu}A+h.c.\right)+\frac{1}{4\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\left(1+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{2\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}\lvert f\rvert^{2}}\right)\bar{G}^{2}\Box G^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{16\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}\left(\frac{\bar{g}^{\prime 2}}{\bar{f}^{4}}G^{2}\Box G^{2}+h.c.\right)\ ,

where, again, we do not need to specify the explicit expression of the functions
\lambda
and
\tilde{d}
.

Proceeding in the same way as for the orthogonal constraint in Section
1.4.1
, we now analyze the goldstino propagation and the causality of the effective theory defined by the constraints (
1.4.43
). It is easy to see that the potentially dangerous operators appearing in the quartic-limit lagrangian (
1.4.50
) automatically meet the associated positivity requirements. They constrain in fact the same scattering amplitudes as in eq. (
1.4.17
), but the improved setup is such that these conditions are now identically satisfied by the new couplings. Furthermore, we can also compute the goldstino sound speed parameter from the quadratic-limit lagrangian (
1.4.47
), evaluated on a time-dependent scalar field background
A(t)
. The new superfield constraints setup thus yield

c_{s}^{2}=1-\frac{4{\dot{A}}^{2}\lvert f\rvert^{2}\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}{\left(\lvert f\rvert^{2}+\frac{\lvert g^{\prime}\rvert^{2}}{\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}}+\kappa_{\phi\bar{\phi}}{\dot{A}}^{2}\right)^{2}}\ ,

(1.4.51)

which is subluminal at all times
[
1
]
.

Therefore, improving the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
) by imposing only the conditions (
1.4.43
), which have a standard physical interpretation in terms of decoupling of degrees of freedom, and without touching the auxiliary fields, yields a completely causal effective field theory, with the same minimal field content of the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
) but resolving all its inconsistencies. The algebraic side of this argument is that decoupling an auxiliary field requires higher-derivative operators already in the UV, as the one in eq. (
1.4.42
) for the orthogonal constraint
[
61
]
, and thus do not define a priori a consistent effective field theory. The main result of
[
1
]
is then that consistent effective field theories of nonlinear supersymmetry must be constructed through superfield constraints that only remove physical, and not auxiliary, degrees of freedom.

From this perspective, the improved models constructed in
[
1
]
through the generalized superfield constraint (
1.4.38
) are indeed well-defined effective field theories and admit therefore a UV completion into a microscopic 2-derivative theory. In
[
1
]
we take a step in this direction by studying the coupling of the improved orthogonal constraint (
1.4.43
) to (nonlinear) supergravity, where the goldstino
G
is now identified with the longitudinal component of the massive gravitino
[
53
]
. The gravitino sound speed in such supergravity model is found to match exactly the rigid one (
1.4.51
), so that subluminal propagation persist also at this level, and therefore the improved constraint (
1.4.43
) defines improved minimal models of inflation in supergravity with nonlinearly realized supersymmetry.

Chapter 2
Linear and nonlinear supergravity

In the first chapter of this thesis, we introduced the general framework of supersymmetry, arising as the maximal possible extension of the global Poincaré group. In this second chapter, we take a further step forward and promote global supersymmetry to a local symmetry. The striking outcome of this standard, but rather deep and powerful procedure is the emergence of Einstein gravity as a necessary and essential element of the spectrum of the resulting theory, which is thus a theory of
supergravity
. The beauty of this framework is that gravity and the other forces and matter fields get unified
1
1

1

Such a unification holds, at the field theory level, only classically, since gravity is not consistent as quantum field theory. The unification of matter and forces at the quantum level is realized by string theory, which we will discuss in Chapter
3
.
in a single, elegant picture and by a unique symmetry principle.

As in the previous chapter, the main focus will be on
D=4
,
\mathcal{N}=1
supergravity and its couplings to matter fields. We begin in Section
2.1
introducing pure supergravity as the gauge theory of the local Super-Poincaré group. We then proceed to discuss its coupling to chiral multiplets, the spontaneous breaking of local supersymmetry, and the corresponding super-Higgs mechanism. In this framework, we present in Section
2.2
the results of the work
[
2
]
, concerning effective theories of nonlinear local supersymmetry, in continuation with Chapter
1
, and the dynamics of massive gravitinos in cosmological scenarios, in particular the phenomenon of
gravitational particle production
. Then, we restore supersymmetry and discuss in Section
2.3
the possible off-shell formulations of
D=4
,
\mathcal{N}=1
pure supergravity in relation to the supercurrent superfield formalism. This sets the stage for presenting the findings of the work
[
4
]
, in which we use this formalism to construct and investigate possible couplings to supergravity of the supermultiplet of the
massive spin-2 field
. In this final section, along with Appendix
C
, we briefly cover some aspects of pure supergravity in five dimensions.

The main review references for this chapter are the books
[
39
]
and
[
40
]
.

2.1
Supergravity as the gauge theory of Super-Poincaré

A theory of supergravity is a theory where supersymmetry has been promoted from global to local symmetry. This means that the supersymmetry parameter
\epsilon
acquires a spacetime dependence:

\epsilon\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\epsilon(x)\ .

(2.1.1)

The outcomes of this promotion can be understood qualitatively starting from a rigid supersymmetric theory and studying the deformations implied by (
2.1.1
). This is the so-called Noether procedure, which we can apply following the standard algorithm of gauge interactions, to which local supersymmetry makes no exception. Thus, the gauging (
2.1.1
) requires the introduction of a gauge field for local supersymmetry, which we call
\psi_{\mu}
. Being a gauge field, it must transform with the derivative of the symmetry parameter:

\delta\psi_{\mu}\sim\partial_{\mu}\epsilon\ ,

(2.1.2)

and couple in the lagrangian to the supersymmetry conserved current, which we call
\Xi^{\mu}
. This current is a Majorana vector-spinor
2
2

2

While in Chapter
1
we used 2-components Weyl spinor, here we employ instead 4-components spinors, in this case of the Majorana type. See Appendix
A
for more details and for the conventions on the Clifford algebra.
of mass dimension
\left[\Xi^{\mu}\right]=\frac{7}{2}
, and so the gauge field
\psi_{\mu}
must also be a Majorana vector-spinor. Its minimal coupling to
\Xi^{\mu}
has the form

\frac{1}{M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\Xi^{\mu}\ ,

(2.1.3)

where
M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}
must be the Planck mass, as will be clear in a moment. The gauge field
\psi_{\mu}
is known as the
gravitino
, which is a Majorana vector-spinor field, of spin-
\frac{3}{2}
. The coupling of the gravitino
\psi_{\mu}
to the supercurrent
\Xi^{\mu}
of eq. (
2.1.3
) is the first deformation of the theory and it requires further ones in order to have full local supersymmetry invariance. Following this Noether procedure, the supersymmetry transformation of the supercurrent
\Xi^{\mu}
contains the stress-energy tensor
T^{\mu\nu}

[
85
]
:

\delta\Xi^{\mu}\sim T^{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\nu}\epsilon\ .

(2.1.4)

In order to embed consistently the stress-energy tensor in the lagrangian, there must exist its own associated gauge field, which is nothing but the metric field
g_{\mu\nu}
:

g_{\mu\nu}T^{\mu\nu}\ .

(2.1.5)

Therefore, we see explicitly from this Noether procedure argument that promoting supersymmetry to a local symmetry requires, by consistency, the existence of the gravitational interaction. The graviton field
g_{\mu\nu}
and the spin-
\frac{3}{2}
gravitino form what is known as the pure supergravity multiplet
\left\{g_{\mu\nu},\psi_{\mu}\right\}
. At the linear level, the supersymmetry transformations characterising this multiplet have the schematic form

\displaystyle\delta\psi_{\mu}\sim M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}\partial_{\mu}\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\delta g_{\mu\nu}\sim\frac{1}{M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{(\mu}\psi_{\nu)}\ .

(2.1.6)

Going beyond the linear order through the Noether procedure highlighted above is rather involved and not much transparent. On the contrary, the complete supersymmetry transformations of the pure supergravity multiplet — which also determine the associated lagrangian — can be elegantly derived in full generality by performing the gauging of the full Super-Poincaré algebra of eq. (
1.1.7
). In this way, the connection between local supersymmetry and gravitational interactions will be manifest. We denote collectively the generators of the (
\mathcal{N}=1
) Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
), namely the translations
P_{a}
, the Lorentz rotations
M_{ab}
and the supersymmetry transformations
Q_{\alpha}
as
3
3

3

From now on we set the Planck mass
M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}
to 1 and reinstate it explicitly when needed.

T_{A}=\left\{P_{a},M_{ab},Q_{\alpha}\right\}\ ,

(2.1.7)

in which the small latin letters denote four-dimensional local Lorentz indices, whereas
\alpha
is a 4-component spinor index. The gauging of this algebra can be realized introducing a set of gauge fields
A_{\mu}^{A}
for each of these generators, which must have the form

A_{\mu}^{A}T_{A}=e_{\mu}{}^{a}P_{a}+\frac{1}{2}\omega_{\mu}^{ab}M_{ab}+\bar{\psi}_{\mu}Q\ .

(2.1.8)

It is clear from this formula that the gauge fields
e_{\mu}{}^{a}
and
\omega_{\mu}^{ab}
are the vierbein and the associated spin-connection, while
\psi_{\mu}
is the gravitino field introduced above. These gauge fields should transform under local supersymmetry as

\delta A_{\mu}^{A}=\partial_{\mu}\epsilon^{A}+f^{A}{}_{BC}A_{\mu}^{B}\epsilon^{C}\ ,

(2.1.9)

where
f^{A}{}_{BC}
are the structure constants of the local Super-Poincaré algebra, which can be read from eq. (
1.1.7
). The resulting supersymmetry transformations of the vierbein and the gravitino are

\displaystyle\delta e_{\mu}{}^{a}=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma^{a}\psi_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\psi_{\mu}=D_{\mu}\epsilon\ ,

(2.1.10)

where
D_{\mu}
is the Lorentz-covariant derivative

D_{\mu}\epsilon=\partial_{\mu}\epsilon+\frac{1}{4}\omega_{\mu}^{ab}\gamma_{ab}\epsilon\ .

(2.1.11)

Moreover, the structure constants
f^{A}{}_{BC}
imply that the vierbein
e_{\mu}{}^{a}
and the spin-connection
\omega_{\mu}^{ab}
satisfy a nontrivial torsion equation

T^{a}_{\mu\nu}=2\left(\partial_{[\mu}e_{\nu]}{}^{a}+\omega_{[\mu}^{a}{}_{b}e_{\nu]}{}^{b}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\psi}_{[\mu}\gamma^{a}\psi_{\nu]}\ ,

(2.1.12)

which involves a gravitino bilinear contribution. The torsionless condition yields therefore not the standard connection
\omega_{\mu}^{ab}(e)
, but rather a connection with torsion:

\hat{\omega}_{\mu}^{ab}\equiv\omega_{\mu}^{ab}\left(e,\psi\right)=\omega_{\mu}^{ab}(e)-\frac{1}{4}\left(\bar{\psi}^{[a}\gamma_{\mu}\psi^{b]}-\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{[a}\psi^{b]}-\bar{\psi}^{[a}\gamma^{b]}\psi_{\mu}\right)\ .

(2.1.13)

This torsion term is a peculiar feature of supergravity theories and summarizes in an elegant way their non-linearities, which is required by general consistency with local supersymmetry.

The pure supergravity lagrangian is therefore given by
[
31
,
32
]

e^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\textup{SUGRA}}=\frac{1}{2}R(\hat{\omega})-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}D_{\nu}\left(\hat{\omega}\right)\psi_{\rho}\ ,

(2.1.14)

and it is invariant under the supersymmetry transformations

\displaystyle\delta e_{\mu}{}^{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma^{a}\psi_{\mu}\ ,

(2.1.15)

\displaystyle\delta\psi_{\mu}

\displaystyle=D_{\mu}\left(\hat{\omega}\right)\epsilon\ .

Unpacking the torsion terms of the connection
\hat{\omega}_{\mu}^{ab}
(
2.1.13
), which are left implicit in eq. (
2.1.14
)-(
2.1.15
), amounts to a four-gravitino contact term in the action, which results in

e^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\textup{SUGRA}}=\frac{1}{2}R-\frac{1}{2}\psi_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}D_{\nu}\psi_{\rho}+\mathcal{L}_{\textup{torsion}}\ ,

(2.1.16)

where
R\equiv R(e)
and
D_{\mu}\equiv D_{\mu}(\omega)
are the standard torsion-free quantities, and

e^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\textup{torsion}}=-\frac{1}{32}\left[\left(\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\psi^{\rho}\right)\left(\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\gamma_{\nu}\psi_{\rho}+\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\gamma_{\rho}\psi_{\nu}\right)-4\left(\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\rho}\psi_{\rho}\right)\left(\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\gamma^{\rho}\psi_{\rho}\right)\right]\ .

(2.1.17)

The closure of the algebra of local supersymmetry is now modified with respect to the global case (
1.1.7
)-(
1.2.6
), and it is realized on a covariant general coordinate transformation:

\left[\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\right]=\delta_{\xi}-\delta_{\hat{\Lambda}}-\delta_{\hat{\epsilon}}\ ,

(2.1.18)

where
\delta_{\xi}
is a general coordinate transformation,
\delta_{\hat{\Lambda}}
is a local Lorentz transformation and
\delta_{\hat{\epsilon}}
is a local supersymmetry transformation, all given by field-dependent parameters:

\displaystyle\xi^{a}=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\epsilon}_{1}\gamma^{a}\epsilon_{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\hat{\Lambda}^{ab}=\xi^{\rho}\omega_{\rho}^{ab}\ ,

\displaystyle\hat{\epsilon}=\xi^{\rho}\psi_{\rho}\ .

(2.1.19)

The closure (
2.1.18
) is exact on the vierbein
e_{\mu}{}^{a}
and it holds on-shell (as expected) on the gravitino
\psi_{\mu}
.

2.1.1
The coupling to chiral multiplets

After having introduced the pure supergravity multiplet (
2.1.15
),
we now discuss its coupling to
n
chiral multiplets
\{\phi^{i},\chi^{i}\}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, of the type we introduced in eq. (
1.1.21
)-(
1.2.11
) of Chapter
1
. As we saw previously, chiral multiplets are inherently associated with Kähler geometry: the scalar fields
\phi^{i}
parameterize in fact a Kähler manifold, whose characteristic isometries are the Kähler transformations

K\left(\phi,\bar{\phi}\right)\quad\longrightarrow\quad K\left(\phi,\bar{\phi}\right)+\Lambda(\phi)+\bar{\Lambda}\left(\bar{\phi}\right)\ ,

(2.1.20)

as in eq. (
1.2.19
). When coupled to supergravity, the requirement of Kähler invariance gives rise to new structures and symmetry relations among the elements of the theory, which turn into new interaction terms in the resulting action. These additional couplings will appear suppressed by appropriate powers of the Planck mass
M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}
, so that the global supersymmetry action (
1.2.28
) is indeed recovered in the decoupling limit of gravity.

The first new element of this construction is that the gravitino
\psi_{\mu}
transforms nontrivially under Kähler transformations (
2.1.20
), in the form of a chiral rotation:

\psi_{\mu}\,\,\to\,\,e^{-\frac{i}{2}\mathfrak{Im}\left(\Lambda\right)\gamma_{5}}\psi_{\mu}\ .

(2.1.21)

The invariance of the action under Kähler reparameterization implies that the derivatives acting on the gravitino should include an appropriate covariantization with respect to this Kähler transformations (
2.1.20
). This Kähler-covariant derivative takes the form

\mathscr{D}_{\mu}\psi_{\nu}=D_{\mu}\psi_{\nu}+\frac{i}{2}Q_{\mu}\gamma_{5}\psi_{\nu}\ ,

(2.1.22)

where
Q_{\mu}
is the composite operator

Q_{\mu}=\frac{i}{2}\left[K_{\bar{i}}\partial_{\mu}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{i}}-K_{i}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{i}\right]\ .

(2.1.23)

Note that
Q_{\mu}
transforms as
Q_{\mu}\,\,\to\,\,Q_{\mu}+\partial_{\mu}\Lambda
under Kähler transformations (
2.1.20
), like a
\mathrm{U}(1)
gauge field, and thus the gravitino derivative
\mathscr{D}_{\mu}\psi_{\nu}
(
2.1.22
) transforms indeed covariantly under (
2.1.20
),
i.e.
like
\psi_{\mu}
in eq. (
2.1.21
). Moreover, consistency of the supersymmetry transformation (
2.1.15
) requires first the supersymmetry parameter
\epsilon
to transform in the same way as the gravitino (
2.1.21
), and consequently also the chiral fermions
\chi^{i}
:

\displaystyle\chi_{\mu L}^{i}\,\,\to\,\,e^{\frac{i}{2}\mathfrak{Im}\left(\Lambda\right)\gamma_{5}}\chi^{i}_{\mu L}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}_{\mu}\chi^{i}_{\nu L}=D_{\mu}\chi^{i}_{\nu}+\Gamma^{i}_{jk}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{j}\chi^{k}_{L}-\frac{i}{2}Q_{\mu}\gamma_{5}\chi^{i}_{L}\ ,

(2.1.24)

for which the Kähler-covariant derivative includes also the Kähler connection term, as we defined in eq. (
1.2.23
)-(
1.2.24
).

Thus, when coupling chiral multiplets to supergravity, consistency with local supersymmetry and Kähler geometry implies that the fermionic fields of the theory must also transform under Kähler transformations, in the form of a chiral
\mathrm{U}(1)
-like symmetry (
2.1.21
)-(
2.1.24
), with respect to which the corresponding lagrangians must be properly covariantized. As a consequence, also the coupling of the superpotential
W\left(\phi\right)
is modified. First, it must come through the combination

e^{\frac{K}{2}}W\ ,

(2.1.25)

and should transform as

W\left(\phi\right)\,\,\to\,\,e^{-\Lambda\left(\phi\right)}W\left(\phi\right)\ ,

(2.1.26)

under Kähler transformations (
2.1.20
). The associated Kähler-covariantization of the derivatives is given by

e^{\frac{K}{2}}\mathscr{D}_{i}W=e^{\frac{K}{2}}\left(\partial_{i}+K_{i}\right)W\ .

(2.1.27)

This new structure linking the superpotential
W
and the Kähler potential implies that the two objects are no longer independent, as in global supersymmetry, and terms can be moved between the two without changing the final result, thanks to the Kähler transformations of eq. (
2.1.20
), (
2.1.25
) and (
2.1.26
). This motivates the definition of a generalized potential
\mathscr{G}
, as

\mathscr{G}\equiv K+\log\lvert W\rvert^{2}\ ,

(2.1.28)

which is manifestly invariant under Kähler transformations (
2.1.20
)-(
2.1.26
).

Collecting all these ingredients, we obtain the following Lagrangian describing the coupling of
n
chiral multiplets to pure
D=4
,
\mathcal{N}=1
supergravity
[
86
,
87
,
88
]
:

\displaystyle e^{-1}\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}R-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}\mathscr{D}_{\mu}\psi_{\nu}-K_{i\bar{j}}\left(\partial_{\mu}\phi^{i}\partial^{\mu}\bar{\phi}^{\bar{j}}+\bar{\chi}_{L}^{i}\not{\mathscr{D}}\chi_{R}^{\bar{j}}+\bar{\chi}_{R}^{\bar{j}}\not{\mathscr{D}}\chi_{L}^{i}\right)

(2.1.29)

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{1}{2}e^{\frac{K}{2}}W\bar{\psi}_{\mu R}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\psi_{\nu R}-e^{\frac{K}{2}}\mathscr{D}_{i}\mathscr{D}_{j}W\bar{\chi}^{i}_{L}\chi^{j}_{L}+h.c.\right]

\displaystyle+\left[K_{i\bar{j}}\bar{\psi}_{\mu L}\gamma^{\nu}\gamma^{\mu}\chi^{i}_{L}\partial_{\nu}{\bar{\phi}}^{\bar{j}}+\bar{\psi}_{\mu R}\gamma^{\mu}\chi^{i}_{L}e^{\frac{K}{2}}\mathscr{D}_{i}W+h.c.\right]-V\left(\phi,\bar{\phi}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{L}_{\textup{4F}}\ .

The first line contains the kinetic terms of all fields, while the second line contains mass-like terms for both the gravitino and the chiral fermions. The third line contains instead the coupling of the gravitino to the supersymmetry current, as in eq. (
2.1.3
), and the scalar potential, which is now given by

V\left(\phi,\bar{\phi}\right)=e^{K}\left(K^{i\bar{j}}\mathscr{D}_{i}W\bar{\mathscr{D}}_{\bar{j}}\overline{W}-3\lvert W\rvert^{2}\right)\ ,

(2.1.30)

where the first term appeared already in global supersymmetry, whereas the second is a consequence of the coupling to pure supergravity. In terms of the generalized potential (
2.1.28
), this scalar potential can be expressed as

V=e^{\mathscr{G}}\left(\mathscr{G}^{i\bar{j}}\mathscr{G}_{i}\mathscr{G}_{\bar{j}}-3\right)\ .

(2.1.31)

The last line of the lagrangian (
2.1.29
) contains instead quartic fermionic terms and reads

\mathcal{L}_{\text{4F}}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{torsion}}-\frac{1}{2}K_{i\bar{j}}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\chi^{i}\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\chi^{\bar{j}}+\frac{1}{4}\left(R_{i\bar{j}k\bar{l}}-K_{i\bar{j}}K_{k\bar{l}}\right)\bar{\chi}^{i}\chi^{k}\bar{\chi}^{\bar{j}}\chi^{\bar{l}}\ .

(2.1.32)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{torsion}}
is the quartic gravitino term of pure supergravity (
2.1.13
) and
R_{i\bar{j}k\bar{l}}
was given in eq. (
1.2.23
).

At quadratic order in the number of fermions, the local supersymmetry transformations that leave invariant the lagrangian (
2.1.29
) are

\displaystyle\delta e_{\mu}{}^{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma^{a}\psi_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\psi_{\mu L}

\displaystyle=\mathscr{D}_{\mu}\epsilon_{L}+\frac{1}{2}e^{\frac{K}{2}}W\gamma_{\mu}\epsilon_{R}\ ,

(2.1.33)

\displaystyle\delta\phi^{i}

\displaystyle=\bar{\epsilon}_{L}\chi^{i}_{L}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\chi^{i}_{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\not{\partial}\phi^{i}\epsilon_{R}-\frac{1}{2}e^{\frac{K}{2}}K^{i\bar{j}}\bar{\mathscr{D}}_{\bar{j}}\overline{W}\epsilon_{L}\ .

Comments on the coupling of vector multiplets

We end this section with a few additional comments on the coupling to supergravity of vector multiplets (
1.1.22
)-(
1.2.35
). Similarly to the inclusion of chiral multiplets, the global supersymmetry setup needs to be modified with appropriate covariantization of the terms and the addition of the gravitino coupling to the supercurrent (
2.1.3
), as well as further quartic fermionic interactions. Moreover, further interactions and covariantizations need to be included if some of the isometries of the scalar manifold are gauged through the newly added vector fields.

We do not display the full lagrangian here. We though highlight that, as in global supersymmetry, adding gauge interactions between the matter fields also give rise in the local case to a new term in the scalar potential
V\left(\phi,\bar{\phi}\right)
, which takes the general form

V\left(\phi,\bar{\phi}\right)=e^{K}\left(K^{i\bar{j}}\mathscr{D}_{i}W\bar{\mathscr{D}}_{\bar{j}}\overline{W}-3\lvert W\rvert^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathfrak{Re}f_{IJ}\right)D^{I}D^{J}\ ,

(2.1.34)

where
f_{IJ}
is the gauge-kinetic function
[
86
,
87
,
88
]
and
D^{I}
are the D-terms that already appeared in global supersymmetry in eq. (
1.2.55
)-(
1.2.56
). For more details, also on the inclusion of Fayet–Iliopoulos terms (
1.2.44
), see
[
86
,
87
,
88
,
89
,
90
,
91
]
.

2.1.2
Local supersymmetry breaking

The scalar potential (
2.1.34
) is the starting point to discuss the spontaneous breaking of local supersymmetry and the consequent supersymmetric Higgs mechanism, in which the goldstino of supersymmetry breaking, which we introduced in Section
1.3
, is eaten by the gravitino, which in this way acquires a mass
[
44
]
.

With respect to the global case, supersymmetric and non-supersymmetric vacua can no longer be identified by the corresponding value of the energy, as depicted in Figure
1.1
. In local supersymmetry, the semi-positivity of the energy (
1.1.11
) does not hold anymore. Indeed, the coupling to pure supergravity generates a negative contribution to the scalar potential, the term
-3e^{K}\lvert W\rvert^{2}
in eq. (
2.1.34
). However, the vacua of a supergravity theory can still be classified based on the homogeneous structure of the vacuum expectation values of the fermionic supersymmetry transformations. They should in fact vanish identically, since the vacuum must be a Lorentz-invariant state, and this translates into consistency conditions on their explicit expressions, which are directly related to the terms in the scalar potential. This structure was also present in the rigid case, as shown in eq. (
1.3.12
). To illustrate this, let us consider pure supergravity
\left\{e_{\mu}{}^{a},\psi_{\mu}\right\}
coupled to
n_{\textup{C}}
chiral multiplets
\left\{\phi^{i},\chi^{i}\right\}
and
n_{\textup{V}}
vector multiplets
\left\{A_{\mu}^{I},\lambda^{I}\right\}
. The vacuum expectation value of the fermionic supersymmetry transformations are

\displaystyle\langle\delta\psi_{\mu L}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathscr{D}_{\mu}\epsilon_{L}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle e^{\frac{K}{2}}W\right\rangle\gamma_{\mu}\epsilon_{R}=0\ ,

(2.1.35)

\displaystyle\langle\delta\chi^{i}_{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\left\langle K^{i\bar{j}}e^{\frac{K}{2}}\bar{\mathscr{D}}_{\bar{j}}\overline{W}\right\rangle\epsilon_{L}=0\ ,

(2.1.36)

\displaystyle\langle\delta\lambda^{I}_{L}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\left\langle D^{I}\right\rangle\epsilon_{L}=0\ .

(2.1.37)

Thus, a vacuum is supersymmetric if these conditions are satisfied identically, while it breaks supersymmetry if they are not. From the matter fermions equations (
2.1.36
)-(
2.1.37
) we have that a supersymmetric vacuum is determined by the conditions

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathscr{D}_{i}W\right\rangle=0\ ,

\displaystyle\left\langle D^{I}\right\rangle=0\ .

(2.1.38)

Note, however, that there is no a priori condition on the vacuum expectation value of the superpotential
\left\langle W\right\rangle
. The gravitino equation (
2.1.35
) can in fact still be satisfied by a nontrivial
\left\langle\mathscr{D}_{\mu}\epsilon_{L}\right\rangle
. This is a purely gravitational effect and and can occur only in anti-de Sitter (AdS) solutions,
i.e.
with negative vacuum energy. On the contrary, the condition
\left\langle W\right\rangle=0
identifies Minkowski vacua, consistently with the global supersymmetry case. The energy of a local supersymmetric vacuum
V_{0}
is in fact given by eq. (
2.1.34
)-(
2.1.38
) to be

V_{0}=-3\left\langle e^{K}\lvert W\rvert^{2}\right\rangle\ .

(2.1.39)

We see therefore that while positive vacuum energy (namely, de Sitter) solutions breaks supersymmetry, the energy of supersymmetric vacua is semi-negative definite. Also note that while the energy scale of supersymmetry breaking
\Lambda_{\not{\textup{susy}}}
is identified by eq. (
2.1.38
) to be the sum of the F and D-terms in the scalar potential:

\Lambda_{\not{\textup{susy}}}^{4}=\left\langle e^{K}K^{i\bar{j}}\mathscr{D}_{i}W\bar{\mathscr{D}}_{\bar{j}}\overline{W}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\left(\mathfrak{Re}f_{IJ}\right)D^{I}D^{J}\right\rangle\ ,

(2.1.40)

the associated vacuum energy
V_{0}
is lower, because of the gravitational term (
2.1.39
):

V_{0}=\Lambda_{\not{\textup{susy}}}^{4}-3\left\langle e^{K}\lvert W\rvert^{2}\right\rangle\ .

(2.1.41)

Let us now consider a vacuum that spontaneously breaks supersymmetry. For simplicity of presentation and relevance for the present manuscript, we focus on the case of a Minkowski vacuum,
i.e.

V_{0}=0
, with non-vanishing F-terms only. The general case with also D-terms and a nontrivial cosmological constant
V_{0}\neq 0
follows anyway the same pattern
[
92
]
. As we discussed in Section
1.3
, the Goldstone theorem predicts the existence in this vacuum of a massless state, the goldstino (
1.3.13
), which is given by the following combination of chiral fermions:

G_{L}=\left\langle\frac{\mathscr{D}_{i}W}{W}\right\rangle\chi^{i}_{L}\ .

(2.1.42)

Similarly to the rigid case, its supersymmetry transformations is of the type

\delta G_{L}=-\sqrt{3}\left\langle e^{\frac{K}{2}}\overline{W}\right\rangle\epsilon_{L}\ ,

(2.1.43)

indicating the residual nonlinear realization (
1.3.16
). However, in the local case supersymmetry has an associated gauge field, the gravitino
\psi_{\mu}
, which reabsorbs the goldstino (
2.1.42
) through the standard Higgs mechanism, becoming a massive field
[
44
]
. This can be seen explicitly from the lagrangian (
2.1.29
). Taking into account the Minkowski background, the relevant sector is the gravitino-goldstino lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}\partial_{\nu}\psi_{\rho}-\left(\bar{G}_{L}\not{\partial}G_{R}+\bar{G}_{R}\not{\partial}G_{L}\right)

(2.1.44)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\left[\left\langle e^{\frac{K}{2}}W\right\rangle\left(\bar{G}_{L}G_{L}+2\sqrt{3}\bar{\psi}_{\mu R}\gamma^{\mu}G_{L}+\bar{\psi}_{\mu R}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\psi_{\nu R}\right)+h.c.\right]\ .

This lagrangian suggests that the goldstino dependence can be completely canceled by performing the redefinition

\psi_{\mu L}\,\,\to\,\,\psi_{\mu L}+\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\left\langle e^{\frac{K}{2}}\overline{W}\right\rangle^{-1}\partial_{\mu}G_{L}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\gamma_{\mu}G_{R}\ ,

(2.1.45)

which yields the lagrangian of a massive gravitino

\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}\partial_{\nu}\psi_{\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\left(m_{3/2}\bar{\psi}_{\mu R}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\psi_{\nu R}+h.c.\right)\ .

(2.1.46)

Comparing the gravitino redefinition (
2.1.45
) with its supersymmetry transformation in eq. (
2.1.33
), which evaluated on the Minkowski vacuum under consideration becomes

\delta\psi_{\mu L}=\partial_{\mu}\epsilon_{L}+\left\langle e^{\frac{K}{2}}\lvert W\rvert\right\rangle\gamma_{\mu}\epsilon_{R}\ ,

(2.1.47)

one realizes that such a redefinition (
2.1.45
) is a special instance of supersymmetry transformation, of composite parameter

\epsilon=\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}\left\langle e^{\frac{K}{2}}\lvert W\rvert\right\rangle^{-1}G\ .

(2.1.48)

Therefore, the difference between the gravitino-goldstino lagrangian (
2.1.44
) and the massive gravitino one (
2.1.46
) is nothing but a gauge transformation. The goldstino
G
is then a pure gauge degree of freedom in (spontaneously broken) supergravity, and can be completely reabsorbed in the gravitino through (
2.1.45
). This identifies the unitary gauge, which can be effectively selected by setting
G=0
.

This is the supersymmetric version of the Higgs mechanism, stating that a spontaneously supersymmetry breaking vacuum yields a massive gravitino, of mass

\lvert m_{3/2}\rvert=\frac{1}{M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}^{2}}\left\langle e^{\frac{K}{2}}\lvert W\rvert\right\rangle\sim\frac{\Lambda_{\not{\textup{susy}}}^{2}}{M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}}\ ,

(2.1.49)

in which we reinstated the Planck mass
M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}
for clarity. The massive gravitino contains four polarization states: two transverse
\pm\frac{3}{2}
polarizations, corresponding to the original massless gravitino, and two longitudinal
\pm\frac{1}{2}
polarizations, corresponding to the goldstino degrees of freedom. In the lagrangian they both couple to the current of supersymmetry
\Xi^{\mu}
as in eq. (
2.1.3
), but the resulting dynamics is substantially different for the two states. While the transverse polarizations interact with gravitational strength, the longitudinal states interact with the scale of supersymmetry breaking (
2.1.40
). This is clear from the gauge transformation (
2.1.45
). Therefore, the couplings of the longitudinal gravitino are enhanced with respect to the transverse one, which is instead gravitationally suppressed.

This property is expressed by the gravitino equivalence theorem
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
, which states that at energies well above of the gravitino mass
m_{3/2}
the scattering amplitudes of the longitudinal gravitino polarizations can be computed in terms of the goldstino
G
through the replacement rule

\psi_{\mu}\,\,\to\,\,\frac{1}{m_{3/2}}\partial_{\mu}G\ .

(2.1.50)

The corresponding interaction terms are then given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}}\overline{\Xi}^{\mu}\psi_{\mu}\,\,\longrightarrow\,\,\frac{1}{m_{3/2}M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}}\overline{\Xi}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}G\sim\frac{1}{\Lambda_{\not{\textup{susy}}}^{2}}\partial_{\mu}\overline{\Xi}^{\mu}G\ ,

(2.1.51)

where we see explicitly that the coupling scale is indeed the scale of supersymmetry breaking. Following this perspective, one can take in particular the decoupling limit of gravity
M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}\to\infty
while keeping the
\Lambda_{\not{\textup{susy}}}
scale fixed. At low-energies, where some of the superpartners of the original theory may have decoupled as well, one obtains in this way an effective theory for the goldstino
G
and the remaining light fields. These are the theories of nonlinear supersymmetry we discussed in Chapter
1
. In particular, one finds that the low-energy interactions of the goldstino are described by the Volkov–Akulov lagrangian (
1.3.19
)
[
52
]
. From the point of view of the constrained superfield formalism and their consistency, that we discuss in Section
1.4
, this shows explicitly how the nilpotent superfield constraint (
1.4.1
) describing the goldstino arises as an effective field theory operator from a 2-derivative microscopic theory.

2.2
Massive gravitino in cosmology

We now focus on supergravity theories with spontaneously broken supersymmetry, discussed in Section
2.1.2
, and present the results we obtained in
[
2
]
, which concern the so-called
gravitational production
of massive gravitinos in cosmological settings.

As we saw already in Section
1.4
for the global case, supersymmetry breaking is a crucial element to investigate the phenomenological consequences of supergravity, not only as a consistency requirement to match observations, but, more importantly, because it drives and fully characterizes the effective low-energy dynamics. The formalism of constrained superfields presented in Section
1.4
is very useful for building effective theories with spontaneous supersymmetry breaking also in the local case
[
58
,
93
,
94
,
95
,
96
,
97
,
98
,
99
,
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
,
107
,
108
,
109
,
110
,
111
,
67
,
60
,
61
,
112
]
. The main difference with respect to rigid supersymmetry is that now the super-Higgs mechanism takes place, so that one ends up with effective theories for the massive gravitino (in curved spacetime) rather than goldstino ones.

A specific application of this type is the construction of supergravity models for inflation
[
97
,
99
,
100
,
68
,
67
,
76
,
69
,
70
,
78
,
79
,
1
]
. In particular, the nilpotent superfield (
1.4.1
) and the orthogonal superfield (
1.4.6
) yield, once coupled to gravity, models of inflation with a minimal spectrum, made of the graviton, the massive gravitino and a real scalar field, which is identified with the inflaton
[
99
,
67
,
68
]
. Although the associated rigid goldstino lagrangian, given in eq. (
1.4.11
), is rather involved, in supergravity the goldstino gets absorbed, as said, by the massive gravitino, so that --- after the constraints have been solved for --- one can work directly in the unitary gauge and set the goldstino field consistently to zero. Thus, starting from a canonical Kähler potential
4
4

4

With respect to eq. (
1.4.9
), we take here
h=1
.
and the most general superpotential with this superfield constraint setup, given in eq. (
1.4.9
), one obtains the very simple on-shell lagrangian
[
67
]

\displaystyle e^{-1}\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}R-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}D_{\nu}\Psi_{\rho}+\frac{m\left(\phi\right)}{2}\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\Psi_{\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi-V\left(\phi\right)+e^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\textup{torsion}}\ ,

(2.2.1)

where
\Psi_{\mu}
is the massive gravitino,
\phi
the real scalar field left independent by the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
)-(
1.4.8
) and
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{torsion}}
is the standard supergravity torsion term (
2.1.17
). The field-dependent gravitino mass parameter
m(\phi)
and the scalar potential
V(\phi)
are parameterized by the two holomorphic functions
f(\phi)
and
g(\phi)
that enter the superpotential (
1.4.9
). Taking these functions to be real, the gravitino mass is given, according to eq. (
2.1.29
)-(
2.1.49
), by

m\left(\phi\right)=g\left(\phi\right)\ ,

(2.2.2)

while the scalar potential is equal to

V\left(\phi\right)=f^{2}\left(\phi\right)-3g^{2}\left(\phi\right).

(2.2.3)

Notice that, with respect to the general formula (
2.1.30
), only the F-term of the nilpotent superfield appears in the scalar potential. The orthogonal superfield one is constrained to be a function of the fermions, according to (
1.4.8
), and therefore does not contribute to the scalar potential. Thus, as anticipated, the lagrangian (
2.2.1
) contains a single scalar field coupled to spontaneously broken supergravity and constitutes a minimal supergravity model for inflation. The known inflationary models can be embedded in this setup by engineering the functions
f\left(\phi\right)
and
g\left(\phi\right)
that determine the scalar potential (
2.2.3
)
[
67
,
68
]
.

2.2.1
Gravitino in FLRW

More generally, the theory (
2.2.1
) is an explicit and simplified supergravity framework in which to further investigate cosmological applications. One can in fact assume a time-dependent scalar field background solution of the Friedmann–Lemaitre–Robertson–Walker (FLRW) type:

g_{\mu\nu}=a(\tau)^{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}\ ,

(2.2.4)

where
a(\tau)
is the scale factor and
\tau
is the so-called conformal time, related to the standard cosmological time
t
as
dt=a(\tau)d\tau
. The Einstein equations that describe this background are

G_{\mu\nu}=T_{\mu\nu}^{(\phi)}\ ,

(2.2.5)

where the gravity side is given by
5
5

5

Derivatives with respect to the conformal time will be denoted as
a^{\prime}\equiv\frac{da}{d\tau}
, while derivatives with respect to the cosmological time
t
will be denoted as
\dot{a}\equiv\frac{da}{dt}
.

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}=a^{2}\left[\left(4H^{2}-\frac{R}{3}\right)\delta^{0}_{\mu}\delta^{0}_{\nu}+\left(H^{2}-\frac{R}{3}\right)\eta_{\mu\nu}\right]\ ,

\displaystyle R=

\displaystyle 6\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a^{3}}\ ,

(2.2.6)

in which
H
is the Hubble parameter

H=\frac{a^{\prime}}{a^{2}}\ ,

(2.2.7)

whereas the energy-momentum tensor of
\phi
is

\displaystyle T^{(\phi)}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial_{\nu}\phi-g_{\mu\nu}\left(\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\rho}\phi\partial^{\rho}\phi+V(\phi)\right)=

(2.2.8)

\displaystyle=a^{2}\delta^{0}_{\mu}\delta^{0}_{\nu}\left(\frac{{\phi^{\prime}}{}^{2}}{2a^{2}}+V(\phi)\right)+a^{2}\delta_{ij}\delta^{i}_{\mu}\delta^{j}_{\nu}\left(\frac{{\phi^{\prime}}{}^{2}}{2a^{2}}-V(\phi)\right)\ .

Recalling eq. (
2.2.2
)-(
2.2.3
), the Einstein equations (
2.2.5
) for the FLRW ansatz (
2.2.4
) result

\displaystyle 3\left(H^{2}+m^{2}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{{\phi^{\prime}}{}^{2}}{2a^{2}}+f^{2}\ ,

(2.2.9)

\displaystyle\left(H^{2}-3m^{2}-\frac{R}{3}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{{\phi^{\prime}}{}^{2}}{2a^{2}}-f^{2}\ .

In such a cosmological setup, the dynamics of the massive gravitino turns out to be quite peculiar
[
113
,
114
,
115
,
116
,
117
,
118
,
76
,
69
,
77
,
70
]
, and we now discuss it in detail. In a curved background, the gravitino sector of the lagrangian (
2.2.1
) is
6
6

6

We neglect the quartic terms
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{torsion}}
in (
2.2.1
) since they do not play a significant role in the present discussion.

e^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\Psi}=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}\nabla_{\nu}\Psi_{\rho}+\frac{m}{2}\bar{\Psi}_{\mu}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\Psi_{\nu}\ ,

(2.2.10)

where
m
is the background-dependent mass, as in eq. (
2.2.1
)-(
2.2.2
), and
\nabla_{\mu}
is the full covariant derivative, including also the Levi--Civita connection.
7
7

7

Despite redundant at the level of eq. (
2.2.10
), the full covariant derivative
\nabla_{\mu}
is necessary to derive the gravitino equations of motion and constraints, given in eq. (
2.2.11
),(
2.2.12
) and (
2.2.13
), consistently with diffeomorphism invariance.
The equations of motion of the massive gravitino that follow from the lagrangian (
2.2.10
) are

\gamma^{\mu\nu\rho}\nabla_{\nu}\Psi_{\rho}=m\gamma^{\mu\nu}\Psi_{\nu}\ .

(2.2.11)

Contracting this equation with
\gamma^{\mu}
and
\nabla_{\mu}
yields two constraint equations, respectively

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\mu}\Psi_{\nu}=

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}m\gamma^{\mu}\Psi_{\mu}\ ,

(2.2.12)

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}\gamma^{\mu}\Psi^{\nu}=

\displaystyle 3m^{2}\gamma^{\mu}\Psi_{\mu}+2\left(\partial_{\mu}m\right)\gamma^{\mu\nu}\Psi_{\nu}\ ,

where
G_{\mu\nu}
is the Einstein tensor.
These constraints allow one to rewrite the equations of motion (
2.2.11
) in alternative form

\cancel{\nabla}\Psi^{\mu}-\nabla^{\mu}\left(\gamma^{\rho}\Psi_{\rho}\right)=-\frac{m}{2}\left(\gamma^{\mu\rho}\Psi_{\rho}+3\Psi^{\mu}\right)\ .

(2.2.13)

Let us now specify these general equations to the FLRW background (
2.2.4
) under consideration. The gravitino lagrangian (
2.2.10
) is typically written in canonical form via the redefinition

\Psi_{\mu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{a}}\psi_{\mu}\ ,

(2.2.14)

and after having expanded the whole dependence on the metric (
2.2.4
), one obtains
8
8

8

For convenience, we employ in this section the following notation for the flat space Dirac matrices, which will carry the same Greek indices as the curved ones but denoted with a bar,
i.e.

\gamma^{\mu}=e^{\mu}{}_{a}\gamma^{a}=a(\tau)^{-1}\delta^{\mu}{}_{a}\gamma^{a}\equiv a(\tau)^{-1}\bar{\gamma}^{\mu}

.

\mathcal{L}_{\psi}=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\bar{\gamma}^{\mu\nu\rho}\partial_{\nu}\psi_{\rho}+\frac{am}{2}\bar{\psi}_{\mu}\bar{\gamma}^{\mu\nu}\psi_{\nu}-aH\bar{\psi}_{\rho}\bar{\gamma}^{\rho}\psi_{0}\ .

(2.2.15)

The equations of motion (
2.2.11
) become

\bar{\gamma}^{\mu\nu\rho}\partial_{\nu}\psi_{\rho}-am\bar{\gamma}^{\mu\rho}\psi_{\rho}+aH\left(\bar{\gamma}^{\mu}\psi_{0}-\delta^{\mu}_{0}\bar{\gamma}^{\rho}\psi_{\rho}\right)=0\ ,

(2.2.16)
