while the constraints (
2.2.12
) are instead given by

\partial_{\mu}\psi^{\mu}-\cancel{\partial}\left(\bar{\gamma}^{\rho}\psi_{\rho}\right)-2aH\psi_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\left(3am-aH\bar{\gamma}^{0}\right)\left(\bar{\gamma}^{\rho}\psi_{\rho}\right)=0\ ,

(2.2.17)

and

\bar{\gamma}^{0}\psi_{0}=C(\tau)\,\bar{\gamma}^{i}\psi_{i}\ .

(2.2.18)

This second constraint implies that the component
\psi_{0}
is actually not an independent field. The function
C(\tau)
is equal to
[
113
,
114
,
69
]

C(\tau)=C_{\textup{R}}(\tau)+C_{\textup{I}}(\tau)\bar{\gamma}_{0}=\frac{H^{2}-3m^{2}-\nicefrac{{R}}{{3}}}{3\left(H^{2}+m^{2}\right)}+\frac{2m^{\prime}}{3a\left(H^{2}+m^{2}\right)}\bar{\gamma}_{0}\ ,

(2.2.19)

it reduces to
C\left(\tau\right)\to-1
in the flat space limit and its square is equal to the sound speed parameter
\lvert C\rvert^{2}=c_{s}^{2}
we introduced in Section
1.4.1
.

In the context of the low-energy effective theories of nonlinear rigid supersymmetry studied in Section
1.4
, the sound speed parameter
c_{s}^{2}
characterized the motion of the goldstino field
G
(see eq. (
1.4.21
), (
1.4.31
) and (
1.4.51
)). In the local case, the sound speed indeed parameterizes the propagation of the longitudinal polarization states of the massive gravitino. The transverse states propagate instead at the speed of light (
i.e.

c_{s}=1
). This can be seen explicitly through a proper identification of the gravitino field
\psi_{\mu}
into transverse and longitudinal modes. This is done in momentum space, in which one works via the expansion

\psi_{\mu}(\tau,\vec{x})=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{3}}\psi_{\mu,k}(\tau)e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\ ,

(2.2.20)

where the independent space components
\psi_{i,k}
can be decomposed as
9
9

9

More details about the notation and the algebraic rules for the momentum space operators are collected in Appendix
A.2
.

[
113
,
114
,
117
]

\psi_{i,k}=\psi_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}+\left(P_{\gamma}\right)_{i}\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\cdot\vec{\psi}_{k}+\left(P_{k}\right)_{i}\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\psi}_{k}\ ,

(2.2.21)

in terms of the two projectors

\displaystyle\left(P_{\gamma}\right)_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{k_{i}}{\vec{k}^{2}}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)-\bar{\gamma}_{i}\right]\ ,

\displaystyle\left(P_{k}\right)_{i}=\frac{1}{2\vec{k}^{2}}\left[\bar{\gamma}_{i}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)-3k_{i}\right]\ .

(2.2.22)

The decomposition (
2.2.21
) is such that

\left(P_{\gamma}\right)^{i}\psi_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}=\left(P_{k}\right)^{i}\psi_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}=0\ ,

(2.2.23)

which characterizes
\psi_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}
as being the transverse gravitino component. Moreover, the two constraints (
2.2.17
)-(
2.2.18
) become, in momentum space,

\displaystyle\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\psi}_{k}=\left[\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}+ia\left(m-H\bar{\gamma}^{0}\right)\right]\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\cdot\vec{\psi}_{k}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\gamma}^{0}\psi_{0,k}=C(\tau)\left(\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\cdot\vec{\psi}_{k}\right)\ ,

(2.2.24)

so that the decomposition (
2.2.21
) writes as

\psi_{i,k}=\psi_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}+\hat{O}_{i}\theta_{k}\ ,

(2.2.25)

where
\theta_{k}\equiv\bar{\gamma}^{i}\psi_{i,k}
specifies the longitudinal gravitino components, which are extracted through the projective operator

\hat{O}_{i}=\frac{1}{\vec{k}^{2}}\left\{-k_{i}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)+\frac{ia}{2}\left[\bar{\gamma}_{i}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)-3k_{i}\right]\left(m-H\bar{\gamma}^{0}\right)\right\}\ .

(2.2.26)

Thus, making use of this decomposition, together with the redefinition

\theta_{k}=\sqrt{\frac{3\left(m^{2}+H^{2}\right)a^{2}}{2\vec{k}^{2}}}\bar{\gamma}^{0}\vartheta_{k}\ ,

(2.2.27)

that canonically normalizes the field
\theta
, one finds that the massive gravitino lagrangian in FRLW (
2.2.10
) splits into transverse and longitudinal sectors
[
113
,
114
,
117
]

\mathcal{L}_{\psi}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{t}}+\mathcal{L}_{\vartheta}\ ,

(2.2.28)

which are completely decoupled from each other. The transverse sector is equal to

\mathcal{L}_{\textup{t}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\bar{\psi}_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}\left[\bar{\gamma}^{0}\partial_{0}+i\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)+am\right]\psi_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}\ ,

(2.2.29)

and thus has the form of a standard spin-
\frac{1}{2}
fermion in FLRW, propagating with unitary sound speed and canonical time-dependent rescaling of the mass. On the contrary, the longitudinal sector is given by

\mathcal{L}_{\vartheta}=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\vartheta}_{k}\left[\bar{\gamma}^{0}\partial_{0}-i\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)C+aM\right]\vartheta_{k}\ ,

(2.2.30)

which shows that the dynamics of the longitudinal gravitino
\vartheta_{k}
it describes is characterized, as anticipated around eq. (
2.2.19
), by a nontrivial sound speed

c_{s}^{2}=\lvert C^{2}(\tau)\rvert=C_{\textup{R}}^{2}(\tau)+C_{\textup{I}}^{2}(\tau)\ ,

(2.2.31)

as well as a nontrivial time-dependent mass parameter

M\equiv-\left[\frac{m}{2}+\frac{3}{2}\left(mC_{\textup{R}}+HC_{\textup{I}}\right)\right]\ .

(2.2.32)

The nontrivial sound speed is what makes the longitudinal gravitino dynamics in FLRW very peculiar. Going back to the starting model (
2.2.1
), which we recall is built coupling pure supergravity to one nilpotent and one orthogonal superfield
[
67
]
, we can write the sound speed (
2.2.31
) in terms of the scalar field background by means of the associated Einstein equations (
2.2.9
). For a general background, one obtains
10
10

10

Note that eq. (
2.2.34
) is written using the cosmological time
t
, for convenience of writing.

c_{s}^{2}=1-\frac{2\dot{\phi}^{2}}{\left(\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}+3m^{2}+V\right)^{2}}\left[V+3m^{2}-2\left(m^{\prime}\right)^{2}\right]\ ,

(2.2.33)

so that in the case of the theory (
2.2.1
), of gravitino mass
m
and scalar potential
V
given in eq. (
2.2.2
) and (
2.2.3
), it results in

c_{s}^{2}=1-\frac{2\dot{\phi}^{2}}{\left(\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}+f(\phi)^{2}\right)^{2}}\left[f(\phi)^{2}-2g^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}\right]\ .

(2.2.34)

This sound speed displays two critical limits, associated with equally critical behaviors. The first issue is that there is no condition, at this level, that prevents the sound speed to become superluminal during the scalar field evolution
[
70
,
1
]
and this is clearly not acceptable from the point of view of the consistency of the theory. The second issue is instead phenomenological. It was in fact argued in
[
69
,
77
]
that the limit in which the sound speed goes to zero is associated with a divergent gravitational production of longitudinal gravitinos, signaling the breakdown of the theory before the Planck scale.

We first encountered the superluminal sound speed problem in Chapter
1
, while presenting the results from
[
1
]
. In the next paragraph, we expand and complete the discussion of this topic from Section
1.4
. In the following paragraph we instead elaborate more on the gravitational overproduction of gravitinos for vanishing sound speed, as discussed in
[
69
]
. Gravitational particle production of gravitinos, on the other hand, is also the focus of the work
[
2
]
, and we will address it in detail in the next section.

The
\bm{c_{s}>1}
limit

As reported in Section
1.4
, we analyzed and solved the problem of superluminal sound speed in nonlinear supergravity in
[
1
]
. The nontrivial causality constraint

c_{s}\leq 1\,\,\longleftrightarrow\,\,f(\phi)^{2}\leq 2g^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}\ ,

(2.2.35)

that follows from eq. (
2.2.34
) is understood as a consequence of the badly defined orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), which is used to construct the model (
2.2.1
). This constraint requires in fact higher-derivative operators already in the UV
[
61
]
, so that the associated effective field theory is not guaranteed to be well-defined a priori. By means of the gravitino equivalence theorem
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
(
2.1.50
), in
[
1
]
we address this problem in terms of the goldstino in the rigid limit. The goldstino sound speed, which we wrote in eq. (
1.4.21
), actually matches the longitudinal gravitino one (
2.2.34
), since no term scales with the Planck mass
M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}
in (
2.2.34
). Then, from this goldstino perspective the causality constraint (
1.4.22
)-(
2.2.35
) is related to positivity bounds on the scattering amplitudes
[
1
,
65
,
66
]
and we interpret it, from the bottom-up point of view, as an obstruction to a UV completion into a microscopic two-derivative theory. This is the main result of
[
1
]
, which we described in detail in Section
1.4
of this manuscript.

In
[
1
]
we also formulate improved theories like (
2.2.1
), based on the generalized constraint (
1.4.38
) of
[
61
]
. These theories, defined by the constraints of eq. (
1.4.40
)-(
1.4.41
), maintain the minimality of the spectrum of (
2.2.1
) but are not subject to any causality constraint. This can be seen explicitly from the associated goldstino sound speed, given in eq. (
1.4.51
), which is subluminal all along the evolution of the background scalar field. Moving then to the associated supergravity model, following the equivalence theorem backwards, the longitudinal gravitino sound speed (
2.2.33
) again matches its rigid goldstino counterpart. The supergravity scalar potential (
2.1.30
) for this improved constraints setup is in fact equal to

V_{\textup{improved}}=f(\phi)^{2}+2g^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}-3g(\phi)^{2}\ ,

(2.2.36)

so that the sound speed of this model is computed from eq. (
2.2.33
) to be

c_{s}^{2}=1-\frac{2\dot{\phi}^{2}f(\phi)^{2}}{\left(\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2}+f(\phi)^{2}+g^{\prime}(\phi)^{2}\right)^{2}}\ ,

(2.2.37)

which is indeed exactly equal to the goldstino one of eq. (
1.4.31
), and thus it is always subluminal. Therefore, the construction we put forward in
[
1
]
defines consistent effective field theories of nonlinear supergravity and represent an improved class of minimal supergravity models for inflation with respect to (
2.2.1
).

Note, however, that also the improved sound speed (
1.4.31
)-(
2.2.37
) can vanish during the scalar field evolution, so that the problem of the longitudinal gravitino overproduction
[
69
]
does affect, from this point of view, also the improved models of
[
1
]
.

The
\bm{c_{s}\to 0}
limit and gravitational particle production

The production of gravitinos, which becomes divergent in the
c_{s}\to 0
limit for the orthogonal constraint model (
2.2.1
), exemplifies a well-known and significant phenomenon in cosmology — and more generally in quantum field theory in expanding spacetime backgrounds — namely, gravitational particle production
[
119
,
120
,
121
,
122
,
123
]
. This is a quantum, non-perturbative process in which particles are produced from the vacuum as a consequence of the expanding spacetime background. This phenomenon plays a crucial role in the theory of reheating of the universe after inflation
[
124
,
125
]
. In supergravity, it has been applied to study the production of gravitinos in the early universe, in order to test its compatibility with primordial nucleosynthesis
[
113
,
114
,
115
,
116
,
117
,
118
]
and the general viability and consistency of supergravity models for cosmology
[
76
,
69
,
77
,
70
,
79
]
.

In an expanding background, the notion of number of particles in a quantum state is intrinsically ambiguous because there is no unique definition of vacuum state
[
122
,
123
]
. The standard procedure is to consider the two initial and final stages of the evolution, the far past and future, in which the time dependence can be assumed to be turned off and a vacuum state can then be defined according to the standard rules of quantum mechanics. In quantum field theory, this is typically done through the Hamiltonian for the canonically normalized fields
[
120
,
125
]
. The two ingoing and outgoing Fock spaces are, a priori, different, but can be related through the so-called Bogolyubov transformations
[
119
,
120
,
121
,
122
,
123
]
. Schematically, these transformations act on the two sets of ladder operators
\{a_{\textup{in}},a^{\dagger}_{\textup{in}}\}
and
\{a_{\textup{out}},a^{\dagger}_{\textup{out}}\}
as

\begin{pmatrix}a_{\textup{out}}\\
a^{\dagger}_{\textup{out}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha&\beta^{\dagger}\\
\beta&\alpha^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{\textup{in}}\\
a^{\dagger}_{\textup{in}}\end{pmatrix}\ .

(2.2.38)

The Bogolyubov coefficients
\alpha
and
\beta
are usually time-dependent and must be normalized as

\lvert\alpha\rvert^{2}-\lvert\beta\rvert^{2}=1\ ,

(2.2.39)

in order to be consistent with the algebra of the ladder operators. Then, particles are said to have been gravitationally produced in the sense that the standard number operator
N_{\textup{out}}=a_{\textup{out}}^{\dagger}a_{\textup{out}}
associated with the outgoing vacuum has a nontrivial expectation value with respect to the ingoing one, parameterized by the off-diagonal Bogolyubov coefficient
\lvert\beta\rvert^{2}
:

\langle N_{\textup{out}}\rangle_{\textup{in}}=\lvert\beta\rvert^{2}\ .

(2.2.40)

This formalism was applied in
[
76
,
116
,
115
,
117
,
118
,
69
]
to study gravitational production of gravitinos, and in
[
69
]
the orthogonal supergravity model (
2.2.1
) is considered.
After decomposing the massive gravitino into its transverse and longitudinal modes, as described in eq. (
2.2.29
) and (
2.2.30
), the Bogolyubov coefficient
\beta
is obtained by numerically solving the equations of motion for both polarizations. While the rate of transverse gravitino production is suppressed at high momenta, as physically expected, the rate of longitudinal gravitino production is instead found to progressively increase almost linearly with the associated momentum in the case where the sound speed parameter (
2.2.31
) vanishes. This relation with the sound speed parameter can be understood more analytically by examining the adiabaticity parameter associated with gravitino propagation
[
69
]
. For a field
\varphi_{k}(\tau)
propagating with oscillator-like dispersion relations,
i.e.
of the type

\partial_{\tau}^{2}\varphi_{k}+\omega_{k}^{2}\varphi_{k}=0\ ,

(2.2.41)

in which
c_{s}
is the sound speed, the adiabaticity parameter can be defined as

A_{k}(\tau)=\frac{\partial_{\tau}\omega_{k}}{\omega_{k}^{2}}\ .

(2.2.42)

The longitudinal gravitino can be seen from (
2.2.30
) to propagate with

\omega_{k}^{2}=c_{s}^{2}k^{2}+a^{2}M^{2}\ ,

(2.2.43)

so that
[
69
]

A_{k}(\tau)=\frac{c_{s}\left(\partial_{\tau}c_{s}\right)\vec{k}^{2}+a^{3}HM^{2}+a^{2}M\partial_{\tau}M}{\left(c_{s}^{2}k^{2}+a^{2}M^{2}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\ .

(2.2.44)

Thus, for a non-vanishing sound speed, the adiabaticity parameter in eq. (
2.2.44
) approaches zero at high momenta, reflecting a suppression in the rate of production of the corresponding states. This is what happens for the transverse gravitino, which propagates with sound speed equal to 1, as seen in eq. (
2.2.29
). On the contrary, if the sound speed vanishes, the adiabaticity parameter no longer depends on the momentum of the produced particles, meaning that particles with arbitrarily large momentum can be produced. Such a process clearly violates unitarity and signals the breakdown of the effective field theory.

This breakdown of the theory when the gravitino is quantized does not have a clear connection with the effective field theory setup itself. In fact, although the orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), upon which the model (
2.2.1
) is based, does not define reliable effective theories due to its higher-derivative UV origin
[
61
,
1
]
, the improved models we constructed in
[
1
]
do have a well-defined microscopic origin, and yet they can suffer from the problematic overproduction, as the associated sound speed (
2.2.37
) is not prevented from vanishing during the background scalar field evolution. Moreover, the same mechanism was studied in
[
70
]
for several effective constrained superfield theories and it was shown that when a second fermion (the would-be inflatino) is present in the spectrum, its mixing with the longitudinal gravitino prevents the problematic unbounded production. Finally, the lagrangian (
2.2.1
) can also describe --- within some assumptions --- pure supergravity coupled to a chiral multiplet,
11
11

11

In this last case, the chiral multiplet is the one containing the inflaton
\phi
, and its fermion superpartner is the goldstino during inflation, which can be gauged away in the unitary gauge. The scalar field is, however, complex, so that the setup (
2.2.1
) applies if the additional scalar is very heavy or frozen during inflation. If, on the contrary, the additional scalar mode undergoes a nontrivial dynamics, some changes are expected.

[
113
,
114
]
which is in principle defined up to the Planck scale.

These unclear and exceptional features of nonlinear supergravity theories motivated us to look more closely into the framework of gravitational particle production. We now report on our findings
[
2
]
in the next sections.

2.2.2
Gravitational particle production revisited – a first example

Gravitational particle production is a subtle mechanism that can reveal unexpected features of a given theory, both phenomenologically and in terms of theoretical consistency. Its richness stems from the way it intertwines particle physics, general relativity, and quantum field theory. From this point of view, the case of the gravitino discussed so far is particularly interesting because of its tight connection with gravity, which strongly persists also when supersymmetry is spontaneously broken and the gravitino becomes massive, as shown by its general equations of motion (
2.2.11
)-(
2.2.12
). This perspective was pushed in
[
69
,
77
]
by trying to make use of gravitino gravitational production to infer information about the properties of quantum gravity along the lines of the Swampland program
[
126
,
127
]
.

However, despite being at the basis of this mechanism, the time-dependence of the background in which gravitational particle production occurs also gives rise to several ambiguities in its physical interpretation. First of all, as mentioned at the end of the previous section, there is no unique, unambiguous way to identify the vacuum state of the theory
[
122
,
123
]
. Consequently, the definition of the number operator and the concept of number of produced particles can only be established relative to two chosen vacua at different times, in the sense expressed by the Bogolyubov transformation (
2.2.38
). No intrinsic definitions of this type are actually possible
[
122
]
.

These ambiguities are reflected also in the hamiltonian formalism that is commonly used to quantify the gravitational particle production phenomenon, which is indeed subtle in time-dependent backgrounds
[
128
,
129
,
130
,
131
]
. The quantization of a theory is typically performed using the hamiltonian of the canonically normalized fields. This hamiltonian — which we will call
instantaneous
in what follows — is usually of the harmonic oscillator type and thus allows one to perform the quantization of the theory in a standard way
[
125
]
. This instantaneous hamiltonian describes correctly the propagation of the associated canonically normalized field. However, it does not define the correct energy operator of the system. This is given by the energy-momentum tensor, entering Einstein equations, which is computed from the original covariant action. In hamiltonian terms, the same energy operator is obtained by working with the original, non-rescaled fields. However, these fields have a more complicated time evolution, so that the quantization of the system is less straightforward.

The differences between the various Hamiltonian formulations are well known
[
128
,
129
,
130
,
131
]
. While they effectively coincide for theories in flat space, they no longer align when the theory is placed in a time-dependent background. The mismatch arises from the fact that, to canonically normalize the starting covariant field, one must perform a time-dependent rescaling that breaks the field transformation properties under the original diffeomorphisms. However, the coupling to gravity selects the stress-energy tensor as unambiguous physical energy operator, being the source of the metric in the Einstein equations. Therefore, the differences with respect to the instantaneous Hamiltonian acquire physical significance once the latter is used to quantize the theory. In particular, it follows that the vacuum state defined via the instantaneous hamiltonian, from which particle production is usually computed
[
120
,
125
]
, is not a state of minimum energy. On the other hand, physical processes involving such produced particles, like for example their backreaction on the spacetime geometry, should have a well-defined description in terms of the Einstein equations. From this point of view, the natural choice for the vacuum would be the state of zero energy as computed via the stress-energy tensor. The time evolution would then be governed by a different Bogolyubov transformation, so that the resulting final state cannot be interpreted as containing particles relative to the instantaneous Hamiltonian vacuum.

The physical consequences of this difference between the instantaneous hamiltonian and the stress-energy tensor are the subject of the work
[
2
]
. In particular, we parameterize it in terms of a specific Bogolyubov transformation, which we compute analytically for massive particles of different spin propagating in an FLRW spacetime. Whereas for fields of spin 0,
\frac{1}{2}
and 1 the described difference is under control from a physical point of view, the case of the gravitino displays, again, some subtle (if not puzzling) features.

We now present the results of
[
2
]
, and we begin with the well-known example of the real scalar field. The more interesting case of the gravitino is discussed in detail in the section that follows.

A massive scalar field
\Phi(\tau,\vec{x})
in FLRW spacetime (
2.2.4
) is described by the action

\displaystyle S_{\Phi}=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x{}\sqrt{-g}{}\ \mathcal{L}_{\Phi}=

(2.2.45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{-g}{}\ \left(\partial_{\mu}\Phi\partial^{\mu}\Phi+m^{2}\Phi^{2}\right)=\int d^{4}x\ \frac{a^{2}}{2}\left[\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)^{2}-\left(\vec{\nabla}\Phi\right)^{2}-a^{2}m^{2}\Phi^{2}\right]\ .

Canonical normalization is obtained via the time-dependent rescaling

\Phi\equiv\frac{\varphi}{a}\ ,

(2.2.46)

leading to

\displaystyle S_{\varphi}=

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x\ {}\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{4}x{}\left\{\left(\varphi^{\prime}\right)^{2}-\varphi\left(-\vec{\nabla}^{2}+a^{2}m^{2}-\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}\right)\varphi\right\}\ .

(2.2.47)

Notice that in this way the action has the simplified form of a massive scalar field in flat space with time-dependent mass. Working in momentum space via the decomposition
12
12

12

The subscript
\bm{k}
denotes dependence on the whole vector
\vec{k}
, while the subscript
k
indicates dependence only on its modulus.

\varphi\left(\tau,\vec{x}\right)=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\left\{\varphi_{\bm{k}}(\tau)e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}+\bar{\varphi}_{\bm{k}}(\tau)e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}\right\}\ .

(2.2.48)

the equation of motion becomes the one of the harmonic oscillator:

\varphi^{\prime\prime}_{\bm{k}}+\omega^{2}{}\varphi_{\bm{k}}=0\ ,

(2.2.49)

with time-dependent frequency

\omega^{2}\equiv\vec{k}^{2}+a^{2}m^{2}-\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}\equiv\omega_{0}^{2}-\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}\ .

(2.2.50)

The instantaneous hamiltonian

Due to its simple, harmonic oscillator-like structure, the setup defined by the canonically normalized field
\varphi
— rather than the original
\Phi
in (
2.2.45
) — is the natural choice for quantizing the system, which can be done in the standard way. The conjugate momentum is given by

\Pi_{\varphi}\equiv\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}}{\partial\varphi^{\prime}}=\varphi^{\prime}\ ,

(2.2.51)

so that the hamiltonian density
\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}
associated with the canonically normalized action of eq. (
2.2.47
) is equal to

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\Pi_{\varphi}^{2}+\varphi\left(-\vec{\nabla}^{2}+a^{2}m^{2}-\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}\right)\varphi\right]\ .

(2.2.52)

This is what we called instantaneous hamiltonian. Quantization is realized upgrading the conjugate variables to operators and imposing the usual (equal-time) commutation relation

\left[\varphi(\tau,\vec{x}),\Pi_{\varphi}(\tau,\vec{y})\right]=i{}\delta^{(3)}\left(\vec{x}-\vec{y}\right)\ .

(2.2.53)

The variables
\varphi
and
\Pi_{\varphi}
can then be expanded in terms of ladder operators as
13
13

13

The mode functions
\varphi_{k}
and
\Pi_{k}
in eq. (
2.2.54
) depend only on
k
because the equation of motion (
2.2.49
) is isotropic.

\displaystyle\varphi(\tau,\vec{x})=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\left\{\varphi_{k}(\tau)e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}{}a_{\bm{k}}+\bar{\varphi}_{k}(\tau)e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}{}a_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\right\},

(2.2.54)

\displaystyle\Pi_{\varphi}(\tau,\vec{x})=

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{3}k}{\left(2\pi\right)^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\left\{\Pi_{k}(\tau)e^{i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}{}a_{\bm{k}}+\bar{\Pi}_{k}(\tau)e^{-i\bm{k}\cdot\bm{x}}{}a_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\right\}\ .

The ladder operators
a_{\bm{k}}
and
a_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}
satisfy as well the usual commutation relation

\left[a_{\bm{k}},a_{\bm{k^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}\right]=\delta^{(3)}\left(\vec{k}-\vec{k}^{\prime}\right),\qquad\qquad\left[a_{\bm{k}},a_{\bm{k^{\prime}}}\right]=0=\left[a_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger},a_{\bm{k^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}\right]\ ,

(2.2.55)

if one imposes the additional "wronskian" condition

\varphi_{k}\bar{\Pi}_{k}-\bar{\varphi}_{k}\Pi_{k}=i\ .

(2.2.56)

Applying this decomposition to the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.52
) yields
[
117
]

\displaystyle H_{\varphi}=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}k\ {}\mathcal{H}_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}k{}\begin{pmatrix}a^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}}&a_{-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{E}_{k}&\mathcal{F}^{\dagger}_{k}\\
\mathcal{F}_{k}&\mathcal{E}_{k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{\bm{k}}\\
a^{\dagger}_{-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.57)

where we introduced the functions

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}=

\displaystyle|\Pi_{k}|^{2}+\omega^{2}|\varphi_{k}|^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}=\Pi_{k}^{2}+\omega^{2}\varphi_{k}^{2}\ .

(2.2.58)

This hamiltonian can be brought in diagonal form by means of a Bogolyubov decomposition

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\omega}}\left(\alpha_{k}+\beta_{k}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\Pi_{k}=-\frac{i\omega}{\sqrt{2\omega}}\left(-\alpha_{k}+\beta_{k}\right)\ .

(2.2.59)

The Bogolyubov coefficients
\alpha_{k}
and
\beta_{k}
are constrained by the wronskian condition (
2.2.56
) to be such that

\lvert\alpha_{k}\rvert^{2}-\lvert\beta_{k}\rvert^{2}=1\ ,

(2.2.60)

while the
\varphi_{k}
equation of motion (
2.2.49
) turns into the system of equations

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}_{k}=-i\omega\alpha_{k}+\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{2\omega}\beta_{k}\ ,

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}_{k}=i\omega\beta_{k}+\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{2\omega}\alpha_{k}\ .

(2.2.61)

As introduced schematically in eq. (
2.2.38
), the Bogolyubov transformations are maps between different sets of ladder operators. They in fact leave invariant, by construction, the commutation relations (
2.2.55
). In the case at hand, the Bogolyubov decomposition (
2.2.59
) defines the basis of ladder operators

\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}\\
\tilde{a}_{-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{k}&\beta_{k}^{\dagger}\\
\beta_{k}&\alpha_{k}^{\dagger}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{\bm{k}}\\
a_{-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.62)

in which the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.57
)) becomes diagonal
[
117
]
:

\displaystyle H_{\varphi}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}k{}\begin{pmatrix}a^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}}&a_{-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{k}^{\dagger}&\beta_{k}^{\dagger}\\
\beta_{k}&\alpha_{k}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\omega&0\\
0&\omega\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{k}&\beta_{k}^{\dagger}\\
\beta_{k}&\alpha_{k}^{\dagger}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{\bm{k}}\\
a^{\dagger}_{-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}=

(2.2.63)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}k{}\frac{\omega}{2}\left(\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}+\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\right)\ ,

In particular, the resulting diagonal hamiltonian is the one of a harmonic oscillator with (time-dependent) frequency
\omega
.

The diagonalization of the hamiltonian is performed because in this form it has a direct interpretation in terms of number of particles and associated occupation numbers. This is computed, at a given (conformal) time
\tau
of the evolution, through the number operator
N_{\varphi}(\tau)=\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}
, as in eq. (
2.2.40
). Following this perspective, it is natural to define the vacuum of the theory at initial time
\tau_{0}
as a state with no particles. This is possible if the initial conditions for the Bogolyubov coefficients (
2.2.59
)–(
2.2.61
) are set to be

\displaystyle\alpha_{l}\left(\tau_{0}\right)=1\ ,

\displaystyle\beta_{k}\left(\tau_{0}\right)=0\ ,

(2.2.64)

so that the vacuum state is the one annihilated by all the operators
a_{k}
:

a_{\bm{k}}|0\rangle_{\rm BD}\equiv{\tilde{a}}_{\bm{k}}(\tau_{0})|0\rangle_{\rm BD}\equiv 0\ .

(2.2.65)

This is the so-called Bunch–Davies vacuum
[
122
]
. Thus, although initially diagonal, the time evolution of the system, governed by the Bogolyubov coefficients through the equations of motion (
2.2.61
), causes the Hamiltonian to evolve into a non-diagonal form. Therefore, the analogue of the Bunch–Davies vacuum (
2.2.65
) at time
\tau>\tau_{0}
has a non-vanishing occupation number with respect to the initial one, proportional to the off-diagonal Bogolyubov coefficient

\left\langle N_{\varphi}(\tau)\right\rangle_{\textup{BD}}=\lvert\beta_{k}(\tau)\rvert^{2}\ .

(2.2.66)

The interpretation of this dynamics is that the time-dependent background sources the production of particles during the evolution of the system.

The stress-energy tensor

Defined in this way, the Bunch–Davies vacuum is the ground-state of the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.52
). In standard flat space quantum field theory, the above discussion would lead to the interpretation of the excited states with respect to this vacuum as states with
\langle N_{\varphi}\rangle_{\textup{BD}}
particles, of total energy given by the (continuous) sum of all the single-particle energies
\sim\omega_{k}\lvert\beta_{k}\rvert^{2}
, according to eq. (
2.2.63
)-(
2.2.66
). However, this is not true anymore for curved backgrounds. In this case, the energy must be described by the energy-momentum tensor, in order to be consistent with Einstein equations and the general curved spacetime setup. The instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.52
) quantifies correctly the time evolution of the field
\varphi
and its quantization, but it does not coincide with the energy operator given by the stress-energy tensor and therefore does not describe the energy of the system.

We now see this explicitly. The stress-energy tensor of the scalar field — computed from the starting covariant action (
2.2.45
) — is

T_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\Phi\partial_{\nu}\Phi-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\left[\left(\partial\Phi\right)^{2}+m^{2}\Phi^{2}\right]\ .

(2.2.67)

The energy operator is encoded in the
00
component of this tensor. In terms of the canonically normalized field defined in eq. (
2.2.46
), it is equal to

\displaystyle E_{\varphi}=\int d^{3}x\sqrt{-g}{}\ T^{0}{}_{0}=

\displaystyle\int d^{3}x{}\frac{a^{2}}{2}\left\{\left(\Phi^{\prime}\right)^{2}+\left(\vec{\nabla}\Phi\right)^{2}+a^{2}m^{2}\Phi^{2}\right\}=

(2.2.68)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}x{}\left\{\left(\varphi^{\prime}-aH\varphi\right)^{2}+\varphi\left(-\vec{\nabla}{}^{2}+a^{2}m^{2}\right)\varphi\right\}\ ,

which is clearly different from the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.52
). Performing then the canonical quantization (
2.2.54
) and applying the Bogolyubov decomposition (
2.2.59
), one obtains

E_{\varphi}=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}k{}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}}&\tilde{a}_{-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{E}_{k}&\mathscr{F}_{k}^{\dagger}\\
\mathscr{F}_{k}&\mathscr{E}_{k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}\\
\tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.69)

where
\{\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}},\tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}}\}
is the Fock space associated with the diagonalized instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.63
), whereas the functions
\mathscr{E}_{k}
and
\mathscr{F}_{k}
are defined to be

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{k}=

\displaystyle\omega_{0}^{2}+\omega^{2}+a^{2}H^{2}\ ,

(2.2.70)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{k}=

\displaystyle\omega_{0}^{2}-\omega^{2}+a^{2}H^{2}-2iaH\omega\ .

Thus, the energy operator
E_{\varphi}
is not diagonal in the basis where the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.63
) is. To put also
E_{\varphi}
in diagonal form, one needs a further Bogolyubov transformation, which can be computed explicitly to be
[
2
]

\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}}\\
\tilde{a}_{-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\alpha}_{k}&\hat{\beta}_{k}^{\dagger}\\
\hat{\beta}_{k}&\hat{\alpha}_{k}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{a}_{\bm{k}}\\
\hat{a}_{-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.71)

with

\hat{\alpha}_{k}=\frac{\omega+\omega_{0}+iaH}{2\sqrt{\omega\omega_{0}}}\ ,\qquad\qquad\hat{\beta}_{k}=\frac{\omega-\omega_{0}-iaH}{2\sqrt{\omega\omega_{0}}}\ .

(2.2.72)

The resulting diagonal energy operator is therefore

E_{\varphi}=\int d^{3}k{}\frac{\omega_{0}}{2}\left(\hat{a}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{\bm{k}}+\hat{a}_{\bm{k}}\hat{a}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\right)\ .

(2.2.73)

Notice that whereas the diagonal instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.63
) involved the shifted frequency
\omega
, the diagonal energy involves instead the simpler
\omega_{0}
, the two frequencies being related according to eq. (
2.2.50
).

On the difference between the two diagonal Fock spaces

This computation shows explicitly the claimed difference between the instantaneous hamiltonian and the energy operator computed from the stress-energy tensor and, with them, their associated Fock spaces. Because of the curved spacetime setting, the hamiltonian and the energy-momentum tensor are formally identified only as long as the covariant formulation is employed. This is clearly not the case for the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.52
) of the canonically normalized field (
2.2.46
)-(
2.2.47
). The time-dependent rescaling (
2.2.46
) breaks by construction the original covariance, and the resulting instantaneous hamiltonian is not connected anymore in a simple way to the starting ("covariant") one
[
131
]
.

The immediate physical consequence of this mismatch is that the zero-particle ground state (
2.2.65
) of the instantaneous hamiltonian is not a state of minimal energy

\left\langle E_{\varphi}(\tau_{0})\right\rangle_{\textup{BD}}=V_{3}\left[\omega_{0}\left(\frac{1}{2}+|{\hat{\beta}}_{k}|^{2}\right)\right](\tau_{0})=V_{3}\left[\frac{\omega^{2}+\omega_{0}^{2}+a^{2}H^{2}}{4\omega}\right](\tau_{0})\ ,

(2.2.74)

where we have introduced the space volume
V_{3}
as the momentum space delta function
V_{3}=\delta^{3}({\bm{k}}=0)
. In the same way, the state of minimum energy that can be defined through (
2.2.73
) does not have a definite interpretation in terms of number of particles from the point of view of the Fock space of the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.63
). Actually, the total number of such particles, obtained by integrating
\lvert\hat{\beta}_{k}\rvert^{2}
(
2.2.72
) over the whole momentum space, can be seen to be divergent. Nevertheless, at the level of the energy-momentum tensor, this divergence is not a problem
per se
, since the energy requires renormalization. This is a further indication that one should always refer to the proper energy operator as defined by the stress-energy tensor, rather then the instantaneous hamiltonian, for all physical applications.

From the physical point of view, the two different diagonal Fock spaces
\{\tilde{a}_{\bm{k}},\tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}}\}
and
\{\hat{a}_{\bm{k}},\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\bm{k}}\}
are expected to align in the large momentum limit
k\to\infty
, where the gravitational effects are suppressed. This physical requirement is realized if the Bogolyubov transformation (
2.2.71
)-(
2.2.72
) that connects the two Fock spaces becomes trivial in this limit, namely

\lim_{k\to\infty}\hat{\beta}_{k}=0\ .

(2.2.75)

From the explicit expression in eq. (
2.2.72
) we easily see that this condition is indeed satisfied, since for
k\to\infty
one has
\omega\to\omega_{0}
, so that
\hat{\beta}_{k}\to 0
.

2.2.3
Gravitational particle production revisited – the massive gravitino

The simple case of the scalar field already exemplifies the subtleties of the gravitational particle production mechanisms. In
[
2
]
we analyze in the same fashion also the fields of spin-
\frac{1}{2}
, 1 and
\frac{3}{2}
. The gravitational particle production of the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
Dirac fermion was originally discussed, from the instantaneous hamiltonian point of view, in
[
117
,
132
,
133
]
. We find that the instantaneous hamiltonian and the energy operator actually coincide, so that there is no difference between the two respective diagonal Fock spaces. The production of the massive spin-1 Proca gauge field was originally discussed in
[
134
,
135
,
136
]
. Similarly to the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
case, the transverse Proca gauge field is found to be free of ambiguities in the different Fock spaces. In contrast, the longitudinal polarization of the Proca field closely follows the case of the real scalar field and the Fock space of the energy operator differs from the one of the standard instantaneous hamiltonian by a Bogolyubov transformation of the type outlined in eq. (
2.2.72
). This correspondence can be understood consistently by means of the equivalence theorem for massive gauge fields.

Instead, the analysis of the physical differences between the energy operator and the instantaneous Hamiltonian of the massive spin-
\frac{3}{2}
field — which is the central element of
[
2
]
— yields nontrivial and intriguing results, which we now present in detail.

The instantaneous hamiltonian

We begin, as before, with the instantaneous hamiltonian and the associated canonical quantization
[
113
,
114
,
117
,
118
,
116
,
115
,
69
]
. The canonically normalized action for the transverse and longitudinal components of the massive gravitino field in momentum space are given respectively in eq. (
2.2.29
) and (
2.2.30
). The associated instantaneous hamiltonian densities are then

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\textup{t}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{3}\bar{\psi}_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}\left[i\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)+am\right]\psi_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta}=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\vartheta}_{k}\left[-i\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)C+aM\right]\vartheta_{k}\ .

(2.2.76)

The quantization can be performed following the prescriptions for the spin-
\frac{1}{2}
fermions discussed in
[
117
,
132
,
133
]
. From this point of view, the transverse gravitino
\psi_{i,k}^{\textup{t}}
has the same exact structure of the standard spin-
\frac{1}{2}
fermion, for which, as we said, there are no ambiguities related to the different energy formulations
[
2
]
. We therefore focus on the longitudinal component
\vartheta_{k}
. Its conjugate momentum is given by

\Pi_{\vartheta}\equiv\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\vartheta}}{\partial\partial_{0}\vartheta}=\frac{i}{2}\vartheta^{\dagger}\ ,

(2.2.77)

so that canonical quantization is realized by imposing the anticommutation relations

\displaystyle\left\{\vartheta(\tau,\vec{x}),\Pi_{\vartheta}(\tau,\vec{y})\right\}=\delta^{(3)}\left(\vec{x}-\vec{y}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\left\{\vartheta(\tau,\vec{x}),\vartheta(\tau,\vec{y})\right\}=\left\{\Pi_{\vartheta}(\tau,\vec{x}),\Pi_{\vartheta}(\tau,\vec{y})\right\}=0\ .

(2.2.78)

These conditions can be met by expanding the field
\vartheta
in momentum space in terms of creation and annihilation operators as

\vartheta(\tau,\vec{x})=\int\frac{d^{3}k}{(2\pi)^{\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}}}\sum_{r}e^{i\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}}\left[\vartheta_{r,\bm{k}}a_{r,\bm{k}}+v_{r,\bm{k}}a_{r,-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\right]\ .

(2.2.79)

Note that the mode functions and ladder operators now depend on the whole momentum vector
\bm{k}
and on the specific
\pm\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
longitudinal polarization, labeled by
r=\pm 1
. Moreover, the mode functions
\vartheta_{r,\bm{k}}
and
v_{r,\bm{k}}
have to satisfy a series of constructional requirements. They are normalized as

\vartheta_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\vartheta_{s,\bm{k}}=\delta_{rs}=v_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger}v_{s,\bm{k}}\ ,

(2.2.80)

and are related by the Majorana condition

\bar{v}_{r,\bm{k}}=\vartheta_{r,-\bm{k}}^{\textup{T}}C_{4}\ ,

(2.2.81)

where
C_{4}
is the charge conjugation matrix (see Appendix
A.1.3
). It is then convenient to express these mode functions using two-component notation
[
117
]
. Working in the reference frame where
\vec{k}=\left(0,0,k\right)
, the mode function
\vartheta_{r,\bm{k}}
is written as

\vartheta_{r,\bm{k}}=\begin{pmatrix}\vartheta_{A}(r,\bm{k})\\
\vartheta_{B}(r,\bm{k})\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}\left(\vartheta_{+}-r\vartheta_{-}\right)\psi_{r}\\
\left(\vartheta_{+}+r\vartheta_{-}\right)\psi_{r}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.82)

where the
\bm{k}
-dependence in
\vartheta_{\pm}
is left implicit and the two-component spinors
\psi_{r}\equiv(\psi_{+},\psi_{-})
are the eigenvectors of the helicity operator
\vec{\sigma}\cdot\hat{k}=\sigma^{3}
:

\psi_{+}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
\end{pmatrix}\ ,\quad\psi_{-}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\\
\end{pmatrix}\ ,\qquad\qquad\sigma^{3}\psi_{r}=r\psi_{r}\ .

(2.2.83)

With this parameterization, the ladder operators entering the decomposition (
2.2.79
) satisfy the canonical commutation relations

\left\{a_{r,\bm{k}},a_{s,\bm{q}}^{\dagger}\right\}=\delta_{rs}\delta^{(3)}\left(\vec{k}-\vec{q}\right)\ ,\qquad\qquad\left\{a_{r,\bm{k}},a_{s,\bm{q}}\right\}=0=\left\{a_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger},a_{s,\bm{q}}^{\dagger}\right\}\ ,

(2.2.84)

if the two-component mode functions
\vartheta_{\pm}
are normalized as

\lvert\vartheta_{+}\rvert^{2}+\lvert\vartheta_{-}\rvert^{2}=2\ .

(2.2.85)

Moreover, the longitudinal gravitino equations of motion, computed from the lagrangian (
2.2.30
), turn into a coupled system of equations for
\vartheta_{\pm}
:

{\vartheta}^{\prime}_{\pm}=-i\left(C_{\textup{R}}\mp iC_{\textup{I}}\right)k\vartheta_{\mp}\mp iaM\vartheta_{\pm}\ .

(2.2.86)

Thus, the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.76
) becomes

\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r}\left[\begin{pmatrix}a_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger}&a_{-r,-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{E}_{k}&\mathcal{F}_{k}^{\dagger}\\
\mathcal{F}_{k}&-\mathcal{E}_{k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{r,\bm{k}}\\
a_{-r,-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\right]\ ,

(2.2.87)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}=

\displaystyle-\frac{C_{\textup{R}}k}{2}\left(\vartheta_{+}^{*}\vartheta_{-}+\vartheta_{+}\vartheta_{-}^{*}\right)+\frac{i}{2}C_{\textup{I}}k\left(\vartheta_{+}^{*}\vartheta_{-}-\vartheta_{+}\vartheta_{-}^{*}\right)+\frac{aM}{2}\left(\lvert\vartheta_{+}\rvert^{2}-\lvert\vartheta_{-}\rvert^{2}\right)\ ,

(2.2.88)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{k}=

\displaystyle-\frac{C_{\textup{R}}k}{2}\left(\vartheta_{+}^{2}-\vartheta_{-}^{2}\right)-\frac{i}{2}C_{\textup{I}}k\left(\vartheta_{+}^{2}+\vartheta_{-}^{2}\right)-aM\vartheta_{+}\vartheta_{-}\ .

Following the same steps as for the scalar field case, we now diagonalize the hamiltonian (
2.2.87
) by means of a Bogolyubov transformation. This is implemented through the following decomposition of the mode functions
\vartheta_{\pm}

[
117
,
132
,
133
]

\displaystyle\vartheta_{+}\ =

\displaystyle c_{+}(k)\ \alpha_{\bm{k}}-c_{-}(k)\ \beta_{\bm{k}}\ ,

(2.2.89)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{-}\ =

\displaystyle c_{-}(k)\ \alpha_{\bm{k}}+c_{+}(k)\ \beta_{\bm{k}}\ .

Consistency with the mode functions equations of motion (
2.2.86
) requires the Bogolyubov coefficients
\alpha_{\bm{k}}
and
\beta_{\bm{k}}
to be respectively even and odd in
\bm{k}
:

\displaystyle\alpha_{-\bm{k}}=\alpha_{\bm{k}}\ ,

\displaystyle\beta_{-\bm{k}}=-\beta_{\bm{k}}\ ,

(2.2.90)

should satisfy

\lvert\alpha_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}+\lvert\beta_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}=1\ ,

(2.2.91)

in order to preserve the commutation relations (
2.2.78
)-(
2.2.84
). Moreover, the real coefficients
c_{\pm}
are such that

\displaystyle c^{2}_{+}({k})+c^{2}_{-}({k})=2\ ,

\displaystyle c_{+}(-k)=c_{+}(k)\ ,

\displaystyle c_{-}(-k)=-c_{-}(k)\ .

(2.2.92)

These are the necessary requirements for the Bogolyubov transformation (
2.2.89
) to be well-defined. The new set of ladder operators is then given by

\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{r,\bm{k}}\\
\tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{-r,-\bm{k}},\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{\bm{k}}&-\beta_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\\
\beta_{\bm{k}}&\alpha_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{r,\bm{k}}\\
a_{-r,-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.93)

and the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.87
) in this basis is equal to

\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{r}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger}&\tilde{a}_{-r,-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A_{k}&B_{k}\\
B_{k}^{\dagger}&-A_{k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{r,\bm{k}}\\
\tilde{a}^{\dagger}_{-r,-\bm{k}},\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.94)

where the functions
A_{k}
and
B_{k}
are given by

\displaystyle A_{k}=

\displaystyle-\left[c_{+}(k)c_{-}(k)\right]C_{\textup{R}}k+\left[\frac{c_{+}^{2}(k)-c_{-}^{2}(k)}{2}\right]aM\ ,

(2.2.95)

\displaystyle B_{k}=

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{c_{+}^{2}(k)-c_{-}^{2}(k)}{2}\right]C_{\textup{R}}k+iC_{\textup{I}}k-\left[c_{+}(k)c_{-}(k)\right]aM\ .

Contrary to the previous scalar field case, the Bogolyubov decomposition (
2.2.89
) is not enough to diagonalize the instantaneous hamiltonian of the longitudinal gravitino. In fact, the non-vanishing imaginary part of the sound speed operator
C_{\textup{I}}
of eq. (
2.2.19
) does not allow one to solve for
B_{k}=0
in eq. (
2.2.95
) in terms of the real coefficients
c_{\pm}
.
14
14

14

It is though possible to reabsorb the complex phase of the sound speed operator
C(\tau)
into a field redefinition of
\vartheta
. In this case, since the resulting sound speed parameter is real, there exist a choice of
c_{\pm}
in (
2.2.89
) that directly diagonalizes the hamiltonian. However, the final physical result does not depend on this specific phase choice.
A further Bogolyubov transformation is actually needed to reach the desired diagonal hamiltonian. This transformation is given by

\begin{pmatrix}\hat{b}_{r,\bm{k}}\\
\hat{b}^{\dagger}_{-r,-\bm{k}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\alpha}_{\bm{k}}&-\hat{\beta}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\\
\hat{\beta}_{\bm{k}}&\hat{\alpha}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{r,\bm{k}}\\
\tilde{a}_{-r,-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.96)

with
15
15

15

In (
2.2.97
) we write only the modulus of the Bogolyubov coefficients because the equation defining them can only be solved up to an unphysical phase.

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle-\frac{B_{k}}{\omega-A_{k}}\hat{\beta}_{\bm{k}},

\displaystyle\lvert\hat{\alpha}_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{\omega+A_{k}}{2\omega},

\displaystyle\lvert\hat{\beta}_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}=\frac{\omega-A_{k}}{2\omega}\ .

(2.2.97)

In these coefficients, the parameter
\omega
is the frequency defined by

\displaystyle\omega^{2}=

\displaystyle c_{s}^{2}k^{2}+a^{2}M^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle c_{s}^{2}=C_{\textup{R}}^{2}+C_{\textup{I}}^{2}\

(2.2.98)

and
c_{s}
is the sound speed of the longitudinal gravitino. The resulting diagonal instantaneous hamiltonian is therefore

\mathcal{H}_{\vartheta}=\frac{\omega}{2}\sum_{r}\left(\hat{b}_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\hat{b}_{r,\bm{k}}-\hat{b}_{r,\bm{k}}\hat{b}_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\right)\ .

(2.2.99)

We remark that this final result is actually independent of the specific choice of the coefficients
c_{\pm}
in (
2.2.89
), since they turn out to appear only in the combination fixed by the normalization (
2.2.92
).

The stress-energy tensor

The instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.99
) has to be compared with the energy operator for the longitudinal gravitino. The massive gravitino energy-momentum tensor, computed in generality from the starting covariant action (
2.2.10
), is equal (on-shell) to
[
2
]

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bar{\Psi}_{\rho}\gamma^{\rho\sigma(\mu}\left({\nabla}_{\sigma}\Psi^{\nu)}-{\nabla}^{\nu)}\Psi_{\sigma}\right)-\frac{m}{2}\bar{\Psi}_{\rho}\gamma^{\rho(\mu}\Psi^{\nu)}

(2.2.100)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\rho}\left(\bar{\Psi}^{\rho}\gamma^{(\mu}\Psi^{\nu)}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{(\mu}\left(\bar{\Psi}^{\nu)}\gamma^{\rho}\Psi_{\rho}\right)-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\,\nabla_{\rho}\left(\bar{\Psi}_{\sigma}\gamma^{\sigma}\Psi^{\rho}\right)\ .

The terms in the first line are the ones coming from the variation of the explicit vierbeins in (
2.2.10
), the second line is instead the contribution from the spin-connection in the covariant derivative. The proof of the symmetry and the covariant conservation of this tensor can be found in Appendix
B
. Starting from this general formula, the energy operator for the canonically normalized gravitino in FLRW spacetime, defined in eq. (
2.2.14
), results in

E_{\psi}=\int d^{3}x\sqrt{-g}\ T^{0}{}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{3}x\left\{\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\gamma}^{ijl}{\partial}_{j}\psi_{l}-am\bar{\psi}_{i}\bar{\gamma}^{ij}\psi_{j}\right\}\ .

(2.2.101)

In order to properly identify the transverse and longitudinal components, one has to go to momentum space via (
2.2.20
) and expand the field
\psi_{i,k}
according to the decomposition (
2.2.25
). The resulting energy operator is given by

E_{\psi}=\int d^{3}k\left[\mathcal{E}_{\textup{t}}+\mathcal{E}_{\theta}\right]\ ,

(2.2.102)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\textup{t}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{3}\left[\bar{\psi}_{j,k}^{\textup{t}}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)\psi_{j,k}^{\textup{t}}+am\bar{\psi}_{j,k}^{\textup{t}}\psi_{j,k}^{\textup{t}}\right]\ ,

(2.2.103)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\bar{\vartheta}_{k}\left[\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\bar{\gamma}}\right)D+am\right]\vartheta_{k}\ .

(2.2.104)

where the operator
D
is equal to

D=d_{\textup{R}}+id_{\textup{I}}\bar{\gamma}^{0}\equiv\frac{3m^{2}+H^{2}}{3\left(m^{2}+H^{2}\right)}+\frac{2mH}{3\left(m^{2}+H^{2}\right)}\bar{\gamma}_{0}\ ,

(2.2.105)

Thus, for transverse gravitino the energy operator and the instantaneous hamiltonian of eq. (
2.2.76
) match exactly, as anticipated previously. On the contrary, the resulting energy operator for the longitudinal gravitino — which agrees with the energy density given in
[
113
]
— does not coincide with the corresponding instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.76
). They have though the same structure: the latter is characterized by the sound speed parameter
C(\tau)
of eq. (
2.2.19
) and non-minimal time-dependent mass parameter
M
(
2.2.32
), the former by sound speed parameter
D\left(\tau\right)
of eq. (
2.2.105
) and minimal mass parameter
m
. This similarity allows one to perform the quantization and diagonalization of the longitudinal energy operator (
2.2.104
) in a simple way, adapting the steps performed for the instantaneous hamiltonian. The resulting diagonal energy of the longitudinal gravitino is

\mathcal{E}_{\vartheta}=\frac{\omega_{d}}{2}\sum_{r}\left(\hat{a}_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{r,\bm{k}}-\hat{a}_{r,\bm{k}}\hat{a}_{r,\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\right)\ ,

(2.2.106)

where the diagonal frequency is equal to

\displaystyle\omega_{d}^{2}=d_{s}^{2}k^{2}+a^{2}m^{2}\ ,

\displaystyle d_{s}^{2}=d_{\textup{R}}^{2}+d_{\textup{I}}^{2}=\frac{m^{2}+\nicefrac{{H^{2}}}{{9}}}{m^{2}+H^{2}}\ .

(2.2.107)

The Bogolyubov transformation between the diagonal ladder operators and the original ones in eq. (
2.2.79
) is

\begin{pmatrix}\hat{a}_{r,\bm{k}}\\
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{-r,-\bm{k}},\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{\bm{k}}&-y_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\\
y_{\bm{k}}&x_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{a}_{r,\bm{k}}\\
\tilde{a}_{-r,-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}x_{\bm{k}}&-y_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\\
y_{\bm{k}}&x_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{\bm{k}}&-\beta_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\\
\beta_{\bm{k}}&\alpha_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a_{r,\bm{k}}\\
a_{-r,-\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\ .

(2.2.108)

The Bogolyubov coefficients
x_{k}
and
y_{k}
are the energy analogue of the coefficients
\hat{\alpha}_{k}
and
\hat{\beta}_{k}
of eq. (
2.2.96
)-(
2.2.97
) for the instantaneous hamiltonian, and are
16
16

16

As for the coefficients in eq. (
2.2.97
), the Bogolyubov coefficients
x_{k}
and
y_{k}
in eq. (
2.2.109
) are defined up to an unphysical phase.

\displaystyle x_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}=-\frac{B_{k}^{\textup{D}}}{\omega_{d}-A_{k}^{\textup{D}}}y_{\bm{k}}\ ,

\displaystyle\lvert x_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}=\frac{\omega_{d}+A_{k}^{\textup{D}}}{2\omega_{d}}\ ,

\displaystyle\lvert y_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}=\frac{\omega_{d}-A_{k}^{\textup{D}}}{2\omega_{d}}\ ,

(2.2.109)

with

\displaystyle A_{k}^{\textup{D}}=

\displaystyle[c_{+}(k)c_{-}(k)]d_{\textup{R}}k+\left(\frac{c^{2}_{+}(k)-c^{2}_{-}(k)}{2}\right)am\ ,

(2.2.110)

\displaystyle B_{k}^{\textup{D}}=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{c^{2}_{+}(k)-c^{2}_{-}(k)}{2}\right)d_{\textup{R}}k-[c_{+}(k)c_{-}(k)]am-id_{\textup{I}}k\ .

(2.2.111)

On the difference between the two diagonal Fock spaces and the equivalence theorem

Therefore, as in the scalar field case, the diagonal energy operator and instantaneous hamiltonian are different, and are associated with distinct diagonal Fock spaces. The relation between the two is again a Bogolyubov transformation, that can be worked out explicitly, combining eq. (
2.2.96
)-(
2.2.97
) with eq. (
2.2.108
)-(
2.2.109
). The resulting transformation is
[
2
]

\begin{pmatrix}\hat{a}_{r,\bm{k}}\\
\hat{a}_{-r,-\bm{k}},^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{A}_{\bm{k}}&-\mathscr{B}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\\
\mathscr{B}_{\bm{k}}&\mathscr{A}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{b}_{r,\bm{k}}\\
\hat{b}_{-r,-\bm{k}},^{\dagger}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.112)

where the composite Bogolyubov coefficients

\displaystyle\mathscr{A}_{\bm{k}}=x_{\bm{k}}\hat{\alpha}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}+y_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\hat{\beta}_{\bm{k}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathscr{B}_{\bm{k}}=y_{\bm{k}}\hat{\alpha}_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}-x_{\bm{k}}^{\dagger}\hat{\beta}_{\bm{k}}\ .

(2.2.113)

are such that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathscr{A}_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[1-\frac{\left(C_{\textup{R}}d_{\textup{R}}+C_{\textup{I}}d_{\textup{I}}\right)}{\omega\omega_{d}}k^{2}+\frac{a^{2}mM}{\omega\omega_{d}}\right],

(2.2.114)

\displaystyle\lvert\mathscr{B}_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left[1+\frac{\left(C_{\textup{R}}d_{\textup{R}}+C_{\textup{I}}d_{\textup{I}}\right)}{\omega\omega_{d}}k^{2}-\frac{a^{2}mM}{\omega\omega_{d}}\right]\ .

Note that, once again, these formulas hold independently of the particular choice of coefficients
c_{\pm}
in eq. (
2.2.89
).

Equipped with this correspondence, we can address the physical consequences on the dynamics of the massive gravitino in FLRW. First of all, we go back to the "catastrophic" production of longitudinal gravitinos for vanishing sound speed in the orthogonal supergravity model (
2.2.1
), that was put forward in
[
69
]
and discussed here in Section
2.2.1
. Despite the conditions for the sound speed of eq. (
2.2.34
) to vanish in this particular model are in tension with the corresponding effective field theory approximation, physical processes associated with the produced particles before the breakdown — like for instance their backreaction on the geometry for small but non-vanishing sound speed — should have an unambiguous description. The gravitino overproduction discussed in
[
69
]
is computed by means of the instantaneous diagonal hamiltonian given in eq. (
2.2.99
), following the standard prescription of eq. (
2.2.40
). This hamiltonian density is indeed proportional to the frequency
\omega
of eq. (
2.2.98
), which directly depends on the sound speed
c_{s}
of eq. (
2.2.34
). However, we highlighted and proved in this section that describing physical processes in curved background with the instantaneous hamiltonian is misleading, since the associated ground state is not the state of minimal energy. We showed this explicitly in eq. (
2.2.74
) for the case of the scalar field, but it holds as long as there is a mismatch between the two Fock spaces. Taking therefore the non-ambiguous perspective of the energy-momentum tensor (
2.2.100
) we computed in
[
2
]
, one has that energy operator of the longitudinal gravitino (
2.2.109
) is proportional to the frequency
\omega_{d}
of eq. (
2.2.107
) rather than
\omega
, which means that it depends on the parameter
d_{s}^{2}
of eq. (
2.2.107
) rather than on the troublesome sound speed
c_{s}^{2}
. This means that the energy of the longitudinal gravitino is not associated with any divergence or non-unitary behavior in the limit
c_{s}\to 0

[
2
]
. Moreover, even if divergences are found at this level they should not be an obstacle a priori, since energy requires anyway renormalization, and clearly the renormalization procedure concerns the energy-momentum tensor.

In addition, the correspondence (
2.2.112
) between the diagonal Fock spaces highlights a further puzzling feature. As we discussed for the scalar field in eq. (
2.2.75
), the energy operator and the instantaneous hamiltonian are physically expected to align in the UV limit
k\to\infty
, where the effect of the coupling to gravity are suppressed. However, contrary to this expectation, this does not happen, since the off-diagonal Bogolyubov coefficient
\mathscr{B}_{\bm{k}}
of eq. (
2.2.112
) does not vanish in the UV limit
[
2
]
:

\lim_{k\to\infty}\lvert\mathscr{B}_{\bm{k}}\rvert^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+\frac{C_{\textup{R}}d_{\textup{R}}+C_{\textup{I}}d_{\textup{I}}}{c_{s}d_{s}}\right)\ .

(2.2.115)

This peculiar misalignment is specific of the spin-
\frac{3}{2}
field case and does not affect the particles of lower spins
[
2
]
.

The same problem can be seen from a second point of view, with respect to the gravitino-goldstino equivalence theorem (
2.1.50
)
[
54
,
55
,
56
]
. According to this theorem, the dynamics of the longitudinal gravitino should be described in terms of the goldstino in the limit of high momenta. In the case of the orthogonal supergravity model (
2.2.1
), one can check that the equivalence theorem is explicitly realized at the lagrangian level, without canonical normalization. For the longitudinal gravitino, this is given by the field
\theta_{k}\equiv\bar{\gamma}^{i}\psi_{i,k}
before the redefinition (
2.2.27
) is applied. The scalar field background of this model is defined by the Einstein equations (
2.2.9
), and in such a background the sound speed takes the form given in eq. (
2.2.34
). Writing the longitudinal gravitino field
\theta_{k}
in two-component notation as

\theta(t,\vec{k})=\begin{pmatrix}\chi_{\bm{k}}(t)\\
\bar{\chi}_{-\bm{k}}(t)\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(2.2.116)

the longitudinal gravitino lagrangian is found from eq. (
2.2.15
) to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\theta}=

\displaystyle\frac{a^{2}f^{2}}{2\vec{k}^{2}}\left\{\left(1+\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2f^{2}}\right)\frac{i}{2}\partial_{0}\chi_{\bm{k}}\sigma^{0}\bar{\chi}_{\bm{k}}+\left(1-\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2f^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{2}\chi_{\bm{k}}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\sigma}\right)\bar{\chi}_{\bm{k}}\right.+

(2.2.117)

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{g^{\prime}\,\dot{\phi}}{f^{2}}\chi_{\bm{k}}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\sigma}\right)\bar{\sigma}^{0}\chi_{-\bm{k}}+\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{f^{2}}-1\right)am+\left(\frac{3g^{\prime}\,\dot{\phi}}{f^{2}}\right)aH\right]\chi_{\bm{k}}\chi_{-\bm{k}}+h.c.\right\}\ .

On the goldstino side, the non-linear supersymmetry goldstino lagrangians were computed in
[
1
]
and reported in detail in Section
1.4
. The relevant one in this case is the quadratic sector of the orthogonal constraint lagrangian of eq. (
1.4.12
). Denoting with
G_{\bm{k}}
the goldstino in the same momentum space parameterization as
\chi_{\bm{k}}
, this lagrangian is found to be

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{G}=

\displaystyle\left(1+\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2f^{2}}\right)\frac{i}{2}\partial_{0}G_{\bm{k}}\sigma^{0}\bar{G}_{\bm{k}}+\left(1-\frac{\dot{\phi}^{2}}{2f^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{2}G_{\bm{k}}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\sigma}\right)\bar{G}_{\bm{k}}+

(2.2.118)

\displaystyle+i\frac{g^{\prime}\,\dot{\phi}}{f^{2}}G_{\bm{k}}\left(\vec{k}\cdot\vec{\sigma}\right)\bar{\sigma}^{0}G_{-\bm{k}}+h.c.\ .

Therefore, in the large momentum limit
k\gg am
and
k\gg aH
, the lagrangians (
2.2.117
) and (
2.2.118
) match via the identification

\chi_{\bm{k}}=\frac{2k}{af}G_{\bm{k}}\ ,

(2.2.119)

which suggests the following prescription for the equivalence theorem:

\psi_{\mu}\longrightarrow\frac{M_{\textup{P}}}{f}\partial_{\mu}G\ ,

(2.2.120)

along the lines of eq. (
2.1.50
).
17
17

17

Notice that during inflation the relation
f=\sqrt{3}mM_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}
is valid only on a flat space background.
However, the same alignment of the lagrangian is not realized at the level of the energy operators. The goldstino hamiltonian computed from the relative lagrangian (
2.2.118
) is in fact equal, in the UV limit, to the instantaneous hamiltonian of eq. (
2.2.76
), and therefore remains disconnected from the actual energy operator (
2.2.104
), according to eq. (
2.2.115
)
[
2
]
.

We still do not have a detailed understanding of the puzzling behavior of the massive gravitino in non-trivial, time-dependent backgrounds. This, once again, provides further motivation to stick to the stress-energy tensor operator (
2.2.104
) to describe the dynamics of the massive gravitino, in any limit.

2.2.4
Summary of results

In this section we analyzed the dynamics of the massive gravitino (
2.2.10
) in the cosmological scenario of the time-dependent FLRW spacetime. These models are embedded in supergravity as effective theories with nonlinearly realized supersymmetry, like the orthogonal supergravity model presented in eq. (
2.2.1
)
[
67
,
68
,
76
,
69
,
70
]
. In time-dependent backgrounds, the dynamics of the longitudinal gravitino — which is the goldstino of spontaneous supersymmetry breaking, absorbed through the super-Higgs mechanism, discussed in Section
2.1.2
— is governed by the sound-speed parameter defined in eq. (
2.2.19
)-(
2.2.31
)
[
76
,
69
,
70
]
. Depending on the specific background solution, this sound speed exposes two major inconsistencies related to the gravitino propagation. The first is the possibility for the gravitino to propagate with superluminal sound speed. We proved in
[
1
]
that this is a consequence of the ill-defined orthogonal constraint (
1.4.6
), which does not define reliable effective field theories. This constraint originates in fact from a higher-derivative theory in the UV, which turns into causality constraints like eq. (
2.2.35
). The issue disappears once the improved setup of eq. (
1.4.3
) proposed in
[
1
]
is employed. This yields a lagrangian analogous to the one in eq. (
2.2.1
) but free of causality problems, as shown in eq. (
2.2.37
). The second issue is more subtle and related to the phenomenon of gravitational particle production
[
119
,
120
,
121
,
125
,
113
,
114
,
116
,
115
,
117
,
118
,
77
,
69
,
136
,
135
,
133
,
122
,
123
]
. It was argued in
[
69
]
that a vanishing sound speed parameter during the background scalar field evolution is related to an unbounded production of gravitinos. More specifically, a vanishing sound speed allows gravitinos to be gravitationally produced with arbitrary large momentum, which is clearly a non-unitary process.

This inconsistent behavior signals that the theory breaks down once the gravitino is quantized. This breakdown can be related only partially to the effective field theory setup under consideration, and therefore motivated us to look more closely into the gravitational particle production mechanism. This is usually computed based on the ground state and the Fock space of what we called instantaneous hamiltonian, which is the hamiltonian for the canonically normalized fields
[
120
,
125
,
121
,
119
,
122
,
123
]
. However, despite describing correctly the field propagation, the instantaneous hamiltonian does not represent the physical energy of the system when placed in curved spacetime. This is rather given by the stress-energy tensor, sourcing the metric field in the Einstein equations. In fact, the time-dependent rescaling that one performs to canonically normalize the fields breaks their transformation properties under diffeomorphism, so that the corresponding instantaneous hamiltonian is not connected in a simple way to the energy operator encoded in the stress-energy tensor
[
128
,
129
,
130
,
131
]
.

In
[
2
]
we investigate the consequences of this difference between the Fock spaces of the instantaneous hamiltonian and the energy-momentum tensor, parameterizing it in terms of a Bogolyubov transformation. In Section
2.2.2
we have reported explicitly this analysis for the simple case of the real massive scalar field. The two relative Fock spaces are indeed different — see eq. (
2.2.71
)-(
2.2.72
) — but they align in the UV limit of large momentum. This is physically consistent, since the gravitational effects are suppressed in such a limit. On the contrary, the analysis on the massive gravitino is more involved and yields a further puzzle. The energy operator (
2.2.104
), computed from the general stress-energy tensor (
2.2.100
), and the instantaneous hamiltonian (
2.2.76
) are related through the Bogolyubov transformation (
2.2.112
)–(
2.2.114
). However, this transformation does not trivialize in the limit of high momenta, meaning that the operators are completely disconnected, even in the limit in which gravity should be suppressed. The counterpart of this puzzling behavior is related to the gravitino equivalence theorem (
2.1.50
)
[
54
,
56
,
55
]
, which we prove at the lagrangian level in eq. (
2.2.116
) and eq. (
2.2.118
), but it is clearly not realized at the level of the energy operators, since the goldstino hamiltonian corresponds to the instantaneous hamiltonian of the longitudinal gravitino. On the other hand, a further consequence of the difference between the two Fock spaces is that the actual energy operator (
2.2.104
) of the longitudinal gravitino is not associated with any specific behavior in the vanishing sound speed limit discussed in
[
69
]
. Moreover, the energy in quantum field theory usually requires renormalization, which must be taken into account in the presence of divergences in the energy operator of the gravitino.

The underlying reason for this mismatch in the gravitino case remains unknown, and we plan on further investigating it in future work. Nevertheless, it clearly suggests that one should rely on the gravitino stress-energy tensor (
2.2.100
) for any physical application, even in setups that are not expected to be sensitive to gravitational coupling. More generally, the results of
[
2
]
point to the need for a re-analysis of non-thermal gravitino production in cosmology.

2.3
Off-shell supergravity and supercurrent superfields

We now leave the framework of spontaneous supersymmetry breaking and return to the pure supergravity multiplet
\{g_{\mu\nu},\psi_{\mu}\}
of eq. (
2.1.15
)-(
2.1.14
). In Chapter
1
we saw that rigid supersymmetry can be conveniently formulated in terms of off-shell degrees of freedom. This description is particularly natural from the superspace point of view
18
18

18

The superspace formalism can indeed be extended to the local supersymmetry case and used to construct supergravity theories. We will not discuss this approach further in the manuscript, and refer for example to
[
137
,
138
,
139
,
140
,
34
]
for more information.
of Section
1.2
. In a similar fashion, supergravity also admits different off-shell formulations
[
141
,
142
,
140
,
143
]
, which we discuss in this section. Although not as convenient as in the rigid case, these formulations have many interesting features and applications. In particular, as will become clear early in this section, they allow one to investigate the leading-order coupling to supergravity of rigid theories (see for example
[
144
,
145
,
146
]
). This is the setup and perspective adopted in the paper
[
4
]
, which we will present in detail in Section
2.4
.

To understand how off-shell formulations of the pure supergravity multiplet
\left\{e_{\mu}{}^{a},\psi_{\mu}\right\}
(
2.1.15
) can be constructed, we go back to the Noether procedure of eq. (
2.1.3
)–(
2.1.6
). In particular, we saw in eq. (
2.1.3
) that the current of supersymmetry
\Xi^{\mu}
associated with a given multiplet transforms under supersymmetry into the stress-energy tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
. This is a general relation imposed by the Super-Poincaré algebra (
1.1.7
), which has the major consequence that the current of supersymmetry and the stress-energy tensor are themselves part of a supersymmetric multiplet, the so-called supercurrent superfield
[
85
,
143
,
147
,
145
]
. As shown in the original paper by Ferrara and Zumino
[
85
]
, the supercurrent multiplet is a vector superfield
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
that satisfies the so-called linear multiplet condition, which in superspace language is

D^{2}\mathcal{S}^{\mu}=0\ ,

(2.3.1)

along with an additional conservation-like equation that holds on-shell and determines its components. This equation can take different forms, hence leading to multiple supercurrent superfields
[
85
,
143
,
147
,
145
]
. They all contain the supersymmetry current and the stress-energy tensor, but differ in the additional superpartners that complete the multiplet.

Then, going back to the coupling to supergravity, which at leading order is given by eq. (
2.1.3
)–(
2.1.6
), the supercurrent superfield is naturally interpreted as the multiplet of currents of the supergravity gauge fields. Thus, following this path, the supercurrent superfield can be used to construct an off-shell extension of the pure supergravity multiplet (
2.1.15
): as the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
and the gravitino
\psi_{\mu}
couple respectively to the stress-energy tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
and the current of supersymmetry
\Xi^{\mu}
, the additional off-shell degrees of freedom for the supergravity multiplet can be identified based on the structure of the additional elements of the supercurrent multiplet
[
141
,
142
,
143
,
140
]
.

Let us now present the different supercurrent superfields and the corresponding off-shell formulation of supergravity they define.

2.3.1
The Ferrara–Zumino supercurrent and old-minimal off-shell supergravity

We begin with the so-called Ferrara–Zumino multiplet
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{FZ}}

[
85
]
. It is defined by the superspace equation

\left(\sigma_{\mu}\right)_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\overline{D}^{\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{S}_{\textup{FZ}}^{\mu}=D_{\alpha}X\ ,

(2.3.2)

where
X
is a chiral multiplet
\overline{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}X=0
, as in eq. (
1.2.11
). This equation fixes the components of the Ferrara–Zumino supercurrent superfield
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{FZ}}
to be

\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{FZ}}=\left\{J^{\mu},\Xi^{\mu},T^{\mu\nu},x\right\}\ ,

(2.3.3)

where
J^{\mu}
is a non-conserved axial current and
x=A+iB
is a complex scalar field. Together with the stress-energy tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
, they account for 12 bosonic degrees of freedom, which match the 12 fermionic ones of the supersymmetry current
\Xi^{\mu}
. The chiral superfield
X
contains the complex scalar
x
as lowest component, together with the divergence
\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}
and the traces
T^{\mu}{}_{\mu}
and
\left(\sigma^{\mu}\Xi_{\mu}\right)_{\alpha}
.
19
19

19

For simplicity of notation, we use the same name to denote the supersymmetry current both in 2-component and 4-component notation.
In the presence of the full superconformal symmetry, the superfield
X
vanishes and
J^{\mu}
becomes the superconformal R-symmetry current
[
85
]
. The supersymmetry transformations characterizing the
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{FZ}}
superfield are

\displaystyle\delta J^{\mu}=-i\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{5}\Xi^{\mu}\ ,

(2.3.4)

\displaystyle\delta\Xi^{\mu}=T^{\mu\nu}\gamma_{\nu}\epsilon+\frac{1}{8}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}J_{\sigma}\gamma_{\nu}\epsilon+\frac{i}{4}\partial_{\rho}J^{\mu}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{\rho}\epsilon+\frac{i}{4}\partial^{\mu}\left(A-iB\gamma_{5}\right)\gamma_{5}\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\delta T^{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{(\mu}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\Xi_{\nu)}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta x=2i\bar{\epsilon}_{R}\gamma^{\rho}\Xi_{\rho L}\ .

This multiplet was used to construct the first off-shell formulation of pure supergravity, known as old-minimal
[
141
,
142
]
. The additional off-shell fields of this old-minimal supergravity multiplet are an axial vector
V_{\mu}
and a complex scalar
Z=M+iN
. These couple respectively to the current
J^{\mu}
and the complex scalar
x
of the Ferrara–Zumino supercurrent
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{FZ}}
. In terms of off-shell degrees of freedom, the bosonic ones are 6 from the graviton, 4 from the axial vector
V_{\mu}
and two from the complex scalar
Z
, which match the 12 off-shell fermionic degrees of freedom of the gravitino. The linearised supersymmetry transformations of this old-minimal off-shell supergravity multiplet are

\displaystyle\delta g_{\mu\nu}=\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{(\mu}\psi_{\nu)}\ ,

(2.3.5)

\displaystyle\delta\psi_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\epsilon-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\nu}g_{\rho\mu}\gamma^{\nu\rho}\epsilon-\frac{1}{6}\left(M+iN\gamma_{5}\right)\gamma_{\mu}\epsilon+\frac{i}{2}\left(V_{\mu}-\frac{1}{3}\gamma_{\mu}\not{V}\right)\gamma_{5}\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\delta V_{\mu}=\frac{i}{4}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{5}\left(\gamma_{\mu}{}^{\rho\sigma}-4\delta_{\mu}{}^{[\rho}\gamma^{\sigma]}\right)\partial_{\rho}\psi_{\sigma}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta Z=\bar{\epsilon}_{R}\gamma^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\psi_{\nu R}\ ,

and the full nonlinear lagrangian is given by
[
141
,
142
]

e^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\textup{OM}}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{SUGRA}}+\frac{1}{3}\left(\lvert Z\rvert^{2}+V_{\mu}V^{\mu}\right)\ ,

(2.3.6)

where
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{sugra}}
is the pure supergravity action of eq. (
2.1.14
).

An example of multiplet that can be coupled to supergravity in this old-minimal off-shell formulation through the Ferrara–Zumino supercurrent (
2.3.3
) is the Wess–Zumino model of eq. (
1.2.32
)-(
1.2.33
), which is the case studied in the original Ferrara–Zumino paper
[
85
]
.

2.3.2
The
R
-multiplet and new-minimal off-shell supergravity

A second type of supercurrent superfield can be defined for theories with a conserved R-symmetry. This multiplet contains the R-symmetry current
J^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}
as lowest component and it is then called the R-multiplet
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}
. Its characteristic equation is
[
143
,
145
]

\left(\sigma_{\mu}\right)_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\overline{D}^{\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}=\chi_{\alpha}\ ,

(2.3.7)

where the superfield
\chi_{\alpha}
is such that

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\chi_{\alpha}=0\ ,

\displaystyle D^{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}=\overline{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\chi}^{\dot{\alpha}}\ ,

(2.3.8)

namely it is a chiral superfield with a field strength-like structure. These two equations imply that
\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}=0
, which means that the components of the R-multiplet are conserved currents. These are fixed by (
2.3.7
)-(
2.3.8
) to be

\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}=\left\{J^{\mu}_{\textup{R}},\Xi^{\mu},T^{\mu\nu},X^{\mu\nu}\right\}\ ,

(2.3.9)

where
J^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}
is indeed the R-symmetry current and
X^{\mu\nu}
is a totally antisymmetric tensor. This field
X^{\mu\nu}
can be written as the dual of a curl:

X^{\mu\nu}=\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}X_{\sigma}\ ,

(2.3.10)

which implies that it is trivially conserved and hence ensures the correct matching of bosonic and fermionic degrees of freedom.
The supersymmetry transformation rules of the R-multiplet (
2.3.9
) are
[
143
]

\displaystyle\delta J_{\mu}=-i\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{5}\Xi_{\mu}\ ,

(2.3.11)

\displaystyle\delta\Xi_{\mu}=T_{\mu\nu}\gamma^{\nu}\epsilon+X_{\mu\nu}\gamma^{\nu}\epsilon+\frac{1}{8}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial^{\rho}J^{\sigma}\gamma^{\nu}\epsilon+\frac{i}{4}\partial_{\rho}J_{\mu}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{\rho}\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\delta T_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{1}{4}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{(\mu}{}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}\Xi_{\nu)}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta X_{\mu\nu}=-\frac{i}{8}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{\rho}\gamma^{\lambda}\partial^{\sigma}\Xi_{\lambda}\ .

This R-multiplet of currents defines an alternative off-shell formulation of supergravity, called new-minimal, in which the supergravity multiplet auxiliary fields are now an axial vector field
V_{\mu}
and an antisymmetric tensor field
C_{\mu\nu}
of field strength
C^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\partial_{\nu}C_{\rho\sigma}
. The linearised supersymmetry transformations of this new-minimal off-shell supergravity multiplet are given by
[
143
]

\displaystyle\delta g_{\mu\nu}=\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{(\mu}\psi_{\nu)}\ ,

(2.3.12)

\displaystyle\delta\psi_{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\epsilon-\frac{1}{4}\partial_{\nu}g_{\rho\mu}\gamma^{\nu\rho}\epsilon+i\gamma_{5}V_{\mu}\epsilon-\frac{i}{4}\gamma_{\mu}G_{\nu}\gamma_{5}\gamma^{\nu}\epsilon\ ,

\displaystyle\delta V_{\mu}=-\frac{i}{4}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{5}\gamma_{\mu}\gamma^{\nu\rho}\partial_{\nu}\psi_{\rho}+\frac{3}{8}\varepsilon_{\mu\nu}{}^{\rho\sigma}\bar{\epsilon}\gamma^{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\psi_{\sigma}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta C_{\mu\nu}=\bar{\epsilon}\gamma_{[\mu}\psi_{\nu]}\ .

and the full nonlinear off-shell lagrangian of pure supergravity is equal to
[
143
]

e^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\textup{NM}}=\mathcal{L}_{\textup{SUGRA}}-3V_{\mu}V^{\mu}+2V^{\mu}C_{\mu}\ ,

(2.3.13)

where again
\mathcal{L}_{\textup{SUGRA}}
is the lagrangian given in eq. (
2.1.14
).

We employed explicitly this new-minimal setup in the original work
[
4
]
, where we studied the coupling to supergravity of the massive spin-2 field. We will discuss in detail the results of this paper in Section
2.4
.

2.3.3
The non-minimal formulations

The two previous supercurrent multiplets
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{FZ}}
(
2.3.2
)-(
2.3.3
) and
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}
(
2.3.7
)-(
2.3.9
) admit a generalization into a further supercurrent superfield
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
interpolating between them. There are in fact instances in which neither the R-multiplet nor the Ferrara–Zumino multiplet can be defined consistently
[
145
]
. This concerns in particular the Ferrara–Zumino supercurrent, which is not well-defined in the presence of Fayet–Iliopoulos terms
[
148
]
and for specific classes of Kähler geometries
[
145
]
. In cases of this type, one has to resort to such a generalized multiplet
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
(
2.3.14
). This generalized supercurrent superfield
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
is defined by the set of equations
[
145
]

\displaystyle\left(\sigma_{\mu}\right)_{\alpha\dot{\alpha}}\overline{D}^{\dot{\alpha}}\mathcal{S}^{\mu}=D_{\alpha}X+\chi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle,

(2.3.14)

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}X=\overline{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\chi_{\alpha}=0

\displaystyle,

\displaystyle D^{\alpha}\chi_{\alpha}=\overline{D}_{\dot{\alpha}}\bar{\chi}^{\dot{\alpha}}

\displaystyle,

and contains the fields of
