]
for the interactions of the massive spin-2 field with gravity. However, this is not the cutoff scale of the whole theory, because the other helicty states scattering actually exhibit worse behavior. More specifically, this concerns the helicity states
\sigma=\pm 1
, whose scattering with the helicity-0 ones grows as
s^{4}
both in the Regge and high-energy limit
[
4
]
. For instance, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(1,1,0,0)

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{384m^{6}s(s-4m^{2})}\times

(2.4.73)

\displaystyle\times\left[s^{6}\left(5-\cos^{2}\theta\right)-m^{2}s^{5}\left(49-9\cos^{4}\theta\right)+2m^{4}s^{4}\left(217-113\cos^{2}\theta+26\cos^{4}\theta\right)\right.

\displaystyle-4m^{6}s^{3}\left(480-265\cos^{2}\theta+69\cos^{4}\theta\right)+32m^{8}s^{2}\left(103+96\cos^{2}\theta-9\cos^{4}\theta\right)

\displaystyle\left.-64m^{10}s\left(56+119\cos^{2}\theta+\cos^{4}\theta\right)-3072m^{12}\cos(2\theta)\right]\ ,

for which one can check that
\mathcal{A}\left(1,1,0,0\right)\sim s^{4}
in both limits.
30
30

30

In addition to the
\mathcal{A}\left(1,1,0,0\right)
amplitude of eq. (
2.4.73
), other amplitudes with the same exact behavior are
\mathcal{A}(0,0,\pm 1,\pm 1)
,
\mathcal{A}(\pm 1,\pm 1,0,0)
,
\mathcal{A}(0,\pm 1,\pm 1,0)
,
\mathcal{A}(\pm 1,0,0,\pm 1)
,
\mathcal{A}(\pm 1,0,\mp 1,0)
and
\mathcal{A}(0,\pm 1,0,\mp 1)
.
Therefore, following
[
153
,
154
,
155
,
156
,
157
,
158
,
159
]
and eq. (
2.4.2
), the coupling of the massive spin-2 field to gravity defined by the new-minimal supercurrent
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
of eq. (
2.4.41
) is associated with the cutoff scale
\Lambda_{4}
. This is slightly improved with respect to generic behavior in energy of the interactions between gravity and the massive spin-2 field, which is typically
\Lambda_{5}

[
153
,
154
,
155
,
156
]
, thanks to local supersymmetry invaraiance. However, it is necessary to include self-interactions of the massive spin-2 field in order to raise the cutoff of the theory up to the
\Lambda_{3}
threshold
[
157
]
.

Let us now deform this new-minimal setup and include the couplings to non-minimal off-shell supergravity (
2.4.28
)-(
2.4.54
). In this formulation, the stress-energy tensor is modified according to eq. (
2.4.52
),
i.e.
in terms of a non-minimal coupling to the Ricci scalar
R
to the composite operator
U=\mathcal{U}|_{\vartheta=0}
of the non-minimal improvement of eq. (
2.4.28
). This operator is, in general, quadratic in fields and contains up to two derivatives. Therefore, the most general operator of this type that contributes nontrivially to the
2\to 2
scattering amplitude of the massive spin-2 fields
h_{\mu\nu}
has the form

T^{\mu\nu}_{U}=\left(\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}-\eta^{\mu\nu}\Box\right)\left(c_{1}h^{\rho\sigma}h_{\rho\sigma}+c_{2}\partial_{\lambda}h^{\rho\sigma}\partial^{\lambda}h_{\rho\sigma}+c_{3}\partial_{\rho}\partial_{\sigma}(h^{\rho\lambda}h^{\sigma}{}_{\lambda})\right)\ .

(2.4.74)

One can verify that this deformation worsens the growth of the
2\to 2
scattering amplitude with energy, causing it to scale as
\mathcal{A}(s,t)\sim s^{5}
or higher in the large
s
limit at fixed angle.

Hence, the supersymmetric coupling to gravity of the massive spin-2 in the new-minimal setup, defined by the supercurrent
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
of eq. (
2.4.41
) and reported in eq. (
2.4.62
), proves to be the one with best behavior in the energy growth of the scattering amplitude, so that the associated effective field theory is the one defined with the highest degree of reliability. All the other couplings of the spin-2 field to gravity in the non-minimal off-shell setup are associated with lower cutoff scales, as just showed. A priori, one could consider non-minimal deformations which do not modify the massive spin-2 scattering amplitude which we are analyzing. This is achieved by any operator
U
that does not contain any of the terms appearing in eq. (
2.4.74
), but involves only the rest of the fields of the massive multiplet (
2.4.3
). However, although we did not compute them explicitly, it is reasonable to expect that, by supersymmetry, also the elastic scattering amplitudes involving the other massive gauge fields, the superpartners of
h_{\mu\nu}
in eq. (
2.4.3
), should scale in the same way, so that any non-minimal deformation
U
(
2.4.52
)-(
2.4.54
) would inevitably raise the cutoff of the theory.

From this perspective, the theory defined by the supercurrent superfield
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
in eq. (
2.4.41
) is therefore not only the unique coupling to new-minimal off-shell supergravity but is also the unique coupling to any off-shell supergravity with improved cutoff
\Lambda_{4}
at tree-level instead of
\Lambda_{5}
.

2.4.5
On the relation between the unique coupling to new-minimal supergravity and string theory

The main result of
[
4
]
we have been discussing in this section is that the coupling of the massive spin-2 multiplet in the unitary gauge (
2.4.4
) to off-shell supergravity is unique at leading-order in the Planck Mass. This is in particular a coupling to new-minimal off-shell supergravity (
2.3.12
), given by the supercurrent superfield
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
of eq. (
2.4.41
), and contains non-minimal, higher-derivative couplings to the massless supergravity fields, as in eq. (
2.4.43
)-(
2.4.49
) and (
2.4.44
)-(
2.4.50
). This non-minimal coupling term involves all the fields in the massive spin-2 supermultiplet and contains in particular the massive spin-1 higher-derivative operator

-\frac{3}{16m^{2}}R_{\mu\sigma\nu\rho}F^{\mu\sigma}F^{\nu\rho}\ .

(2.4.75)

It was shown in
[
177
]
that in the case of a massless gauge field, this operator leads to causality violations in
D>4
dimensions. We expect this result to hold also in the present four-dimensional setup involving a massive gauge field if the mass is such that
m\ll M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}
. In
[
177
]
it is also argued that acausality problems of this type can be resolved through the introduction of a tower of higher-spin fields of increasing mass. This resembles clearly the typical Regge tower of states that occurs in string theory, so that effectively proving that the operator (
2.4.75
) does lead to violations of causality would hint towards a UV completion of the string theory type directly from a bottom-up point of view.

Actually, the coupling defined by
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
(
2.4.41
) has further connections with superstring theory. Expanding the metric field around Minkowski space as
g_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}+2\kappa g_{\mu\nu}^{\scalebox{0.6}{(1)}}
, the on-shell three-point coupling of two massive spin-2 fields and a massless graviton, given previously in eq. (
2.4.62
), can be written in the form

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{2}

\displaystyle\left[g_{\mu\nu}^{\scalebox{0.6}{(1)}}\left(\partial^{\mu}h^{\rho\sigma}\partial^{\nu}h_{\rho\sigma}-4\partial^{\mu}h^{\rho\sigma}\partial_{\rho}h^{\nu}{}_{\sigma}\right)+h^{\mu\nu}\left(\partial_{\mu}g_{\rho\sigma}^{\scalebox{0.6}{(1)}}\partial_{\nu}h^{\rho\sigma}-\partial_{\rho}g_{\mu\sigma}^{\scalebox{0.6}{(1)}}\partial_{\nu}h^{\rho\sigma}\right)\right.

(2.4.76)

\displaystyle+\left.\partial_{\mu}\partial^{\rho}g_{\nu\rho}^{\scalebox{0.6}{(1)}}\left(2h^{\mu}{}_{\sigma}h^{\nu\sigma}-\tfrac{1}{4}\eta^{\mu\nu}h_{\sigma\lambda}h^{\sigma\lambda}\right)\right]\;.

This term exactly matches the coupling of a massive open string state of mass
m=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}
to the graviton in superstring theory
[
178
]
, as well as in bosonic string theory
[
179
]
.
31
31

31

The massive open string theory states also have self-interactions, which are, however, beyond the limit of the analysis carried out in
[
4
]
.
Therefore, the coupling
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
(
2.4.41
) has a natural interpretation in superstring theory as the coupling to gravity of a non-trivial spin-2 Regge excitation of the string. This is a nontrivial top-down match of the UV completion of the theory based on the causality violation related to the operator of eq. (
2.4.75
). Moreover, string theory brings a whole Regge tower of particles of mass of the same order
\sqrt{\frac{n}{\alpha^{\prime}}}
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and thus the
\Lambda_{3}
threshold for the interactions of the massive spin-2 field and gravity is far beyond the scale of new physics for
m\ll M_{\scalebox{0.6}{P}}
, according to this picture.

Starting from type I superstring theory,
32
32

32

A general introduction to string theory is given in Chapter
3
.
a non-BPS massive multiplet for the massive spin-2 field is obtained by compactifying the theory on
T^{6}
from the first oscillator mode of the open string, of mass
m=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}

[
178
]
. In
\mathcal{N}=4
supergravity, the massive spin-2 field is contained in a long multiplet, describing
128+128
degrees of freedom. On the other hand, the Kaluza–Klein reduction along
T^{6}
of the ten-dimensional massless fields associated with the closed string yields the massive spin-2 field within
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS spin-2 multiplets, comprising
24+24
degrees of freedom. The two compactifications lead to the same tree-level interactions once an appropriate orbifold projection is included, breaking supersymmetry down to
\mathcal{N}=1
in four dimensions. One can then take the bottom-up perspective of the unique coupling found in
[
4
]
and extrapolate the connection with extended supersymmetry in higher dimensions. The supercurrent
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
of eq. (
2.4.41
) describes the unique coupling to supergravity of the massive spin-2 multiplet of the long type, like the one arising from the first string oscillator mode
[
178
]
. By supersymmetry, we also expect the gravitational coupling of the
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS multiplet to be unique and equal to the Kaluza–Klein one, since it is associated with the only nontrivial deformation of the massless supersymmetry algebra discussed in Section
2.4.3
. This picture fits within the swampland infinite distance paradigm
[
180
]
. For an asymptotic point in the moduli space of vacua in which the mass of the
\frac{1}{2}
-BPS spin-2 multiplet vanishes, we must get an infinite tower of massless spin-2 multiplets that will be interpreted as a decompactification limit. On the other hand, an asymptotic point in moduli space where the mass of a long multiplet vanishes will lead to an infinite tower of higher-spin fields becoming massless, reproducing the tensionless limit of a perturbative string theory.

This picture is in agreement with the string lamppost principle, proposing that all theories in the quantum gravity landscape are realized as string theories
[
181
,
182
,
183
,
184
,
185
]
. Our results in
[
4
]
actually provide evidence in favor of this principle within a four-dimensional setup and minimal supersymmetry.

2.4.6
Summary of results

Let us conclude by summarizing the results of
[
4
]
presented in this section. In this work we investigate the couplings to supergravity of the massive spin-2 supermultiplet (
2.4.4
). We do so by means of the formalism of the supercurrent superfields, which encode the leading-order couplings to off-shell supergravity, as discussed in Section
2.3
. We construct this multiplet explicitly for the massive multiplet (
2.4.4
) and we find that the essential requirements of invariance under supersymmetry and general diffeomorphisms, imposed in full generality through the superfield improvement of eq. (
2.4.28
), yield a unique class of couplings to off-shell supergravity. The core, fundamental element of this set of couplings is the supercurrent
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
of eq. (
2.4.41
), which is the unique consistent coupling to new-minimal off-shell supergravity, with the highest possible cutoff scale
\Lambda_{4}
. It is characterized by non-minimal, higher-derivative couplings to the massless supergravity fields. Extensions of this coupling can be obtained by deforming the new-minimal setup associated to
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
to a non-minimal off-shell formulation of supergravity, by means of the
\mathcal{U}
-type improvement of eq. (
2.4.28
). These additional theories only differ by non-minimal couplings to the Ricci scalar
R
and the gravitino field strength
\rho_{\mu\nu}
, as described in eq. (
2.4.52
)-(
2.4.54
). Moreover, all couplings to non-minimal supergravity have a lower cutoff scale than the new-minimal one defined by
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
.

We also remark that this class of couplings is a genuine alternative to the Kaluza–Klein theory arising from pure 5D supergravity on
S^{1}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. As we argue in Section
2.4.3
, this theory does not fall within the framework considered in
[
4
]
, since working with the massive spin-2 supermultiplet in the unitary gauge of the Stückelberg symmetry, as the one in eq. (
2.4.4
), leads to deformed supersymmetry transformations of the massless fields in the Kaluza–Klein reduction, so that they are not described by any of the off-shell supergravities considered in this analysis. Moreover, the unique new-minimal coupling defined by the supercurrent
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
of eq. (
2.4.41
) has a natural interpretation in superstring theory, as it reproduces exactly, from the bottom-up, the coupling of an open string state of mass
\frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha^{\prime}}}
to the graviton
[
178
]
.

Further questions that we plan on investigating in the future are the following. First, the presence of higher-derivative operators, as in eq. (
2.4.46
) and (
2.4.54
), require checking their compatibility with unitarity and causality, which is a further and strong constraint on the spectrum of allowed such couplings. In particular, this concerns the massive spin-1 operator of eq. (
2.4.75
). If this operator were found to be the source of causality violations along the lines of
[
177
]
, it would provide even stronger motivation for a string-like UV completion, grounded in the effective field theory setup itself. On the other hand, it would be interesting to investigate even higher-derivative extensions of the theories determined in
[
4
]
, which are restricted by construction to the 4-derivatives level. This could be done by properly extending the ansatz (
2.4.36
) with higher-derivative axial improvements, starting for example from the 6-derivative case. This would be an interesting test of the uniqueness property of the new-minimal coupling
\mathcal{S}^{\mu}
. Higher-derivative deformations could in fact lead, on-shell, to modifications of the single 4-derivative consistent solution of eq. (
2.4.40
), or rather be also constrained to a unique solution, pointing in the direction of a full perturbative resummation of the theory. Finally, the study of the supersymmetric couplings involving a deformation of the massless supersymmetry algebra to prove the uniqueness of the Kaluza–Klein deformation discussed in Section
2.4.3
is a further subject of future investigations.

Chapter 3
String theory and orientifolds

The last chapter of the present manuscript is devoted to string theory. The main focus will be on orientifold projections and the string vacuum amplitudes in the presence of the non-dynamical objects known as orientifold planes. In particular, we will present the novel orientifold construction that we put forward in
[
3
]
. The main properties of the resulting string theory are that it lives in nine dimensions, has no open-string background sector, does not preserve supersymmetry, and that the orientifold planes couple only to the twisted states in the vacuum amplitudes. Because of this feature, we refer to them as
twisted O-planes
. Moreover, we argue that this string theory is invariant under S-duality.

The content of the chapter is organized as follows. We provide a general review of the bosonic string in Section
3.1
and superstring theory in Section
3.2
. We discuss the dynamics of these theories, their light-cone quantization, the resulting spectra of excitations, and, in particular, we describe the formalism of the string vacuum amplitudes and their physical interpretation. Orientifold projections are introduced in Section
3.2.5
, while in Section
3.3
we analyze in detail the orientifold compactifications of the type IIB string in nine dimensions. These are the three Scherk–Schwarz orientifolds previously known in the literature, and the supersymmetric orientifold of Dabholkar and Park. In Section
3.4
we present the detailed construction of the new orientifold of
[
3
]
. This includes its definition, its interpretation in the supergravity limit, and the computation of the one-loop effective potential and of the D-brane spectrum. We also provide evidence that the resulting nine-dimensional string theory is invariant under S-duality and argue for its realization as an F-theory compactification.

In addition to the standard books
[
186
,
187
,
188
,
189
,
190
]
, the introductory part of this chapter is mostly based on the reviews
[
191
,
192
,
193
]
.

3.1
The bosonic string

Strings are one-dimensional objects, arising as the generalization of the concept of point-particle to extended dimensions. Their basic description follows from this analogy. The propagation of strings in spacetime spans a 2-dimensional surface, known as the
worldsheet
, embedded in spacetime. It is parameterized by two coordinates
\xi^{a}=(\tau,\sigma)
, where
\tau\in(-\infty,+\infty)
is the proper time, as for point-particles, and
\sigma
describes instead the specific point along the string. Strings can be either closed or open. In the former case, one has
\sigma\in(0,2\pi)
with proper periodicity condition
\sigma=0\simeq\sigma=2\pi
. In the latter, one has
\sigma\in(0,\pi)
, corresponding to two boundary points. The relativistic action describing this worldsheet evolution has the natural form

S=-T\int dA=-T\int d^{2}\xi\sqrt{-\gamma}\ ,

(3.1.1)

where
T
is the string tension — namely, the string analogue of the mass of a particle — and
dA
is the infinitesimal worldsheet area element, which is written in terms of the induced metric on the worldsheet
\gamma_{ab}
. The position of the string in spacetime is given by the coordinates
X^{\mu}=X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
, known as the
target space
coordinates. Calling
G_{\mu\nu}
the target space metric — which we take to be of general dimensions
D
— the induced metric on the worldsheet is equal to

\gamma_{ab}=\frac{\partial X^{\mu}}{\partial\xi^{a}}\frac{\partial X^{\nu}}{\partial\xi^{b}}G_{\mu\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}\dot{X}^{\mu}\dot{X}_{\mu}&\dot{X}^{\mu}{X^{\prime}}_{\mu}\\
{X^{\prime}}^{\mu}\dot{X}_{\mu}&{X^{\prime}}^{\mu}{X^{\prime}}_{\mu}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(3.1.2)

where we employed the standard notation
\dot{X}^{\mu}\equiv\partial_{\tau}X^{\mu}
and
{X^{\prime}}^{\mu}\equiv\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}
. With this formula, the action (
3.1.1
) becomes

S_{\textup{NG}}=-T\int d^{2}\xi\sqrt{\left(\dot{X}\cdot{X^{\prime}}\right)^{2}-\left(\dot{X}\cdot\dot{X}\right)\left({X^{\prime}}\cdot{X^{\prime}}\right)}\ ,

(3.1.3)

which is known as the Nambu–Goto action. As in the case of the relativistic particle, an alternative action can be written by introducing an auxiliary metric
g_{ab}
on the worldsheet. This is known as the Polyakov action, and it is equal to

S_{\textup{P}}=-\frac{T}{2}\int d^{2}\xi\sqrt{-g}\,\,g^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}\ .

(3.1.4)

The variation of the Polyakov action with respect to its auxiliary worldsheet metric yields a vanishing condition for its associated stress-energy tensor:

T_{ab}=\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X_{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}\left(g^{cd}\partial_{c}X^{\mu}\partial_{d}X_{\mu}\right)=0\ .

(3.1.5)

This constraint is explicitly solved by
g_{ab}=f\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X_{\mu}
, with
f
being a proportionality constant. When plugged back into the Polyakov action (
3.1.4
), this solution gives back the Nambu–Goto action (
3.1.3
). On the other hand, the Polyakov action is best suited to discuss the dynamics and especially the quantization of the strings, in which the vanishing condition of the stress-energy tensor (
3.1.5
) plays a central role.

3.1.1
The string dynamics

We thus focus on the Polyakov action (
3.1.4
). We observe that this action enjoys a Weyl symmetry
g_{ab}\to e^{\omega}g_{ab}
. This is reflected in the stress-energy tensor (
3.1.5
), which is traceless:
T^{a}{}_{a}=0
. Exploiting this symmetry, along with the reparameterization and Lorentz invariance of the action (
3.1.4
), the worldsheet metric can be set to the two-dimensional Minkowski metric
g_{ab}=\eta_{ab}
, in what is known as the conformal gauge. Then, the string action is conveniently parameterized in terms of the light-cone coordinates

\displaystyle\xi^{\pm}=\tau\pm\sigma\ ,

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-d\xi^{+}d\xi^{-}\ ,

\displaystyle\eta=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{1}{2}\\
-\frac{1}{2}&0\end{pmatrix}\ .

(3.1.6)

Choosing a flat target space
G_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}
as well, the Polyakov action (
3.1.4
) takes the form

S_{\textup{P}}=2T\int d\xi^{+}d\xi^{-}\,\,\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X_{\mu}\ .

(3.1.7)

The equations of motion for the string are computed from the variation of this action. This is equal to

\displaystyle\delta S_{\textup{P}}

\displaystyle=2T\int d\xi^{+}d\xi^{-}\left(\partial_{+}\delta X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X_{\mu}+\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}\delta X_{\mu}\right)=

(3.1.8)

\displaystyle=2T\int d\xi^{+}d\xi^{-}\left[\partial_{+}\left(\delta X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X_{\mu}\right)+\partial_{-}\left(\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\delta X_{\mu}\right)-2\left(\partial_{+}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}\right)\delta X_{\mu}\right]=

\displaystyle=-4T\int d\xi^{+}d\xi^{-}\left(\partial_{+}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}\right)\delta X_{\mu}-T\int d^{2}\xi\,\,\partial_{\sigma}\left(\delta X^{\mu}\partial_{\sigma}X_{\mu}\right)\ .

Since strings are extended objects, the boundary term in the
\sigma
coordinate is, a priori, nontrivial. For closed strings, the periodicity condition
\sigma=2\pi\simeq\sigma=0
is such that the boundary term automatically vanishes, since
X^{\mu}(\sigma=2\pi)=X^{\mu}(\sigma=0)
must be satisfied by consistency. In contrast, the open string has, by construction, two boundary points
\sigma=0
and
\sigma=\pi
, on which the boundary term in eq. (
3.1.8
) depends explicitly:

\int_{0}^{\pi}d\sigma\partial_{\sigma}\left(\delta X^{\mu}\partial_{\sigma}X_{\mu}\right)=\left(\delta X^{\mu}\partial_{\sigma}X_{\mu}\right)|_{\sigma=\pi}-\left(\delta X^{\mu}\partial_{\sigma}X_{\mu}\right)|_{\sigma=0}\ .

(3.1.9)

Boundary conditions are thus required for the boundary term of open strings to vanish. These conditions can be either of the Neumann or Dirichlet type, imposed at the two ends of the open string:

\displaystyle\mathrm{NN:}\quad\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}|_{\sigma=0}=\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}|_{\sigma=\pi}=0\ ,

\displaystyle\mathrm{DD:}\quad\delta X^{\mu}|_{\sigma=0}=\delta X^{\mu}|_{\sigma=\pi}=0\ .

(3.1.10)

In addition, also mixed ND/DN boundary conditions can be defined accordingly.

Therefore, the string equations of motion that one finds from the Polyakov action (
3.1.4
)-(
3.1.7
) are, with these assumptions,

\partial_{+}\partial_{-}X^{\mu}=0\ .

(3.1.11)

They have the form of a D’Alembertian equation, resembling the dynamics of a system of
D
free scalar fields. The solution has then the form of a superposition of waves, left-movers and right-movers:

X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=X^{\mu}_{\textup{L}}\left(\tau+\sigma\right)+X^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}\left(\tau-\sigma\right)\ ,

(3.1.12)

which have the general form of an expansion in constant, linear, and oscillating terms in the progressive/regressive wave coordinates:

\displaystyle X^{\mu}_{\textup{L}}\left(\tau+\sigma\right)

\displaystyle=x^{\mu}_{\textup{L}}+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}p^{\mu}_{\textup{L}}\left(\tau+\sigma\right)+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\alpha_{n}^{\mu}}{n}e^{-in\left(\tau+\sigma\right)}\ ,

(3.1.13)

\displaystyle X^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}\left(\tau-\sigma\right)

\displaystyle=x^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}+\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}p^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}\left(\tau-\sigma\right)+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\bar{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}}{n}e^{-in\left(\tau-\sigma\right)}\ ,

where
\alpha^{\prime}=\left(2\pi T\right)^{-1}
. This general solution must be supplemented with the appropriate periodicity/boundary conditions for closed/open strings. In addition, this solution is further constrained by the requirement that the stress-energy tensor must vanish on-shell (
3.1.5
). In light-cone coordinates (
3.1.6
), the nontrivial components of the stress-energy tensor (
3.1.5
) are

\displaystyle T_{++}=\partial_{+}X^{\mu}\partial_{+}X_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle T_{--}=\partial_{-}X^{\mu}\partial_{-}X_{\mu}\ ,

(3.1.14)

and its conservation implies
T_{++}=T_{++}\left(\xi^{+}\right)
and
T_{--}=T_{--}\left(\xi^{-}\right)
.

Closed string dynamics

The closed string solution must satisfy the periodicity condition
X^{\mu}(\sigma=2\pi)=X^{\mu}(\sigma=0)
. This implies that
p^{\mu}_{\textup{L}}=p^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}\equiv p^{\mu}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
in eq. (
3.1.12
)-(
3.1.13
). Calling
x^{\mu}\equiv x^{\mu}_{\textup{L}}+x^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}
, eq. (
3.1.12
) becomes

X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+\alpha^{\prime}p^{\mu}\tau+i\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{1}{n}\left[\alpha_{n}^{\mu}e^{-in\left(\tau+\sigma\right)}+\bar{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}e^{-in\left(\tau-\sigma\right)}\right]\ .

(3.1.15)

The reality condition
\left(X^{\mu}\right)^{*}=X^{\mu}
yields then
\left(\alpha^{\mu}_{n}\right)^{*}=\alpha_{-n}^{\mu}
and
\left(\bar{\alpha}^{\mu}_{n}\right)^{*}=\bar{\alpha}_{-n}^{\mu}
. Defining, as customary,

\alpha_{0}^{\mu}\equiv\sqrt{\frac{\alpha^{\prime}}{2}}p^{\mu}\ ,

(3.1.16)

the stress-energy tensor (
3.1.14
) takes the form

\displaystyle T_{++}=\alpha^{\prime}\sum_{n}L_{n}e^{-in\left(\tau+\sigma\right)}\ ,

\displaystyle T_{--}=\alpha^{\prime}\sum_{n}\bar{L}_{n}e^{-in\left(\tau-\sigma\right)}\ ,

(3.1.17)

where
L_{n}
and
\bar{L}_{n}
are the Virasoro generators

\displaystyle L_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\alpha_{n-m}^{\mu}\alpha_{m}{}_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{L}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m}\bar{\alpha}_{n-m}^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}_{m}{}_{\mu}\ ,

(3.1.18)

satisfying the Virasoro algebra

\displaystyle\left\{L_{n},L_{m}\right\}=-i\left(n-m\right)L_{n+m}\ ,

\displaystyle\left\{\bar{L}_{n},\bar{L}_{m}\right\}=-i\left(n-m\right)\bar{L}_{n+m}\ .

(3.1.19)

Therefore, the vanishing condition on the stress-energy tensor (
3.1.5
) turns into the infinite set of constraints

L_{n}=\bar{L}_{n}=0\quad\forall\,\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\ .

(3.1.20)

The
n=0
constraint yields the mass formula for the propagating states:

M^{2}=\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\alpha_{-m}^{\mu}\alpha_{m}{}_{\mu}\ ,

(3.1.21)

along with the so-called
level matching condition

\displaystyle\sum_{m\neq 0}\alpha_{-m}^{\mu}\alpha_{m}{}_{\mu}=\sum_{m\neq 0}\bar{\alpha}_{-m}^{\mu}\bar{\alpha}_{m}{}_{\mu}\ ,

(3.1.22)

relating the progressive and regressive oscillators.

Open string dynamics

For open strings one proceeds in the same way as for closed ones, but enforcing the boundary conditions (
3.1.10
). These imply the main feature of open strings: that left and right oscillators are now related to each other.

NN open strings

The NN boundary conditions in eq. (
3.1.10
) are satisfied by
p^{\mu}_{\textup{L}}=p^{\mu}_{\textup{R}}
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, as for the closed strings, together with

\displaystyle\alpha_{n}^{\mu}=\bar{\alpha}_{n}^{\mu}\ .

(3.1.23)

This yields

X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+2\alpha^{\prime}p^{\mu}\tau+i\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\alpha_{n}^{\mu}}{n}\cos(n\sigma)e^{-in\tau}\ .

(3.1.24)

In this case, one has only one set of Virasoro generators
L_{n}
, defined as in eq. (
3.1.18
), where one now takes
\alpha^{\mu}_{0}\equiv\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}p^{\mu}
. These generators are constrained to identically vanish by the energy-momentum tensor condition (
3.1.5
). The constraint on
L_{0}
yields the mass of the open-string states, which is found to be

M^{2}=\frac{1}{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\alpha^{\mu}_{-m}\alpha_{m}{}_{\mu}\ .

(3.1.25)

DD open strings

The DD boundary conditions in eq. (
3.1.10
) are such that the open string endpoints are completely fixed, so that the string can only have vibration modes and no time translation one. The correspondent solution reads

X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=x^{\mu}+d^{\mu}\sigma+\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}}\frac{\alpha_{n}^{\mu}}{n}\sin(n\sigma)e^{-in\tau}\ .

(3.1.26)

3.1.2
String quantization

Once the solutions to the equations of motion (
3.1.11
) for closed and open strings are obtained, one can perform their quantization in the standard way. This amounts to the promotion of the target space coordinates
X^{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
and their conjugate momenta
\Pi_{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)=\dot{X}_{\mu}(\tau,\sigma)
to quantum operators, satisfying the canonical equal-time commutation relations

\left[X^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right),\Pi^{\nu}\left(\tau,\sigma^{\prime}\right)\right]=i\eta^{\mu\nu}\delta\left(\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}\right)\ .

(3.1.27)

Combining this quantum condition with the explicit solutions (
3.1.15
) and (
3.1.24
)-(
3.1.26
) and the vanishing stress-energy tensor condition (
3.1.5
)-(
3.1.20
) leads to the rich and powerful features of string theory.

We illustrate this explicitly in the case of open strings with NN boundary conditions, given in eq. (
3.1.24
). The DD open string and the closed string cases follow in complete analogy. We do so by following the paradigm of the light-cone quantization
[
194
]
. This method exploits a residual gauge freedom of the Polyakov action — which reflects the invariance of the D’Alembert equations (
3.1.11
) under a reparameterization of the light-cone coordinates of the type
\xi^{\pm}{}^{\prime}\to\tilde{\xi}^{\pm}\left(\xi^{\pm}\right)
— to align the worldsheet light-cone with the one in target space. This is given by

\displaystyle X^{\pm}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(X^{0}\pm X^{D-1}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle ds^{2}=-2dX^{+}dX^{-}+\sum_{i=1}^{D-2}dX^{i}dX_{i}\ .

(3.1.28)

The identification condition, known as the light-cone gauge, that can be fixed with the residual gauge invariance is

X^{+}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)=x^{+}+2\alpha^{\prime}p^{+}\tau\ .

(3.1.29)

The rest of the target space coordinates are written in this setup as

\displaystyle X^{-}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

\displaystyle=x^{-}+2\alpha^{\prime}p^{-}\tau+i\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\alpha_{n}^{-}}{n}\cos(n\sigma)e^{-in\tau}\ ,

(3.1.30)

\displaystyle X^{i}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

\displaystyle=x^{i}+2\alpha^{\prime}p^{i}\tau+i\sqrt{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n\neq 0}\frac{\alpha_{n}^{i}}{n}\cos(n\sigma)e^{-in\tau}\ .

This light-cone gauge parameterization breaks explicitly Lorentz-invariance, so that one has to restore it at the end. Its main advantage is that it allows one to solve very easily the stress-energy tensor constraint (
3.1.5
). In particular, it implies that also the coordinate
X^{-}(\tau,\sigma)
is constrained, as

\displaystyle T_{++}=T_{--}=0\quad\implies\quad\partial_{\pm}X^{-}=\frac{1}{2\alpha^{\prime}p^{+}}\partial_{\pm}X^{i}\partial_{\pm}X_{i}\ ,

(3.1.31)

which turns into a condition on the
\alpha_{n}^{-}
oscillators:

\alpha_{n}^{-}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}}\frac{1}{4p^{+}}\sum_{m}\alpha_{n-m}^{i}\alpha_{m}{}_{i}\ .

(3.1.32)

Evaluated for the
n=0
oscillator, this relation yields the mass formula

M^{2}=\frac{1}{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{m\neq 0}\alpha^{i}_{-m}\alpha_{m}{}_{i}\ ,

(3.1.33)

depending only on the physical transverse oscillators.

Quantization is then performed following the standard eq. (
3.1.27
). The light-cone gauge is such that the canonical commutation relations act nontrivially only on a restricted, simplified set of operators. This concerns, in particular, the transverse oscillators, which take the form of creation and annihilation operators:

\left[\alpha_{n}^{i},\alpha_{m}^{j}\right]=n\delta_{n+m,0}\delta^{ij}.

(3.1.34)

Therefore, the transverse oscillators define a Fock space for the string quantum excitations, which is obtained starting from the vacuum
\left|0\right\rangle
— such that
\alpha_{m}\left|0\right\rangle=0
— and then acting iteratively with
\alpha_{-m}^{i}
, where
m
is taken positive. The mass in eq. (
3.1.33
) becomes a quantum operator as well, and being quadratic in the ladder operators it requires to be normal-ordered. A proper renormalization of the zero-point contributions yields

M^{2}=\frac{1}{2\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{m\neq 0}:\alpha^{i}_{-m}\alpha_{m}{}_{i}:=\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{m>0}\alpha^{i}_{-m}\alpha_{m}{}_{i}-\frac{D-2}{24\alpha^{\prime}}\equiv\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N-\frac{D-2}{24}\right)\ .

(3.1.35)

We can now fully characterize the spectrum of the open string quantum states:

•

beginning with the vacuum
\left|0\right\rangle
, the above mass formula immediately tells that this state is tachyonic:

M^{2}\left|0\right\rangle=-\frac{D-2}{24\alpha^{\prime}}\left|0\right\rangle\ ,

(3.1.36)

denoting a fundamental instability of bosonic string theory.

•

The first excited state is given by
\alpha_{-1}^{i}\left|0\right\rangle
. It contains
D-2
degrees of freedom and its mass is equal to

M^{2}\left(\alpha_{-1}^{i}\left|0\right\rangle\right)=-\frac{D-26}{24\alpha^{\prime}}\left(\alpha_{-1}^{i}\left|0\right\rangle\right)\ .

(3.1.37)

This state transforms, by construction, in the vectorial representation of the little group
\mathrm{SO}(D-2)
, and since this state contains
D-2
degrees of freedom, this representation must be massless. Thus, consistency with Lorentz symmetry implies that the bosonic string can be defined only in the critical dimension

D=26\ .

(3.1.38)

•

All the higher excitations are massive. The first such massive level
M^{2}=\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}
is given by the states

\displaystyle\alpha_{-2}^{i}\left|0\right\rangle\ ,

\displaystyle\alpha_{-1}^{i}\alpha_{-1}^{j}\left|0\right\rangle\ .

(3.1.39)

These two states organize into a symmetric representation of the little group
\mathrm{SO}(D-1)
corresponding to the degrees of freedom of a massive spin-2 field:

\displaystyle\left(D-2\right)+\frac{\left(D-2\right)\left(D-1\right)}{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{D\left(D-1\right)}{2}-1

(3.1.40)

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{\left(D-2\right)\left(D-1\right)}{2}-1\right]+\left(D-2\right)+1\ .

The second line represents in fact a massless spin-2 tensor plus a vector and a scalar field, which correspond to the polarization states of a massive spin-2 field (see for example eq. (
2.4.67
) in Chapter
2
).

Following the same pattern, the higher massive states correspond to higher-spin representations. Therefore, the string excitations spectrum is characterized by a tower of states of increasing mass and spin: this is the famous Regge trajectory, which is a consequence of the non-canonical normalization of the commutation relations among the ladder operators of eq. (
3.1.34
).

The closed-string spectrum

As remarked at the beginning of this section, the quantization procedure is analogous for closed strings. The main difference is that now the left and right oscillators define two distinct set of ladder operators, related by the level-matching condition (
3.1.22
), so that the resulting spectrum is richer. The mass operator in eq. (
3.1.21
) becomes

M^{2}=\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left[\sum_{n>0}\left(\alpha^{i}_{-m}\alpha_{m}{}_{i}+\bar{\alpha}^{i}_{-m}\bar{\alpha}_{m}{}_{i}\right)-\frac{D-2}{12}\right]\equiv\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N+\bar{N}-\frac{D-2}{12}\right),

(3.1.41)

and the level-matching condition enforces
N=\bar{N}
as a relation on the action of these quantum operators on physical states.

The closed string vacuum state
\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle
is also tachyonic, as the open string one in eq. (
3.1.36
). Then, the first excited state that is allowed by the level-matching condition is the tensor state

\alpha_{-1}^{i}\bar{\alpha}_{-1}^{j}\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle\ .

(3.1.42)

Its
\left(D-2\right)\times\left(D-2\right)
degrees of freedom can be organized into

\left(D-2\right)\left(D-2\right)=\left[\frac{\left(D-2\right)\left(D-1\right)}{2}-1\right]+\frac{\left(D-2\right)\left(D-3\right)}{2}+1\ ,

(3.1.43)

namely, into the symmetric traceless, antisymmetric and singlet representations of the little group
\mathrm{SO}(D-2)
. As before, these states must be massless in order to be consistent with Lorentz invariance, which is indeed the case for the critical dimension
D=26
. In the field theory limit, these states are identified, respectively, with the graviton
G_{\mu\nu}
, a rank-2 antisymmetric tensor field
B_{\mu\nu}
, known as the Kalb–Ramond field, and the dilaton
\phi
. Therefore, the quantization of the bosonic string yields, in a natural way, the gravitational interaction. This string realization is the only known consistent quantum description of gravity.

3.1.3
The field theory limit and background fields

We have seen that the spectrum of excitations of the quantized string is built on a tachyonic vacuum and includes a massless state and a tower of higher-spin states of increasing mass. Physically, there should exist an energy threshold below which this massive tower of states can be neglected. This corresponds to the field theory limit of the string, where the theory reduces to an effective field theory describing the massless degrees of freedom of the string. For the closed string, this field theory limit consists of the graviton, the Kalb–Ramond antisymmetric field, and the dilaton, as discussed above.

This field theory limit of closed strings can be understood also from an alternative perspective, in terms of a background-field description. To see this, we return to the Polyakov action of eq. (
3.1.4
), in which the string coordinates couple to the auxiliary worldsheet metric
g_{ab}
and to the target space metric
G_{\mu\nu}
. Taking a nontrivial background metric
G_{\mu\nu}
is already a generalization of the above discussion on the string dynamics and quantization, which was carried out in flat Minkowski space. Nevertheless, further generalizations of the Polyakov action (
3.1.4
) are actually possible. Two additional nontrivial terms can be introduced through appropriate background fields. The first such term is the Einstein–Hilbert action for the worldsheet metric
g_{ab}
. Since the worldsheet metric is two-dimensional, the Einstein–Hilbert action reduces to the Euler characteristic of the worldsheet,
i.e.
a constant. This can be made nontrivial through a coupling to a background scalar field, which we call
\phi
. The second term is the antisymmetric version of the string coordinates kinetic term, in which the contraction of the worldsheet indices is made with the two-dimensional Levi–Civita tensor
\varepsilon^{ab}
(instead of the worldsheet metric), and is introduced in the action via coupling to an antisymmetric background field
B_{\mu\nu}
(instead of the target space metric). The resulting generalization of the Polyakov action is then

S_{\textup{P}}=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}\xi\left\{g^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X^{\nu}G_{\mu\nu}(X)+\varepsilon^{ab}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial_{b}X^{\nu}B_{\mu\nu}(X)-\alpha^{\prime}R(g)\phi(X)\right\}\ .

(3.1.44)

However, this background is not completely arbitrary. In particular, it must be consistent with the conformal symmetry of the worldsheet and not source any Weyl anomaly at the quantum level. The conformal symmetry can be imposed on the background fields by thinking of them as coupling constants and requiring them to define a fixed point of the renormalization group flow, as fixed points indeed correspond to conformal field theories. This condition is met if the
\beta
-functions associated with the background coupling fields identically vanish. These equations can be computed to be
[
195
]

\displaystyle\beta{}_{\mu\nu}^{(G)}

\displaystyle=R_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{4}H_{\mu}{}^{\lambda\rho}H_{\nu\lambda\rho}-2\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\phi+\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha^{\prime}\right)=0\ ,

(3.1.45)

\displaystyle\beta_{\mu\nu}^{(B)}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\lambda}H^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}-2\nabla_{\lambda}\phi H^{\lambda}{}_{\mu\nu}+\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha^{\prime}\right)=0\ ,

\displaystyle\beta^{(\phi)}

\displaystyle=4\nabla_{\lambda}\phi\nabla^{\lambda}\phi-4\nabla_{\lambda}\nabla^{\lambda}\phi+R+\frac{1}{12}H_{\lambda\rho\sigma}H^{\lambda\rho\sigma}+\mathcal{O}\left(\alpha^{\prime}\right)=0\ ,

in which
H_{\mu\nu\rho}=3\partial_{[\mu}H_{\nu\rho]}
is the antisymmetric tensor field strength and
\nabla_{\mu}
is the covariant derivative associated with the target space metric. The striking feature of these equations is that they exactly match the equations of motion associated with the action

S=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{26}x\sqrt{-G}\,\,e^{-2\phi}\left(R-\frac{1}{12}H^{\mu\nu\rho}H_{\mu\nu\rho}+4\partial_{\mu}\phi\partial^{\mu}\phi\right)\ ,

(3.1.46)

which is the field theory limit of the closed bosonic string. Consequently,
G_{\mu\nu}
,
B_{\mu\nu}
and
\phi
are identified with the graviton, the Kalb–Ramond field and the dilaton introduced in eq. (
3.1.42
)-(
3.1.43
). Higher-order corrections in
\alpha^{\prime}
in the
\beta
-functions equations (
3.1.45
) correspond to higher-derivative, stringy corrections to the low-energy effective field theory action.

3.1.4
The bosonic string partition function and 1-loop vacuum amplitudes

A quantity of crucial importance in understanding the physics described by a given string theory is the string
partition function

\mathcal{Z}
. Schematically, it has the form

\mathcal{Z}=\int DX(\tau,\sigma)e^{-S_{\textup{P}}}\ ,

(3.1.47)

where
S_{\textup{P}}
is the Polyakov action (
3.1.4
). The meaning of this formula can be understood in analogy with the path integral of the one-particle state in quantum mechanics. This is given in terms of the position operator
q(t)
by

\left\langle q_{\textup{F}},t_{\textup{F}}|q_{\textup{I}},t_{\textup{I}}\right\rangle=\int_{q_{\textup{I}}}^{q_{\textup{F}}}Dq(t)e^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}S(q,\dot{q})}\ .

(3.1.48)

The interpretation of this formula is that the transition amplitude for the quantum one-particle state to go from the position
q_{\textup{I}}
at time
t_{\textup{I}}
to the position
q_{\textup{F}}
at time
t_{\textup{F}}
is given by the infinite sum over all possible trajectories between
q_{\textup{I}}
and
q_{\textup{F}}
, weighted by a Boltzmann-like factor
e^{-\frac{1}{\hbar}S}
. When going from the point-particle to the extended string, the trajectories in spacetime become two-dimensional manifolds,
i.e.
the worldsheet, so that a partition function like eq. (
3.1.47
) can be interpreted as the sum over all possible geometries that the worldsheet can describe. Actually, the set of symmetries enjoyed by the Polyakov action are such that this sum can be effectively performed over the possible
topologies
of the worldsheet. This is made precise and manifest by the Einstein–Hilbert term of the (generalized) Polyakov action:

\frac{\phi}{4\pi}\int d^{2}\xi\sqrt{-g}\,\,R(g)=\phi\chi\ ,

(3.1.49)

Here
\phi
is the dilaton background field and
\chi
is the Euler characteristic of the worldsheet. Assuming a vacuum expectation value
\langle\phi\rangle
, the infinite sum over the worldsheet geometries described by the partition function (
3.1.47
) is actually a sum over topologies, each one associated with a different Euler characteristic
\chi
and weighted by a factor
e^{-\langle\phi\rangle\chi}\equiv g_{s}^{-\chi}
, with
g_{s}
being the string coupling. The Euler characteristic of a Riemann surface is equal to

\displaystyle\chi=2-2h-b-c\ ,

(3.1.50)

where
h
is the number of handles,
b
the number of boundaries and
c
the number of crosscaps. Thus, the partition function (
3.1.47
) is given by an expansion of the form

\mathcal{Z}\sim\frac{1}{g_{s}^{2}}\left[\chi=2\right]+\frac{1}{g_{s}}\left[\chi=1\right]+\left[\chi=0\right]+\sum_{n>0}g_{s}^{n}\left[\chi=n\right]\ .

(3.1.51)

This is known as Polyakov expansion
[
196
,
197
]
, and it is the string analogue of the loop expansion of amplitudes in quantum field theory. In contrast to the field theory case, we see that in string theory the dominant term of the expansion (
3.1.51
) is a loop contribution, given by the topologies of Euler characteristic
\chi=0
. There are four Riemann surfaces with vanishing Euler characteristic:

\begin{array}[]{rcccc}\text{Torus}&\mathcal{T}:&h=1,&b=0,&c=0,\\
\text{Cylinder}&\mathcal{A}:&h=0,&b=2,&c=0,\\
\text{Klein bottle}&\mathcal{K}:&h=0,&b=0,&c=2,\\
\text{M\"{o}bius strip}&\mathcal{M}:&h=0,&b=1,&c=1.\\
\end{array}

For the bosonic strings at hand, the worldsheet of closed strings must be a closed (oriented) Riemann surface with no boundaries, so that the corresponding partition function is dominated by the torus amplitude
\mathcal{T}
. On the contrary, the open strings worldsheet is, by definition, a Riemann surface with nontrivial boundaries, so that the leading contribution in the corresponding partition function is the cylinder amplitude
\mathcal{A}
. The Klein bottle and the Möbius strip — and, in general, Riemann surfaces with crosscaps
c\neq 0
— appear instead in the case of unoriented strings, which we will discuss in Section
3.2.5
.

The torus amplitude

We now focus on the case of closed strings and their corresponding torus amplitude
\mathcal{T}
. In order to derive its analytic expression, we start again from an analogy, as for eq. (
3.1.47
)-(
3.1.48
), this time drawing from quantum field theory
[
191
]
. The vacuum energy
\Gamma
in quantum field theory is obtained from the trace over the particles masses as

\Gamma=-\log\mathcal{Z}=-\frac{V_{D}}{2\left(4\pi\right)^{\frac{D}{2}}}\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{t^{\frac{D}{2}+1}}\mathrm{Str}\left(e^{-tM^{2}}\right)\ ,

(3.1.52)

in which
\epsilon
is a UV cutoff,
V_{D}
is the total spacetime volume,
t
is a Schwinger time parameter and
\mathrm{Str}
is the super-trace operator counting the masses of bosonic and fermionic fields with appropriate multiplicity. A first extension of this formula to string theory is obtained by inserting the masses of the closed string excitations, given in eq. (
3.1.41
). In the critical dimension
D=26
, these are given by

M^{2}=\frac{2}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N+\bar{N}-2\right)\ .

(3.1.53)

In addition, one must also account for the level-matching condition
N=\bar{N}
of eq. (
3.1.22
). We do this by supplementing the formula (
3.1.52
) with a Dirac
\delta
-function of the type

\delta\left(N-\bar{N}\right)=\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}ds\,\,e^{2\pi i\left(N-\bar{N}\right)s}\ ,

(3.1.54)

yielding

\Gamma=-\frac{V_{26}}{2\left(4\pi\right)^{13}}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{\frac{1}{2}}ds\int_{\epsilon}^{\infty}\frac{dt}{t^{14}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[e^{-\frac{2t}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N+\bar{N}-2\right)}e^{2\pi i\left(N-\bar{N}\right)s}\right]\ .

(3.1.55)

This formula can be rewritten in compact form introducing

\displaystyle\tau=\tau_{1}+i\tau_{2}\equiv s+\frac{i}{\pi\alpha^{\prime}}t\ ,

\displaystyle q=e^{2\pi i\tau}\ ,

(3.1.56)

as

\Gamma=-\frac{V_{26}}{2\left(4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{13}}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\frac{d^{2}\tau}{\tau_{2}^{14}}\mathrm{Tr}\left(q^{N-1}\bar{q}^{\bar{N}-1}\right)\ .

(3.1.57)

The domain of integration is, by now,
\mathcal{D}=\left\{\tau\in\mathbb{H}^{+},\lvert\tau_{1}\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2}\right\}
, corresponding to the strip of width one centered in the complex upper-half plane
\mathbb{H}^{+}
. We first focus on the integrand. The trace in eq. (
3.1.57
) factorizes into contributions from left- and right-moving modes. The former is computed explicitly to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\left(q^{N-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}\mathrm{Tr}\left(q^{\sum_{n>0}\alpha^{i}_{-n}\alpha_{n}{}_{i}}\right)=\frac{1}{q}\prod_{i=1}^{24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\left(q^{\alpha^{i}_{-n}\alpha_{n}^{i}}\right)=

(3.1.58)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}\prod_{i=1}^{24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{n}}\left\langle k_{n}\right|q^{\alpha^{i}_{-n}\alpha_{n}^{i}}\left|k_{n}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{q}\prod_{i=1}^{24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{n}}q^{k_{n}n}=

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q}\prod_{i=1}^{24}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1-q^{n}}=\left[\frac{1}{q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1-q^{n}\right)}\right]^{24}=\frac{1}{\eta(\tau)^{24}}\ ,

where we introduced the famous Dedekind function
\eta(\tau)
. The right-movers give the conjugate contribution
\bar{\eta}(\bar{\tau})
, so that eq. (
3.1.57
) becomes

\Gamma=-\frac{V_{26}}{2\left(4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{13}}\int_{\mathcal{D}}\frac{d^{2}\tau}{\tau_{2}^{14}}\frac{1}{\lvert\eta(\tau)\rvert^{48}}\ .

(3.1.59)

However, this is not yet the final result. Defined in this way, this integral is actually divergent. The reason is that the integration domain
\mathcal{D}
, which was drawn from the analogy with the field theory case (
3.1.52
), is redundant in the case of the string. In fact, the string worldsheet enjoys a significant number of symmetries, which must be taken into account in order to properly count the physical contributions to the vacuum energy. In the present case, the topology of the worldsheet is that of the torus, and the integration in eq. (
3.1.57
) sums over all torus geometries, parameterized by
\tau\in\mathcal{D}
. This integral turns out to be divergent because this integration overcounts the number of inequivalent tori. In fact, equivalent tori are identified by modular transformations of
\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
, acting on the complex parameter
\tau
as

\displaystyle\tau\to\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\ ,

(3.1.60)

with
\left\{a,b,c,d\right\}\in\mathbb{Z}
and
ad-bc=1
. This group is generated by the transformations

\displaystyle T=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\ ,

\displaystyle S=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\ ,

\displaystyle\left(S\right)^{2}=\left(ST\right)^{3}=-\mathbb{1}\ ,

(3.1.61)

acting respectively as the shift
\tau\to\tau+1
and the reflection
\tau\to-\tau^{-1}
. The inequivalent tori are therefore identified by the so-called fundamental domain

\mathscr{F}=\mathbb{H}^{+}\setminus\mathrm{PSL}(2,\mathbb{Z})=\left\{-\frac{1}{2}<\tau_{1}\leq\frac{1}{2},\lvert\tau\rvert\geq 1,\tau_{2}>0\right\}\ .

(3.1.62)

Therefore, the resulting torus amplitude, describing the leading vacuum energy contribution from closed oriented strings, is found to be
1
1

1

With respect to the above formulae, the torus amplitude in eq. (
3.1.63
) is rescaled to canonical normalization.

\mathcal{T}=\frac{1}{\left(4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{13}}\int_{\mathscr{F}}\frac{d^{2}\tau}{\tau_{2}^{14}}\frac{1}{\lvert\eta(\tau)\rvert^{48}}\ .

(3.1.63)

The emergence of the Dedekind
\eta
-function in the combination of eq. (
3.1.63
) has now a nice interpretation in terms of modular invariance of the whole amplitude, consistently with the symmetry properties of the worldsheet and the Polyakov action. This can be verified using the following transformation properties of the
\eta
-function:

\displaystyle T\text{:}\quad\eta(\tau+1)=e^{\frac{i\pi}{12}}\,\eta(\tau)\ ,

\displaystyle S\text{:}\quad\eta\left(-\textstyle{\frac{1}{\tau}}\right)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\,\eta(\tau)\ .

(3.1.64)

Vacuum amplitudes such as the torus have the interesting property that they encode the entirety of the degrees of freedom of the quantized string. The degrees of freedom associated with each mass level can be identified through a Taylor expansion around
q=0
of the integrand of eq. (
3.1.63
). Recalling its definition (
3.1.52
) as an (infinite) sum over mass states, the expansion has the form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta(q)^{24}\bar{\eta}(\bar{q})^{24}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m}c(n,m)q^{n}\bar{q}^{m}\ ,

(3.1.65)

and the physical states are identified by the level-matching condition, which selects only the states with equal powers of
q
and
\bar{q}
,
i.e.
with
n=m
in the sum. The relative coefficient
c(n,n)
gives the number of degrees of freedom of the states of mass
M=\frac{n}{\alpha^{\prime}}
. Restricting the expansion above to the physical states, one obtains

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\eta(q)^{24}\bar{\eta}(\bar{q})^{24}}\Big|_{\textup{physical}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n}c(n,n)\left(q\bar{q}\right)^{n}=\frac{1}{q\bar{q}}+576+104976q\bar{q}+\dots\ ,

(3.1.66)

in which we recognize the tachyonic vacuum, the 576 degrees of freedom given by the 26-dimensional massless graviton, Kalb–Ramond field and dilaton, and so on with the massive tower of states.

The cylinder amplitude and the double channel interpretation

The cylinder amplitude
\mathcal{A}
is the open string analogue of the torus amplitude for closed strings. Following this analogy, one obtains

\mathcal{A}=\frac{1}{2\left(4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{24}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d\tau_{2}}{\tau_{2}^{14}}\frac{1}{\eta\left(\frac{i\tau_{2}}{2}\right)^{24}}\ .

(3.1.67)

As for the torus in eq. (
3.1.66
), we can count the degrees of freedom of the quantized open string by Taylor expanding this cylinder amplitude around
q=0
, obtaining:

\frac{1}{\eta(q)^{24}}=\frac{1}{q}+24+324q\dots\ .

(3.1.68)

In this expansion, one clearly recognizes the tachyonic vacuum, the massless vector, and the massive spin-2 state of the open-string spectrum discussed in eq. (
3.1.36
), (
3.1.37
) and (
3.1.39
).

Because of the open string setting, only one power of the Dedekind
\eta
-function appears in the amplitude (
3.1.67
), depending on the parameter
\tau=\frac{i\tau_{2}}{2}
. This is the so-called modulus of the doubly covering torus, describing the cylinder in the real projective plane
[
191
]
. As for the torus in eq. (
3.1.63
), it parameterizes the cylinder shapes, and the associated fundamental domain — which must be the domain of integration of the vacuum amplitude — defines all inequivalent cylinders. This is the general structure of vacuum string amplitudes.

With this choice of the "time" integration parameter, the amplitude is called the loop (or direct) channel. In this picture, one looks at the cylinder vertically, from the point of view of an open string that sweeps it closing a loop. However, one can also move to a "horizontal time" integration parameter
l=\frac{2}{\tau_{2}}
. The amplitude obtained with this choice is the so-called transverse channel, equal to

\tilde{\mathcal{A}}=\frac{2^{-13}}{2\left(4\pi^{2}\alpha^{\prime}\right)^{24}}\int_{0}^{\infty}d\ell\frac{1}{\eta\left(i\ell\right)^{24}}\ .

(3.1.69)

Formally, it can be obtained from the loop channel (
3.1.67
) via an
S
transformation (
3.1.61
). In this basis the cylinder is parameterized horizontally, as a hollow tube. While in the direct channel (
3.1.67
) the amplitude describes the 1-loop propagation of an open string, the transverse channel (
3.1.69
) allows for a dual interpretation in terms of tree-level propagation of a closed string.

3.2
Superstring theory

The bosonic string discussed in Section
3.1
already displays a number of striking features, the main one being achieving a quantum description of gravity. However, this theory has two important drawbacks. First, its vacuum is tachyonic, meaning that the theory is inherently unstable. Second, it does not describe any fermionic degrees of freedom.

These two problems are solved at once introducing fermionic degrees of freedom on the worldsheet
[
197
,
198
,
199
]
. We do so following the Ramond–Neveu–Schwarz formalism
[
200
,
201
]
and introduce the fermionic coordinate
\psi^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)
, which is a vector in target space — like the bosonic coordinates
X^{\mu}
— and a spinor on the worldsheet. The two-dimensional Clifford algebra is described by the matrices

\displaystyle\rho^{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\ ,

\displaystyle\rho^{1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\ ,

\displaystyle\left\{\rho^{a},\rho^{b}\right\}=2\eta^{ab}\ ,

(3.2.1)

and the worldsheet spinor
\psi^{\mu}
is a Majorana fermion, which one writes as

\displaystyle\psi^{\mu}=\begin{pmatrix}\psi^{\mu}_{-}\\
\psi^{\mu}_{+}\end{pmatrix}\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}^{\mu}{}^{*}=\psi_{\pm}^{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\bar{\psi}^{\mu}=i\psi^{\mu}{}^{\dagger}\rho^{0}\ .

(3.2.2)

Working in the conformal gauge, the extended Polyakov action is

S_{\textup{P}}=-\frac{1}{4\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\int d^{2}\xi\left(\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\partial^{a}X_{\mu}+\bar{\psi}^{\mu}\rho^{a}\partial_{a}\psi_{\mu}\right)\ .

(3.2.3)

The crucial property of this action is that it enjoys worldsheet supersymmetry:

\displaystyle\delta X^{\mu}=\bar{\epsilon}\psi^{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\delta\psi^{\mu}=\rho^{a}\partial_{a}X^{\mu}\epsilon\ .

(3.2.4)

As a consequence, this theory has now two conserved currents, the stress-energy tensor
T_{ab}
, as in the bosonic case, and the supersymmetry current
\Xi_{a}
. In the light-cone gauge, their nontrivial components are

\displaystyle T_{\pm\pm}=\partial_{\pm}X^{\mu}\partial_{\pm}X_{\mu}+\frac{i}{2}\psi^{\mu}_{\pm}\partial_{\pm}\psi_{\pm}{}_{\mu}\ ,

\displaystyle\Xi_{\pm}=\partial_{\pm}X^{\mu}\psi_{\pm}{}_{\mu}\ .

(3.2.5)

As in a chiral multiplet like eq. (
1.1.38
), the dependence on the left- and right-moving components factorizes in the currents, as well as in the supersymmetry transformations, as can be seen from eq. (
3.2.4
). Because of the supersymmetry relation with the stress-energy tensor, also the supersymmetry current
\Xi_{a}
must vanish. This can be seen also by writing the action (
3.2.3
) in an arbitrary gauge: in addition to the worldsheet metric
g_{ab}
, supersymmetry requires the introduction of an auxiliary gravitino, and its equation of motion implies the vanishing of the supersymmetry current.

3.2.1
Superstring dynamics and quantization

The analysis of the theory now proceeds in the same way as for the bosonic string, although the new supersymmetric structure will imply important differences. First, we need to understand which periodicity/boundary conditions are satisfied by the fermionic string. The equations of motion boundary term are found to be

\left.\int d\tau\left(\psi_{-}^{\mu}\delta\psi_{-}{}_{\mu}-\psi_{+}^{\mu}\delta\psi_{+}{}_{\mu}\right)\right\rvert_{\sigma=0}^{\sigma=\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2\pi}{\pi}}=0\ ,

(3.2.6)

For closed strings, this amounts to imposing periodic or antiperiodic boundary conditions:
\psi_{\pm}\left(\sigma=2\pi\right)=\pm\psi_{\pm}\left(\sigma=0\right)
. For open strings, eq. (
3.2.6
) must be satisfied independently at the two endpoints, so that the inequivalent choices are given by
\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\sigma=0\right)=\psi_{-}^{\mu}\left(\sigma=0\right)
and
\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\sigma=\pi\right)=\pm\psi_{-}^{\mu}\left(\sigma=\pi\right)
. The corresponding expansions involve, for both closed and open strings, a sum over integer oscillators when the boundary conditions are periodic, over half-integer when they are antiperiodic. The former are referred to as the Ramond sector (R), the latter as the Neveu–Schwarz sector (NS)
[
202
,
200
,
203
,
204
,
205
]
. The corresponding expansions are

•

closed strings:

R:

\displaystyle\psi_{+}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}d_{n}^{\mu}e^{-2i\pi n\left(\tau+\sigma\right)}\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{-}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{d}_{n}^{\mu}e^{-2i\pi n\left(\tau-\sigma\right)}\ ,

(3.2.7)

NS:

\displaystyle\psi_{+}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}b_{r}^{\mu}e^{-2i\pi r\left(\tau+\sigma\right)}\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{-}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{b}_{r}^{\mu}e^{-2i\pi r\left(\tau-\sigma\right)}\ ,

(3.2.8)

where the Majorana condition fixes
\left(d_{n}^{\mu}\right)^{\dagger}=d_{-n}^{\mu}
and similarly for all the other oscillators.

•

Open strings:

\displaystyle\text{R:}\quad\psi_{\pm}^{\mu}=\sqrt{\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}d_{n}^{\mu}e^{-i\pi n\left(\tau\pm\sigma\right)}\ ,

\displaystyle\text{NS:}\quad\psi_{\pm}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\pi\alpha^{\prime}}\sum_{r\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{b}_{r}^{\mu}e^{-i\pi r\left(\tau\pm\sigma\right)}\ .

(3.2.9)

Closed strings

Open strings

Bosonic

X^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma+2\pi\right)=X^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

NN:

\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}\left(\tau,0\right)=\partial_{\sigma}X^{\mu}\left(\tau,\pi\right)=0

DD:

\delta X^{\mu}\left(\tau,0\right)=\delta X^{\mu}\left(\tau,\pi\right)=0

Fermionic

R-R:

\psi_{\pm}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma+2\pi\right)=+\psi_{\pm}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\tau,0\right)=\psi_{-}^{\mu}\left(\tau,0\right)

NS-NS:

\psi_{\pm}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma+2\pi\right)=-\psi_{\pm}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

R-NS:

\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma+2\pi\right)=+\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

R:

\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\pi\right)=+\psi_{-}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\pi\right)

\psi_{-}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma+2\pi\right)=-\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

NS-R:

\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma+2\pi\right)=-\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

NS:

\psi_{+}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\pi\right)=-\psi_{-}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\pi\right)

\psi_{-}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma+2\pi\right)=+\psi_{-}^{\mu}\left(\tau,\sigma\right)

Table 3.1:
Summary of the various boundary conditions and corresponding sectors of superstrings.

All the boundary conditions and the associated sectors of the theory are summarized in Table
3.1
. As we will see, the R-R and NS-NS sectors of the closed string and the R sector of the open strings describe bosonic excitations, while the closed R-NS and NS-R sectors and the open NS sector describe fermionic ones.

We now turn to the quantization, which we perform in the light-cone gauge. As in the bosonic case (
3.1.28
)-(
3.1.29
), one can use the residual worldsheet symmetry to set

\displaystyle X^{+}=x^{+}+2\pi\alpha^{\prime}p^{+}\tau\ ,

\displaystyle\psi^{+}_{\pm}=0\ ,

(3.2.10)

and the vanishing conditions on the stress-energy tensor and the current of supersymmetry (
3.2.5
) are solved by

\displaystyle\partial_{\pm}X^{-}=\frac{1}{2\pi\alpha^{\prime}p^{+}}\left(\partial_{\pm}X^{i}\partial_{\pm}X_{i}+\frac{i}{2}\psi_{\pm}^{i}\partial_{\pm}\psi_{\pm}{}_{i}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\psi_{\pm}^{-}=\frac{1}{\pi\alpha^{\prime}p^{+}}\psi^{i}_{\pm}\partial_{\pm}X_{i}\ .

(3.2.11)

This constraint identifies the transverse oscillators as the physical ones, on which one imposes canonical commutation relations to quantize the theory. The fermionic number operators result in

\displaystyle\text{R:}\quad N_{\psi}=\sum_{n>0}nd_{-n}^{i}d_{n}{}_{i}\ ,

\displaystyle\text{NS:}\quad N_{\psi}=\sum_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}rb_{-r}^{i}b_{r}{}_{i}\ .

(3.2.12)

Denoting with
N_{X}
the bosonic string number operators found in the previous section in eq. (
3.1.35
)-(
3.1.41
), the mass operators for the various sectors — resulting from the proper regularized normal ordering of both bosonic and fermionic oscillators — are given by

•

open strings:

\displaystyle\text{R:}\quad M^{2}=\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N_{X}+N_{\psi}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\text{NS:}\quad M^{2}=\frac{1}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N_{X}+N_{\psi}-\frac{D-2}{16}\right)\ .

(3.2.13)

•

closed strings left-movers:

\displaystyle\text{R:}\quad M^{2}_{L}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N_{X}+N_{\psi}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle\text{NS:}\quad M^{2}_{L}=\frac{4}{\alpha^{\prime}}\left(N_{X}+N_{\psi}-\frac{D-2}{16}\right)\ ,

(3.2.14)

accompanied by analogous expressions for the right-movers and, most importantly, the level-matching condition
M^{2}_{L}=M^{2}_{R}
.

These mass formulae allow us to discuss the spectrum of excitations of the quantized superstring. We proceed, again, sector by sector:

Open NS sector

the vacuum
\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}
of the NS open strings is a tachyonic scalar, of mass

M^{2}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}=-\frac{\left(D-2\right)}{16\alpha^{\prime}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\ .

(3.2.15)

The first excited state is obtained acting on
\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}
with the fermionic string creation operator
b^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and its mass is equal to

M^{2}\left(b^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\right)=\frac{10-D}{2\alpha^{\prime}}\left(b^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\right)\ .

(3.2.16)

This state transforms in the vectorial representation of the little group
\mathrm{SO}(D-2)
and contains
\left(D-2\right)
degrees of freedom. Lorentz invariance requires therefore this state to be massless, and thus fixes the superstring theory critical dimension

D=10\ .

(3.2.17)

The excited states are then built acting with
b_{-r}^{i}
and
\alpha_{-n}^{i}
and represent massive bosonic higher-spin fields.

Open R sector

In the case of the R open sector, the vacuum state is massless, as it is clear from eq. (
3.2.13
), but it carries a spinorial representation of the little group, now fixed to
\mathrm{SO}(8)
. This spinorial structure can be understood from the level-zero oscillators
d_{0}^{i}
, which satisfy the Clifford algebra
\left\{d_{0}^{i},d_{0}^{j}\right\}=2\eta^{ij}
. More specifically, the vacuum
\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}
transforms in the Majorana–Weyl
\bm{8}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{c}
representation of
\mathrm{SO}(8)
, where
s
and
c
denote two different chirality. Therefore, the open R sector contains fermionic excitations, which were missing in the bosonic string case.

Closed NS-NS sector

In the closed string case, there are two possible choices for each left and right-mover, so that four different sectors arise. We begin from the NS-NS one. The vacuum
\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NSNS}}
is again a tachyonic scalar, of mass

M^{2}\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NSNS}}=-\frac{\left(D-2\right)}{4\alpha^{\prime}}\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NSNS}}\ .

(3.2.18)

The first excited state is instead

\displaystyle b_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{i}\bar{b}_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{j}\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NSNS}}\ ,

\displaystyle M^{2}\left(b_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{i}\bar{b}_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{j}\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NSNS}}\right)=\frac{10-D}{2\alpha^{\prime}}\left(b_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{i}\bar{b}_{-\frac{1}{2}}^{j}\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NSNS}}\right)\ .

(3.2.19)

In the critical dimension
D=10
this state is indeed massless, consistently with Lorentz symmetry, and its degrees of freedom organize in the symmetric traceless plus antisymmetric plus singlet representations of
\mathrm{SO}(8)
: these are the graviton, the Kalb–Ramond field and the dilaton degrees of freedom. Gravity — which belonged to the closed-string sector of the bosonic string — is therefore described by the NS-NS sector of closed superstrings.

Closed R-R sector

The R-R sector also yields bosonic excitations, originating from the tensor composition of the left and right movers vacuum representations of the type
\bm{8}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{c}
, as for the open string case. This takes the form

\displaystyle\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{RR}}

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\bm{8}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{c}\right)\otimes\left(\bm{8}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{c}\right)=

(3.2.20)

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{8}_{s}\otimes\bm{8}_{s}\right)\oplus\left(\bm{8}_{c}\otimes\bm{8}_{c}\right)\oplus\left[\left(\bm{8}_{s}\otimes\bm{8}_{c}\right)\oplus\left(\bm{8}_{c}\otimes\bm{8}_{s}\right)\right]=

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{1}\oplus\bm{28}\oplus\bm{35}_{+}\right)\oplus\left(\bm{1}\oplus\bm{28}\oplus\bm{35}_{-}\right)\oplus\left[\left(\bm{8}_{\textup{v}}\oplus\bm{56}\right)\oplus\left(\bm{8}_{\textup{v}}^{\prime}\oplus\bm{56}^{\prime}\right)\right]\ .

Thus, this spectrum comprises, in ten dimensions, two real scalars (
\bm{1}
), two antisymmetric 2-form fields (
\bm{28}
) and two 4-form fields, respectively self-dual (
\bm{35}_{+}
) and anti self-dual (
\bm{35}_{-}
), together with two vectors (
\bm{8}_{\textup{v}}
and
\bm{8}_{\textup{v}}^{\prime}
) and two 3-form fields (
\bm{56}
and
\bm{56}^{\prime}
). These form fields are known as the Ramond–Ramond forms.

Closed NS-R / R-NS sectors

The mixed NS-R and R-NS sectors of the closed superstring have instead fermionic excitations. For instance, the NS-R tensor product of the massless vacuum representations for the NS-R sector yields

\displaystyle\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NSR}}\simeq\bm{8}_{\textup{v}}\otimes\left(\bm{8}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{c}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{8}_{\textup{v}}\otimes\bm{8}_{s}\right)\oplus\left(\bm{8}_{\textup{v}}\otimes\bm{8}_{c}\right)

(3.2.21)

\displaystyle=\left(\bm{56}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{s}\right)\oplus\left(\bm{56}_{c}\oplus\bm{8}_{c}\right)\ ,

which correspond to two spin-
\frac{3}{2}
gravitinos, of chirality
s
and
c
, and two spin-
\frac{1}{2}
"dilatini", of chirality
c
and
s
. The R-NS sector yields the same type of spectrum.

3.2.2
The GSO projection and type II closed superstring theories

The superstring spectrum described above is indeed very rich. In particular, it contains fermionic excitations, which were missing in the purely bosonic string theory. However, this spectrum still contains tachyons, in the closed NS-NS sector (
3.2.18
) and in the open NS sector (
3.2.15
). A revealing feature of this tachyon is that it breaks supersymmetry, since it lacks a superpartner state. From the R-sector point of view, although the vacuum is massless, it lies in a spinorial representation that is too large to be supersymmetric.

According to this perspective, supersymmetry thus exhibits a kind of anomaly, since it is preserved at the level of the classical worldsheet theory but it is broken at the quantum level. Therefore, one is led to try and solve the tachyon problem by consistently imposing supersymmetry on the quantum spectrum. This is done via the Gliozzi–Scherk–Olive (GSO) projection
[
206
]
. This is constructed from the worldsheet fermion number operator
\left(-1\right)^{G}
, which for the NS and R sectors is defined as

\displaystyle\text{NS:}\quad\left(-1\right)^{G_{\textup{NS}}}=\left(-1\right)^{\sum_{r\geq\frac{1}{2}}b^{i}_{-r}b_{r}{}_{i}}\ ,

\displaystyle\text{R:}\quad\left(-1\right)^{G_{\textup{R}}}=\gamma_{9}\left(-1\right)^{\sum_{n\geq 1}d^{i}_{-n}d_{n}{}_{i}}\ ,

(3.2.22)

such that states built out of an even number of oscillators have positive eigenvalue, whereas states built out of an odd number of oscillators have negative eigenvalue. The
\gamma_{9}
matrix appearing in the R-sector operator encodes the chiral nature of its action on the fermionic states built out of the fermionic R-vacuum. Conventionally, one chooses the
s
chirality to be even and the
c
to be odd. According to this definition, the NS tachyonic vacuum is such that

\left(-1\right)^{G_{\textup{NS}}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}=\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\ ,

(3.2.23)

so that it can be removed by projecting the NS spectrum with

\mathcal{P}_{\textup{GSO}}^{\textup{NS}}=\frac{1-\left(-1\right)^{G_{\textup{NS}}}}{2}\ .

(3.2.24)

In the R-sector there is instead no tachyon to remove, and there is additional freedom in choosing the chirality onto which to project:

\mathcal{P}_{\textup{GSO}}^{\textup{R}}=\frac{1\pm\left(-1\right)^{G_{\textup{R}}}}{2}\ .

(3.2.25)

Projecting the quantum excitations of the superstring removes the NS tachyons, as desired, and yields a supersymmetric spectrum. Focusing now on the closed superstring case, the NS-NS sector must be projected as of eq. (
3.2.24
) in both left and right-movers in order to remove the tachyon (
3.2.18
). Instead, there exist two inequivalent projections of the R-R vacuum, corresponding to opposite or identical sign choices in the R-sector projector of eq. (
3.2.25
). The former defines the so-called
type IIA
superstring theory, the latter the
type IIB
. Their massless spectrum is the following:

Common NS-NS sector

as previously mentioned, the two theories share the same NS-NS sector. The massless vacuum is given by the state

b^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{b}^{j}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|0,\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NSNS}}=b^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\otimes\bar{b}^{j}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|\bar{0}\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\ ,

(3.2.26)

of eq. (
3.2.19
), describing the degrees of freedom corresponding to the 10D graviton
G_{\mu\nu}
, Kalb–Ramond field
B_{\mu\nu}
and dilaton
\phi
.

R-R sectors

The vacua of the R-R sectors are conventionally denoted as

\displaystyle\text{type\penalty\ IIA:}\quad\left|+\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}\otimes\left|-\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}\ ,

\displaystyle\text{type\penalty\ IIB:}\quad\left|+\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}\otimes\left|+\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}\ ,

(3.2.27)

referring to the choices of chiral GSO projection (
3.2.25
). The massless spectrum contains the so-called Ramond–Ramond forms. Following eq. (
3.2.20
), type IIA contains a 1-form
C^{(1)}_{\mu}
and a 3-form
C^{(3)}_{\mu\nu\rho}
, sitting in the representation
\bm{8}_{s}\otimes\bm{8}_{c}=\bm{8}_{\textup{v}}\oplus\bm{56}
, whereas type IIB contains a scalar "0-form"
C^{(0)}
, a 2-form
C^{(2)}_{\mu\nu}
and a self-dual 4-form
C^{(4)}_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
, associated with the representation
\bm{8}_{s}\otimes\bm{8}_{s}=\bm{1}\oplus\bm{28}\oplus\bm{35}_{+}
.
2
2

2

Furthermore, the Ramond–Ramond forms can be dualized, adding in this way a
C^{(6)}
and a
C^{(8)}
form to the type IIA spectrum, and a
C^{(5)}
and a
C^{(7)}
form to the type IIB one.

NS-R and R-NS sectors

The NS-R and R-NS sectors contain the fermionic excitations. The massless vacua of the NS-R sector are given by

\displaystyle\text{type\penalty\ IIA:}

\displaystyle b^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\otimes\left|-\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}\in\bm{56}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{s}\ ,

(3.2.28)

\displaystyle\text{type\penalty\ IIB:}

\displaystyle b^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\otimes\left|+\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}\in\bm{56}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{s}\ ,

while those of the R-NS sector are

\displaystyle\text{type\penalty\ IIA:}

\displaystyle\left|+\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}\otimes\bar{b}^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\in\bm{56}_{c}\oplus\bm{8}_{c}\ ,

(3.2.29)

\displaystyle\text{type\penalty\ IIB:}

\displaystyle\left|+\right\rangle_{\textup{R}}\otimes\bar{b}^{i}_{-\frac{1}{2}}\left|0\right\rangle_{\textup{NS}}\in\bm{56}_{s}\oplus\bm{8}_{s}\ .

Thus, both type II theories contain two gravitini,
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
, and two dilatini
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
. In the type IIA case, the two pairs of fermions have opposite chirality, whereas in type IIB they all have the same chirality, which is the left-handed one in these conventions.

The field theory limit of closed type II superstrings are the type IIA and type IIB ten-dimensional supergravity theories, that enjoy respectively
\mathcal{N}=\left(1,1\right)
and
\mathcal{N}=\left(2,0\right)
supersymmetry. Note that, despite being chiral, the type IIB theory is anomaly-free
[
207
,
208
,
209
,
210
]
. The spectra of the two theories are summarized in Table
3.2
.

,

,

,

,

,

,

,
